24.0 Specialized Metabolites in Cereal Crops Under Abiotic Stress: Regulatory Mechanisms, Functional Validation, and Breeding Implications.
Shah MA, Azeem I, Ullah R … Jan MF.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-23
matched: Ca2+, ROS, reactive oxygen species, CPK*, CDPK*, CBL*, CIPK*, MAPK*, signaling, hormone*, defence, salinity
abstract

Cereal crops increasingly encounter drought, salinity and alkalinity, heat, waterlogging, potentially toxic elements, and compound stresses that constrain productivity and grain quality. Secondary metabolites, also referred to as specialized metabolites in the recent literature, represent a dynamic component of cereal stress adaptation rather than a passive catalogue of stress markers. This review aims to synthesize current evidence on how specialized-metabolite pathways contribute to abiotic stress responses in cereals, with particular emphasis on regulatory mechanisms, evidence strength, chemical form, subcellular localization, recovery, and translational relevance. We critically assess evidence from transcriptomics, metabolomics, genetic perturbation, biochemical assays, multi-omics integration, and physiological studies across major cereal crops and evaluate evidence according to causal, associative, and in vitro categories. The evidence reveals that perturbations at the plasma membrane, cell wall, chloroplast, mitochondrion, and endomembrane system levels generate reactive oxygen species, Ca2+ signatures, phospholipid signals, and hormone changes that are decoded by CDPK/CPK, CBL-CIPK, SnRK2, and MAPK cascades. These signaling networks converge on MYB-bHLH-WD40, WRKY, NAC, ERF/AP2, HSF, and bZIP regulators and redirect carbon and reducing power through phenylpropanoid, flavonoid, lignin, carotenoid, terpenoid, benzoxazinoid, cyanogenic, and related pathways. Comparative analysis across rice (Oryza sativa L.), wheat (Triticum aestivum L.), maize (Zea mays L.), barley (Hordeum vulgare L.), oat (Avena sativa L.), sorghum (Sorghum bicolor L.), and millets shows conserved regulatory features but substantial crop-, genotype-, tissue-, developmental-stage-, and dose-dependent variation. Functional evidence further indicates that enzyme activity, chemical modification, transport, and subcellular compartmentation can determine whether metabolites contribute to ROS buffering, photosynthetic and membrane protection, cell-wall reinforcement, osmotic or ionic homeostasis, toxic-ion sequestration, or signaling. The review also identifies important limitations in current research, including overreliance on associative omics evidence, insufficient consideration of combined stresses and rehydration, growth-defence trade-offs, and limited field and reproductive-stage validation. To distinguish growth dilution from genuine biosynthetic increases, absolute metabolite content per grain or organ should be measured alongside concentration per unit dry weight. We conclude that improving cereal resilience requires context-dependent and experimentally validated coordination of regulators, biosynthetic enzymes, chemical modification, transport, and compartmentation rather than indiscriminate elevation of total specialized-metabolite concentration.

19.5 Calcium-dependent protein kinase signalling in plant immune responses: current status and future prospects.
Amrutha K, Resmi MS, Soniya EV, Vivek PJ.
reviewKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPlanta · 2026-10-16
matched: calcium, ROS, reactive oxygen species, CDPK*, kinase*, MAPK*, signal transduction, signalling, immun*, pathogen*, defence, development
abstract

Main conclusionThis review focuses on the critical role of CDPKs in advancing plant immunity research, highlighting how their functions can uncover key defence mechanisms and bridge critical knowledge gaps in the field. Calcium serves as the cornerstone of immune signalling, propagating pathogen-induced signals through specific sensors and decoders. Among these, calcium-dependent protein kinases (CDPKs) stand out as plant-specific signal transducers, directly converting calcium fluxes into defence activation. By modulating key immune components, CDPKs act as key hubs in plant immunity. Deciphering their role and crosstalks with other signalling networks is crucial for understanding the complexities of plant defence mechanisms. Although functional redundancy and extensive crosstalk among CDPK family members have historically hindered precise functional characterization, these features also highlight the robustness and adaptability of plant immune systems. Recent advances including high-throughput transcriptomics, comprehensive CDPK family-wide analyses, and multiplex genome-editing approaches such as CRISPR-Cas-based multi-gene targeting are now enabling finer functional resolution of CDPK members within complex multigene families. This review synthesizes current insights into CDPK-mediated signal transduction, with particular emphasis on their roles in coordinating defence responses and integrating with major signalling pathways, including mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) cascades, reactive oxygen species (ROS) signalling, and WRKY transcription factor-mediated gene regulation. Importantly, the continued development of integrative and high-resolution approaches is poised to overcome longstanding limitations in the field, facilitating deeper dissection of CDPK-specific functions. Collectively, these advances position CDPK research at the forefront of plant immunity studies, with strong potential to reveal novel regulatory mechanisms.

18.5 A Review of Nuclear-Organellar Communication in Response to Heat Stress.
Zhang L, Li X, Pan A … Ni S.
reviewKinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-30
matched: calcium, Ca2+, ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, second messenger*, heat stress, plasma membrane, endoplasmic reticulum, ER, nucleus, nuclear
abstract

Plants coordinate their growth and developmental programs through dynamic communication in the core of the nucleus, enabling them to adapt to persistently high-temperature environments. Upon heat stress, plants primarily perceive and transduce thermal signals between the nucleus and other organelles, a process defined as nuclear-organellar communication. The published research on plants in response to heat environments mainly focuses on sensing heat physical signals from the plasma membrane (PM)/cytosol to the nucleus, eliciting nuclear transcriptional reprogramming and decoding organelle-derived retrograde signals to the nucleus. Consequently, plants have evolved sophisticated systems to decode adverse temperature signals relying on efficient nuclear-organellar coordination. This review summarizes how plants maintain cellular homeostasis via nuclear crosstalk. Heat signals are initially perceived, which triggers the production of secondary messengers including phosphatidic acid (PA), calcium ions (Ca2+) and reactive oxygen species (ROS). In addition, proteins also deliver thermal signals to the nucleus. As the central signaling hub, the nucleus orchestrates global transcriptional reprogramming predominantly through the Heat Shock Transcriptional Factors (HSFs)-heat shock proteins (HSPs) regulatory module, and sends instructions to the endoplasmic reticulum (ER), chloroplasts and mitochondria. Conversely, organelle-derived retrograde signals coordinate stress adaptation: the unfolded protein response relieves ER stress, and heat stress activates ROS-mediated chloroplast-to-nucleus and mitochondria-to-nucleus feedback pathways. We further outline candidate thermosensing proteins residing in different organelles, and highlight major unsolved issues. Critical knowledge gaps include the intrinsic thermosensing capacity of chloroplasts and mitochondria, inter-organellar coordination of second-messenger dynamics, specific organelle functions, and the biological roles of membrane contact sites. Further mechanistic insights will facilitate the application of genome editing, synthetic biology and artificial-intelligence-aided design to breed heat-tolerant crops without yield penalties.

18.5 The potassium homeostasis-stress adaptation continuum: An integrative signaling platform for plant survival.
Hu B, Ma S, Chen N … Li T.
reviewKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-13
matched: Ca2+, ROS, reactive oxygen species, membrane potential, CBL*, CIPK*, kinase*, signaling, receptor*, immun*, pathogen*, salinity
abstract

Potassium (K+) is the predominant cation in plants and has traditionally been viewed as essential for nutrition and osmotic regulation. Studies over the past decade have demonstrated that it is also key to plant response coordination under conditions such as drought, salinity, temperature extremes, and pathogen attack. This review discusses the literature on K+ homeostasis-stress adaptation that highlights K+ transmembrane flux as an integrative signaling platform connecting Ca2+ signaling, reactive oxygen species (ROS) production, hormonal regulation, and immune responses. The K+ pathway from rhizosphere perception to shoot function is assessed, and the CBL (calcineurin B-like protein)-CIPK (CBL-interacting protein kinase) signaling module is highlighted as a context-dependent regulator of K+ transport. We identify the absence of a dedicated K+ sensor as an evolutionary puzzle and propose that plants may rely on distributed sensing-membrane potential, Ca2+ signatures, and transporter-kinase complexes-rather than a single receptor. We define the K+ homeostasis-stress adaptation continuum as the temporal, spatial, and functional integration of K+ perception, transport and signaling during stress adaptation. The review suggests future opportunities for crop improvement, including the use of natural allelic variation in Clade IV high-affinity K+ transporters to engineer the CBL-CIPK-TORC (target of rapamycin complex) regulatory network that can improve potassium use efficiency and tolerance to multiple stresses. By presenting K+ homeostasis as an integral part of plant stress adaptation, this review provides a scientific basis for future breeding and biotechnological approaches.

18.0 Plant Stress Signaling in a Cross-Kingdom Context: Metabolic Control of Redox and ROS-Ca2+-Hormone Interactomes.
Warda M, Bolat M, Tekin S … Abd El-Aty AM.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-29
matched: calcium, Ca2+, calcium signal*, ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, hormone*, stress respon*, development
abstract

Biological systems maintain functional integrity under environmental stress through tightly coordinated signaling networks, yet the mechanisms that preserve signal fidelity in the presence of stochastic oxidative and ionic fluctuations across taxa remain incompletely understood. Although redox regulation, coupling between reactive oxygen species (ROS) and Ca2+, and hormone-mediated feedback loops have been extensively characterized within individual kingdoms, a cross-kingdom framework linking these pathways through metabolite-mediated modulation is lacking. Here, a cross-kingdom compilation of evidence from plant, animal, and microbial systems is presented to examine how specific endogenous and microbe-derived metabolites and plant fructans modulate the fidelity of stress signaling networks. We propose a metabolite-guided signal fidelity hypothesis in which certain specialized metabolites act as molecular stabilizers in the response of organisms to stress. They buffer transient ROS bursts, limit variability in calcium signaling, and strengthen hormone-regulated feedback loops. Together, these functions preserve the signal-to-noise ratio of stress responses, independent of overt toxicity. These insights lay the groundwork for testable predictions and the development of translational strategies in agriculture, biotechnology, and medicine aimed at improving stress tolerance by stabilizing the flow of biological information.

16.0 Transcription Factor TabZIP12 Orchestrates the ABA Signaling Pathway to Regulate Drought Tolerance in Wheat (Triticum aestivum L.).
Hou X, Wang Z, Zhang J … Xiao K.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPlant physiology · 2026-10-19
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, kinase*, phosphatase*, signaling, receptor*, PIN\d+, abscisic acid, ABA, osmotic stress, drought, stress respon*
abstract

Drought-induced osmotic stress severely compromises crop yield productivity and threatens global food security. This study reports TabZIP12, a wheat bZIP transcription factor, in regulating osmotic stress tolerance. Under drought and abscisic acid (ABA) treatment, TabZIP12 expression was significantly upregulated with a dose- and time-dependent manner. Y-2H, BiFC, Co-IP, and In vitro pull-down assays confirmed a physical interaction between TabZIP12 and the kinase TaSnRK2.6. This kinase further interacted with the phosphatase TaPP2C50, which in turn interacted with the ABA receptor TaPYL1. These interactions suggest the TabZIP12-involved constitution of a synergistic signaling module (TaPYL1-TaPP2C50-TaSnRK2.6-TabZIP12). Transgene analyses revealed the essential functions of the signaling module members. Of which, TaPYL1, TaSnRK2.6, and TabZIP12 positively while TaPP2C50 negatively regulated drought tolerance, due to their roles in modulating key physiological processes associated with stomatal movement, osmolyte biosynthesis, root morphology, ABA biosynthesis, and reactive oxygen species (ROS) scavenging. TabZIP12 possessed an ability in binding with the promoters of critical stress-responsive genes, namely SLAC1 gene TaSLAC1-4, P5CS gene TaP5CS1, PIN-FORMed gene TaPIN3, NCED gene TaNCED3, and APX gene TaAPX6 and activated their activation. Transgenic validation confirmed these genes in positively modulating drought adaptation. Notably, in a wheat variety panel sharing contrasting drought response, the transcripts of TabZIP12 and its target genes correlated significantly with plant dry matter and yield under drought conditions. Haplotype analysis identified TabZIP12-Hap1 as a favorable allele associated with enhanced drought tolerance. Our findings elucidate novel mechanisms underlying the bZIP-mediated drought response and provide valuable genetic resources for breeding drought-resistant wheat varieties.

15.0 Heavy metal stress mitigation in plants by metal-resistant fungi: molecular and biotechnological insights.
Choudhary S, Singh K, Sharma A … Juneja G.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlant cell reports · 2026-10-24
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, MAPK*, signaling, auxin*, ABA, ethylene, JA, mycorrhiz*, nutrient*, heavy metal*, vacuol*
abstract

Heavy Metals (HMs) are persistent, non-biodegradable metallic elements that accumulate into soils due to weathering processes and anthropogenic activities, such as mining, smelting and industrial discharges. They can be classified as essential (Cu, Zn, Ni, Fe, Mn), which are toxic in excessive amounts, and non-essential (Cd, Pb, As, Hg), which are dangerous even in small amounts. HMs affect plant metabolism causing production of Reactive Oxygen Species (ROS), growth inhibition and nutrient deficiency, Arabidopsis thaliana seedlings germination was lowered to 55% and 21% at concentrations of 50 and 100 mg L⁻1 CdCl₂. Phytochelatins, metallothioneins and vacuole sequestration protect plants, but become ineffective at high concentrations of HMs. Metal-resistant fungi help to cope with stress conditions using mechanisms of biosorption, bioaccumulation, biomineralization, bioleaching and biotransformation, adapted Aspergillus niger managed to leach 78.5% Ni and 82.3% Mo during 30 days, while Penicillium chrysogenum eliminated 63% Cd in soil. Partners of fungi, such as Trichoderma and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi, regulate production of ROS, activation of MAPKs and ABA/JA/SA/ethylene/auxin signaling pathway and expression of MT, PCS, GST, ABC, NRAMP and HMA genes under control of WRKY, MYB, NAC and bZIP transcription factors.

14.0 A Mechanochemical Model of Plant Cell Expansion Regulation: Beyond the Acid Growth Hypothesis
Khablak SH, Bondareva LM, Kolomiiets YV … Abdullaieva YA.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesPreprints.org · 2026-10-20
matched: Ca²⁺, reactive oxygen species, apoplast*, mechanosens*, pH, signalling, auxin*, cell wall*, plasma membrane, cell expansion, growth
abstract

The acid growth hypothesis explains the role of apoplastic acidification in auxin-induced plant cell expansion; however, it does not, on its own, explain the conditions under which this process develops into sustained, irreversible growth. Apoplastic acidification, cell-wall loosening, and the presence of turgor pressure do not invariably result in the same growth response. This indicates that cell expansion should be understood as an outcome of interactions among hormonal regulation, cell-wall mechanical properties, water availability, and metabolic status. Here, we propose an integrative mechanistic model of plant cell expansion regulation in which the transition to a growth-permissive functional state is a central component. Within this framework, apoplastic pH is not considered a sufficient determinant of growth in itself, but rather a regulatory factor that influences the capacity of the cell wall to undergo mechanical deformation. The realization of growth depends on the coordination of this process with turgor pressure, water transport, metabolic capacity, and mechanosensory feedback mechanisms. This approach integrates the regulation of plasma membrane H⁺-ATPases, cell-wall remodelling, the TOR–SnRK1 signalling axis, and Ca²⁺- and reactive oxygen species-mediated pathways into a unified conceptual framework. The model describes cell expansion as a sequence of transitions among restricted, growth-permissive, actively expanding, and reinforcing states. Rather than rejecting the acid growth hypothesis, it extends its explanatory scope by linking local changes in apoplastic pH and cell-wall mechanics to the regulation of water relations, metabolism, and sustained growth. The proposed framework provides a basis for experimental investigation of the mechanisms through which plant cells convert regulatory signals into irreversible expansion.

14.0 Interaction between FmRAV4 (a RAV transcription factor) and FmMYB1 (a MYB transcription factor) confers drought tolerance via ROS scavenging in Fraxinus mandshurica.
Nie X, Nie X, Xu X … Ji X.
Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesGrowthInternational journal of biological macromolecules · 2026-10-17
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, drought, stress respon*, nucleus, leaves, growth, development, woody
abstract

Drought stress severely restricts plant growth and development. Although AP2/ERF family transcription factors (TFs) participate extensively in abiotic stress responses, they remain largely unexplored in Fraxinus mandshurica. In this study, we identified and functionally characterized FmRAV4, a TF from the RAV subfamily. FmRAV4 is localized in the nucleus, lacks transcriptional activation activity, and is significantly upregulated in leaves under drought stress. Functional analyses using transient overexpression, gene silencing in F. mandshurica, and stable transformation in Arabidopsis thaliana demonstrated that FmRAV4 enhances drought tolerance by increasing SOD and POD activities, scavenging excessive reactive oxygen species (ROS), and alleviating membrane lipid peroxidation. Mechanistically, yeast one-hybrid, EMSA, and ChIP-qPCR assays revealed that FmRAV4 directly binds to conserved CAACA motifs in the promoters of FmSOD2, FmPOD1, FmPOD2, and FmPOD4 to activate their transcription. Importantly, yeast two-hybrid, luciferase complementation imaging, and pull-down assays confirmed that FmRAV4 physically interacts with FmMYB1 both in vivo and in vitro, and that FmMYB1 upregulation improves drought tolerance in F. mandshurica. Co-overexpression of these two genes synergistically activates antioxidant genes in a concentration-dependent manner, thereby enhancing ROS scavenging and conferring stronger drought tolerance than single-gene overexpression. Collectively, these findings reveal that the FmRAV4-FmMYB1 interaction forms a synergistic regulatory module mediating drought tolerance, providing valuable genetic resources for the molecular breeding of drought-tolerant woody plants.

14.0 On her cue: Papilla-derived jasmonic acid triggers rapid calcium signaling for pollen germination.
Hafidh S, Tichy J.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthMolecular plant · 2026-10-21

13.0 The ROS wave as a central catalyst of systemic acclimation and stress tolerance in plants.
Mittler R, Mohanty D, Fichman Y … Peláez-Vico MÁ.
reviewKinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesTrees and woody plantsCurrent opinion in plant biology · 2026-10-16
matched: calcium, ROS, reactive oxygen species, systemic signal*, signaling, plasma membrane, hydraulic*
abstract

Plants survive in environments where stress conditions fluctuate rapidly and unpredictably and their survival depends on their ability to transform local stress perception into coordinated whole-plant acclimation. Among the systemic signaling mechanisms studied to date, the 'reactive oxygen species (ROS) wave' has appeared as a fundamental process linking local stress perception to whole-plant acclimation. Initially defined as a self-propagating burst of ROS mediated by the plasma membrane enzyme RESPIRATORY BURST OXIDASE HOMOLOG D, the ROS wave is now recognized as one component of a highly integrated signaling network that dynamically interacts with calcium, electrical, hydraulic, redox, hormonal and other signaling molecules (i.e., gasotransmitters). Recent work has reshaped this view by revealing how different systemic signaling components interact with the ROS wave to mount stress-specific systemic responses and coordinate systemic acquired acclimation. Furthermore, ROS-dependent signals were found to propagate between neighboring plants, contributing to stress resilience at the community level. Here, we discuss recent advances that transformed our understanding of the ROS wave from an isolated signaling mechanism to a central regulator within an interconnected systemic signaling network.

12.5 MdMPK6a/b-mediated phosphorylation of MdANN2 restricts cytosolic Ca2+ influx and attenuates cold tolerance in apple.
Wang J, Yang M, Li Y … Mao K.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesThe Plant journal : for cell and molecular biology · 2026-10-01 · also: The Plant Journal
matched: Ca2+, annexin*, kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, cold stress, stress respon*, plasma membrane, nucleus
abstract

Annexins (ANNs) are Ca2+-dependent phospholipid-binding proteins that play important roles in plant responses to environmental stress. However, the apple ANN gene family and the regulatory mechanisms by which ANNs participate in cold-stress responses remain poorly understood. In this study, 15 MdANN family members were identified in the apple genome and divided into three subfamilies. Among them, MdANN2 expression was strongly induced by low temperature. Functional analyses showed that MdANN2 overexpression markedly enhanced cold tolerance in apple, whereas virus-induced gene silencing (VIGS) of MdANN2 compromised cold tolerance. Subcellular localization assays revealed that MdANN2 localized to the plasma membrane, cytoplasm, and nucleus. Non-invasive micro-test technology (NMT) further showed that MdANN2 is associated with Ca2+-permeable channel activity: cold stimulation significantly enhanced cytosolic Ca2+ influx in MdANN2-overexpressing plants, whereas exogenous application of the Ca2+ channel blocker LaCl3 or the Ca2+ chelator EGTA substantially inhibited this influx. The kinases MdMPK6a and MdMPK6b physically interacted with and phosphorylated MdANN2 both in vitro and in vivo. Silencing MdMPK6a/b significantly increased apple cold tolerance, whereas overexpression of MdMPK6a/b reduced apple cold tolerance. Moreover, transient overexpression of MdMPK6a/b in the MdANN2-overexpression background attenuated MdANN2-mediated cold tolerance and Ca2+ influx. In addition, co-localization assays showed that MdMPK6a/b decreased the accumulation of MdANN2 at the plasma membrane. Together, these findings demonstrate that MdANN2 positively regulates apple cold tolerance by promoting cold-induced Ca2+ influx, a process inhibited by MdMPK6a/b-mediated phosphorylation. This study identifies a novel MdMPK6a/b-MdANN2 module that governs Ca2+ signaling in apple under cold stress, thereby providing promising molecular targets for improving cold tolerance in apple and other crop species.

12.5 Stomatal Dynamics Multilevel Regulation and Physiological Response of Prunus mira Koehne under Extreme CO2 Toxicity Stress.
Li J, Li M, Qi X … Meng F.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthTrees and woody plantsPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-22
matched: Ca2+, ROS, reactive oxygen species, H2O2, signaling, stoma*, guard cell*, woody
abstract

Rising atmospheric CO2 levels not only drive global warming but also exert toxic effects on plant photosynthesis and physiology under extreme exposure, however the adaptive responses of stress tolerant species such as Prunus mira Koehne remain poorly understood. In this study, we investigated the physiological and stomatal response mechanisms of P. mira under 5% CO2 conditions. The results of our physiological and biochemical analyses revealed that 5% CO2 concentrations reduced chlorophyll content and impaired photosynthetic efficiency. Concurrently, high CO2 stimulated alternative respiration, disrupted membrane integrity, elevated ROS (reactive oxygen species) levels, and increased antioxidant enzyme activities. In guard cells, high CO2 triggered accumulations of ROS and NO (nitric oxide), leading to stomatal closure, reduced stomatal conductance, while transpiration rates fluctuated during the treatment period. Moreover, pharmacological analyses suggested the involvement of Ca2+ in regulating ROS and NO accumulation, while ASA (ascorbic acid) promoted scavenging of excess H2O2. Our results suggest that ROS accumulation and Ca2⁺ signaling are involved in stomatal regulation under extreme CO2 toxicity stress. In summary, these findings provide mechanistic insights into plant responses under acute extreme CO2 toxicity and lay a foundation for understanding stress signaling robustness in woody species.

12.0 Integrated physiological, biochemical, and transcriptomic analyses reveal the mechanisms by which light intensity affects flowering traits and secondary metabolism in Dendrobium denneanum.
Wang H, He S, Fan Y … Luo A.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthTrees and woody plantsPhysiology and molecular biology of plants : an international journal of functional plant biology · 2026-09-06
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, kinase*, MAPK*, signaling, stoma*, flowering, growth, development, dendro*
abstract

Dendrobium denneanum, valued for its ornamental and medicinal properties, exhibits significant regulation of flowering and secondary metabolism by light intensity. This study employed physiological, biochemical, and transcriptomic analyses to compare the regulatory effects of five light intensity gradients (50, 100, 200, 400, and 600 µmol m⁻² s⁻¹) on flowering traits and secondary metabolism in D. denneanum at different developmental stages. Results demonstrated that 400 µmol m⁻² s⁻¹ markedly promoted flower organ growth, extending the flowering period, increasing the flowering rate, and enhancing flower color saturation, while simultaneously improving photosynthetic capacity and secondary metabolite accumulation. Notably, the regulatory effects of light intensity on floral morphological traits were most pronounced during the full blooming stage (S2), when flowers exhibited the greatest improvements in organ expansion, pigmentation, and bioactive compound accumulation. Transcriptomic analysis revealed that this light intensity upregulated photosynthetic light-harvesting processes and regulated carbon metabolism, optimizing light energy utilization and carbon flux to support flower development and flavonoid synthesis. Genes in the phenylpropanoid/flavonoid pathway were significantly upregulated, likely serving as key genes of flower coloration and medicinal component accumulation. Additionally, the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) signaling pathway suppressed stress-related genes and upregulated growth-related genes, promoting stomatal development and maintaining reactive oxygen species (ROS) balance to enhance stress tolerance.The transcriptomic results were further validated by quantitative real-time PCR (qRT-PCR). These findings highlight how 400 µmol m⁻² s⁻¹ optimizes photosynthetic efficiency, metabolic redirection, and growth-stress equilibrium, offering novel insights into light-mediated regulatory mechanisms in Orchidaceae plants and a foundation for precision cultivation strategies.Supplementary informationThe online version contains supplementary material available at https://doi.org/10.1007/s12298-026-01815-2.

12.0 The redox-phytohormone nexus, illustrated by the jasmonate paradigm.
Song RF, Foyer CH, Liu WC.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesTrends in plant science · 2026-10-19
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, H2O2, signaling, receptor*, jasmon*, JA, phytohormone*, hormone*
abstract

Redox-sensitive protein switches and transcriptional networks are integral to phytohormone signaling networks. The jasmonate (JA) signaling pathway, which incorporates the coronatine insensitive 1 (COI1)-Jasmonate ZIM-domain (JAZ) co-receptor and the MYC2 transcription factor, is a relatively recent evolutionary innovation that has emerged only in land plants. Earlier JA signaling systems relied on electrophilic oxylipins, such as 12-oxo-phytodienoic acid (OPDA) and dinor-12-oxo-10,15(Z)-phytodienoic acid (dn-OPDA), and reactive oxygen species (ROS), particularly H2O2. All plant 14-3-3 proteins, such as General regulatory factor 8 (GRF8), contain a conserved redox-sensitive cysteine. Sulfenylation of GRF8 relieves inhibition of MYC2, thereby facilitating JA signaling in arabidopsis (Arabidopsis thaliana). This mechanism may represent a primordial oxylipin signaling system, overlain by the more recent COI1-JAZ system, revealing hormone signaling plasticity that can be targeted for engineering stress-resilient crops.

11.5 CaZAT10-CaWRKY11 module positively regulates pepper (Capsicum annuum L.) salt stress response.
Wang Y, Yu Y, Qiu L … Liu J.
Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesGrowthJournal of plant physiology · 2026-10-13
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, salt sens*, salt stress, salinity, stress respon*, nucleus, growth
abstract

Salinity stress markedly impairs plant growth and yield. Pepper (Capsicum annuum L.), a prominent economic horticultural species, demonstrates a pronounced sensitivity to saline conditions. However, the adaptive mechanism of pepper plants under salt stress remains largely unclear. This study explores the physiological function of the transcription factor CaZAT10 in modulating the responses of pepper to salt stress. Our results reveal that CaZAT10 is primarily localized in the nucleus, and its expression is significantly upregulated following salt exposure. The silenced CaZAT10 pepper plants exhibit increased sensitivity to salt stress. Furthermore, the CaZAT10-silenced pepper plants also present heightened reactive oxygen species (ROS) concentrations. We also show that CaWRKY11 acts as an interacting partner of CaZAT10. Silencing of CaWRKY11 also results in a comparable salt-sensitive phenotype and increased ROS levels, mirroring the observations in CaZAT10-silenced pepper plants. Collectively, our findings suggest that the CaZAT10-CaWRKY11 regulatory module plays a crucial role in regulating pepper's resistance to salt stress by maintaining ROS homeostasis.

11.5 Melatonin and auxin act synergistically to restore photosynthetic efficiency and redox homeostasis in copper-stressed Brassica juncea.
Mir AR, Arif Y, Bi MH … Fang XW.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-12
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, H2O2, hydrogen peroxide, auxin*, IAA, defence, nutrient*, heavy metal*, chloroplast*, mitochondri*, growth
abstract

Copper (Cu) is an essential micronutrient involved in mitochondrial respiration, photosynthesis, and oxidative stress regulation, however, at supra-optimal concentrations it displays toxicity and inhibit plant growth and productivity. Melatonin (MT), an indoleamine with potent antioxidant activity, while as, indole-3-acetic acid (IAA), a key growth and photosynthesis promoter, both hold strong potential to mitigate abiotic stress. Considering their potential, we hypothesized that whether the antioxidant action of MT and the growth-promoting action of IAA would act synergistically or antagonistically. Results demonstrated that Cu stress enhanced the accumulation of reactive oxygen species (ROS), hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), superoxide (O2-) malondialdehyde (MDA), and electrolyte leakage (EL) levels, thereby damaging chloroplast organization and structure, decreasing photosynthetic efficiency, CO2 assimilation, and ultimately hinder plant growth. However, the follow-up application of IAA and/or MT proved effective in alleviating Cu-stress in plants by improving all the physio-biochemical traits via maintaining the redox homeostasis and reducing oxidative stress biomarkers. Moreover, both these indolamines proved effective to repairs the anatomical damage induced by Cu. The prominent results were obtained by combined application of both IAA and MT. The findings highlight the interplay between PGR's, antioxidant defence mechanisms and nutrient homeostasis, providing insights to develop new strategies for improving crop resilience and addressing environmental concerns associated with heavy metal accumulation in agricultural ecosystems.

11.5 Molecular Basis of Silicon-Mediated Plant Defense: Transporters, Cell Wall Integrity, Redox-Hormone Crosstalk, and Epigenetic Priming.
Li JJ, Shabbir R, Huang JH … Gao SJ.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlant, cell & environment · 2026-10-22
matched: Ca2+, ROS, signaling, receptor*, FERONIA, JA, hormone*, defense, cell wall*, vesicle*
abstract

Silicon (Si) is emerging as a key modulator, and its molecular mechanism of defense regulation keeps emerging. This review focus on Si-uptake by Lsi-type transporters, deposition in cuticles and cell wall, redox buffering, modify cell wall integrity (CWI) and hormonal signaling to increase resistance against adverse conditions and to reshape plant-microbe-soil interaction in rhizosphere. By highlighting the emerging research on Si-mediated regulations at molecular scale, we proposed two working hypotheses which are 'mechanical buffer model' for soluble-Si and 'dynamic signaling model' for Si-nanomaterials (Si-NPs). Mechanical buffer model states that Si-fortified walls could stabilize the ROS profile and wall mechanics to maintain and primed state for cell while dynamic signaling model states that Si-NPs follow an additional vesicle-mediated uptake forming a corona protein, elicit ROS-bursts, robust activation of FERONIA-like receptors, Ca2+ channels, and JA/SA signaling. Furthermore, association of Si-associated chromatin and siRNAs were summarized and how these Si-mediated alterations in DNA methylation under stress conditions could support the stress memory and defense priming. Overall, this framework could provide a molecular basis for Si-mediated inputs and breeding approaches with the aim of climate-resilient crops.

11.0 Genome-wide identification and expression profiling of IQM family genes in Chrysanthemum nankingense in response to abiotic stresses.
Wang T, Xie Z, Li S … Pu S.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressGenetica · 2026-10-25
matched: calcium, calcium signal*, calmodulin*, signaling, phytohormone*, salinity, drought, nucleus, growth, development
abstract

IQM proteins are plant-specific central modulators of calcium signaling, coordinating plant growth, development, and stress adaptation via calmodulin binding, yet no systematic characterization of the IQM family has been reported in Chrysanthemum nankingense. Here, we performed a genome-wide identification of the CnIQM gene family and conducted systematic bioinformatic analyses of their physicochemical traits, chromosomal distribution and phylogenetic relationships. RT-qPCR was used to detect transcriptional responses of CnIQM genes to exogenous phytohormones and major abiotic stresses, and yeast heterologous expression assays validated the stress tolerance of key CnIQM candidates. A total of nine CnIQM genes were identified. Most encode alkaline proteins predominantly localized to the nucleus or cytoplasm. Tandem duplication appears to be a major contributor to CnIQM family expansion, and all duplicated gene pairs are under strong purifying selection; high syntenic conservation of IQM loci is observed between C. nankingense and its congener Chrysanthemum lavandulifolium. All CnIQM proteins contain the canonical conserved IQ motif, whereas several paralogs exhibit structural variation with truncated UTR regions. Transcriptional profiling revealed differential induction under phytohormone, salt, drought, and cold treatments, with most genes rapidly upregulated by salinity and drought stress. Yeast heterologous expression assays showed that CnIQM5, CnIQM6, and CnIQM9 significantly improve salt and drought tolerance in yeast, while none enhances cold resistance in this heterologous system. This study clarifies the evolutionary and functional features of the CnIQM family and provides theoretical references and promising candidate genes for future functional research and stress-resistant molecular breeding of chrysanthemum.

10.5 CPK10 phosphorylates zinc-sensing transcription factors bZIP19/23 to establish time-delayed negative feedback in plant zinc homeostasis.
Deng Y, Yang C, Wu Y … Ju C.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressGrowthJournal of integrative plant biology · 2026-10-19
matched: calcium, Ca2+, CPK*, kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, nutrient*, growth
abstract

Zinc (Zn) homeostasis is essential for plant growth; yet, how zinc-deficiency-triggered calcium (Ca) signals are decoded into homeostatic adjustments remains elusive. Here, we identify the calcium-dependent protein kinase 10 (CPK10) as a key decoder of zinc-deficiency-triggered Ca2+ signals. Under zinc-deficient conditions, both the protein level and the kinase activity of CPK10 increase. Subsequently, CPK10 interacts with and phosphorylates the zinc-sensing transcription factors bZIP19 and bZIP23, thereby repressing their DNA-binding and transcriptional activities. Zrt/Irt-like protein (ZIP) family members are the primary transporters responsible for zinc uptake from the soil, and CPK10-mediated phosphorylation of bZIP19 and bZIP23 reduces the expression of ZIP1, ZIP4, and defensin-like (DEFL) genes such as DEFL208. CPK10 activation is time-delayed, peaking at 72 h after zinc-deficiency treatment, providing a braking mechanism that prevents prolonged high-rate ion flux driven by sustained high-level expression of zinc transporters. The CPK10-targeted phosphorylation site Ser241 of bZIP19 is conserved in dicots but absent in monocots, suggesting evolutionary divergence in this zinc homeostasis-regulatory module. Our work reveals a Ca2+-CPK10-bZIP19/23 module that mediates spatiotemporal control of zinc homeostasis, highlighting a key regulatory layer in plant nutrient signaling.

10.5 Effects of short-term salt stress on photosynthetic characteristics, physiological metabolism, and ion homeostasis in soybean seedlings.
Zhou X, Zhen J, Wang X … Li X.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesBMC plant biology · 2026-07-25
matched: Ca²⁺, salt sens*, defense, salt stress, NaCl, mitochondri*, leaf, stems, seed*, growth
abstract

BackgroundSalt stress is a major abiotic stress limiting soybean production, with the seedling stage being the most salt-sensitive period. Elucidating the growth, photosynthetic, and physiological response mechanisms of soybean seedlings to salt stress holds significant theoretical value for breeding salt-tolerant cultivars.MethodsIn this study, we systematically investigated the effects of varying NaCl stress intensities (0-160 mmol⋅kg⁻¹) on plant growth, photosynthetic gas exchange, chlorophyll fluorescence, antioxidant physiology, and ion homeostasis using a salt-tolerant cultivar (Zhonglongdou 136, ZLD136) and a salt-sensitive cultivar (Henong 85, HN85).ResultsThe results demonstrated that increasing salt concentrations significantly inhibited plant growth and photosynthetic carbon assimilation. However, ZLD136 exhibited superior phenotypic stability and photoprotective mechanisms, evidenced by a significant increase in non-photochemical quenching (NPQt). Salt stress triggered a substantial rise in malondialdehyde (MDA) content and relative electrical conductivity (REC); notably, ZLD136 effectively mitigated membrane lipid peroxidation by maintaining higher antioxidant enzyme (superoxide dismutase SOD, peroxidase POD, and catalase CAT) activities and accumulating more osmolytes, including proline (Pro) and soluble sugars (SS). Furthermore, the salt-tolerant cultivar more efficiently coordinated the mitochondrial respiratory electron transport chain (cytochrome c oxidase COX and alternative oxidase AOX pathways) to dissipate excess reducing power and maintain cellular energy homeostasis. ZLD136 also possessed a stronger capacity for ion regulation, significantly outperforming HN85 in maintaining higher leaf K⁺/Na⁺ and Ca²⁺/Na⁺ ratios.ConclusionsSalt tolerance in soybean seedlings involves a comprehensive synergistic network, and the salt tolerance advantage of ZLD136 primarily stems from its superior ability to maintain ion balance, highly efficient photoprotective mechanisms, and a robust antioxidant defense system. Comprehensive analysis revealed that with intensifying salt stress, the physiological responses of soybean gradually transitioned from relying on the antioxidant enzyme system to the synthesis of osmolytes. Moreover, maintaining ion homeostasis (particularly a high leaf K⁺/Na⁺ ratio) is the core prerequisite for alleviating physiological and metabolic disorders, safeguarding photosynthetic functions, and ensuring short-term plant growth.

10.5 The MdVQ22-MdWRKY33d complex regulates MdPER55 and MdDREB1D expression to modulate apple salt tolerance by reactive oxygen species scavenging.
Hou H, Li S, Zhang Q … Zhang Y.
Abiotic stressTrees and woody plantsThe Plant journal : for cell and molecular biology · 2026-10-01 · also: The Plant Journal
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, salt sens*, salt stress, poplar*, tree, trees
abstract

Apple is a globally significant fruit tree with considerable economic value. However, it is severely affected by soil salinization, which leads to a reduction in both yield and quality. Since salt tolerance in grafted apple trees largely depends on the rootstock, this study compared transcriptomic responses between salt-tolerant (R5-5) and salt-sensitive (M9T337) rootstocks under salt stress. A key salt-responsive gene, MdWRKY33d, was identified. Overexpressing MdWRKY33d in apple improved salt tolerance, while silencing it had the opposite effect. Additionally, under salt stress, compared with the control, chlorophyll fluorescence values, superoxide dismutase (SOD), peroxidase (POD), and catalase (CAT) levels were elevated in MdWRKY33d transgenic apple and poplar materials. Functional analyses demonstrated that MdWRKY33d directly activates the expression of Peroxidase55 (MdPER55) and Dehydration-Responsive Element-Binding protein1D (MdDREB1D), thereby enhancing reactive oxygen species (ROS) scavenging and salt tolerance in transgenic plants. 10.5 The impact of leachate from waste rubber particles on duckweed: growth, oxidative stress, and cell structure.
Zhao S, Gao Y, Wang Z … Duo L.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-05
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, hydrogen peroxide, cell wall*, mitochondri*, root, root tip*, growth
abstract

Waste rubber particles, especially tire wear particles (TWPs), have become major contributors to microplastic pollution. Through rainwater and road runoff, TWPs can enter the water environment, posing potential threats to aquatic organisms. However, systematic research on the effects of waste rubber particles on duckweed remains limited. Thus, Spirodela polyrhiza was exposed to rubber leachate at 10%, 15%, 20%, and 25% levels, aiming to evaluate the effects of rubber leachates on duckweed at the individual, physiological, and cellular levels. The results showed that rubber leachates significantly inhibited duckweed growth and reproduction. Exposure to the 25% leachate significantly reduced root length, root volume, and root activity of duckweed plants by 74.6%, 63.9%, and 29.7% (p p p < 0.05) under the 25% treatment. This was accompanied by reactive oxygen species (ROS) overproduction, reflected in significantly elevated superoxide anion and hydrogen peroxide contents in root tips. Furthermore, the leachates induced oxidative stress in duckweed, as evidenced by increased antioxidant enzyme activities and antioxidant contents. In addition, the leachates compromised the integrity of both cell and mitochondrial membrane, reduced cell wall component contents, and induced apoptosis. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that the toxicity of rubber leachates is mediated through oxidative stress and cellular structural damage.

10.0 Calmodulin: The Essential Biological Regulator Self-Regulates Its Activity, Structure and Function.
O'Day DH.
reviewKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityBiology · 2026-10-02
matched: calcium, calcium signal*, calmodulin*, phosphoryl*, effector*
abstract

Calmodulin is an evolutionarily conserved critical controller protein found in animals, plants, fungi, and microbes. As the primary calcium sensor and effector in eukaryotes, calmodulin translates calcium signals into vital biological responses by binding to and regulating calmodulin-binding proteins. While it was previously shown to control several hundred proteins, evidence now indicates it binds to over 100 in its calcium-free state and more than 500 when activated by calcium ions. While calmodulin function is modulated by calcium levels, the final cytosolic levels of calcium are often under calmodulin's control. Human calmodulin is encoded by three unique genes (CALM1-3), each of which produces an identical functional protein. The CALM genes are among the most mutation-intolerant sequences in the human genome. They are under the control of two calmodulin-binding transcription activators (CAMTAs) that are also ancient, highly conserved, and, as their name implies, under the control of calmodulin. Thus, calmodulin controls the transcription of the genes that encode it. Finally, two primary post-translational events that affect the function and stability of calmodulin are also regulated by it: methylation, which stabilizes the functional protein, and phosphorylation, which regulates its activity as needed. This review covers those topics to reveal that calmodulin controls its own expression, structure, and function, reflecting its unique position as an essential major regulator of cell function.

10.0 Comparative transcriptome analysis reveals coordinated ABA, ROS, MAPK, and hormone-mediated regulatory networks underlying cold tolerance in noni (Morinda citrifolia L.).
Liu XD, Wang XS, Li MF … Wassan GM.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-15
matched: ROS, MAPK*, signaling, ABA, hormone*, cold stress, stress respon*
abstract

Low-temperature stress severely limits the cultivation and geographical distribution of noni (Morinda citrifolia L.), a tropical medicinal plant with high economic and pharmaceutical values. However, the molecular mechanisms underlying cold tolerance in noni remain largely unknown. We conducted an integrated physiological, biochemical, and comparative transcriptomic analysis of a cold-susceptible genotype, Sanya (SY), and a cold-tolerant genotype, Kanghan-4 (KH-4), to elucidate the molecular mechanisms underlying differential cold tolerance. Relative to SY, KH-4 exhibited superior membrane stability, enhanced proline accumulation, and stronger antioxidant enzyme activities under cold stress. Transcriptome analysis revealed that KH-4 activated 1025 more differentially expressed genes (DEGs) than SY during the early cold exposure, reflecting a rapid transcriptional response. Comparative analysis across genotypes and time points identified 4286 candidate genes associated with cold tolerance, demonstrating that cold adaptation in noni involves coordinated activation of multiple stress-responsive pathways rather than a single dominant mechanism. Genes associated with ABA-dependent and ABA-independent signaling, ROS signaling, MAPK cascades, and other hormone pathways were preferentially induced in KH-4. Among the identified candidates, Contig_2712 exhibited the strongest genotype-specific induction, while NAC, AHP, GID2, ERF1, and PSαT were consistently downregulated in KH-4. Notably, the canonical ICE1-CBF cold signaling pathway was not prominently induced; instead, the data point to a MAPKKK, MKK, MPK, CAMTA3 module as a candidate alternative regulatory axis for cold tolerance in noni. These findings provide a molecular framework for cold tolerance in noni and identify candidate genes and pathways for functional studies and molecular breeding of cold-tolerant cultivars.

10.0 Proline transporters coordinate immune activation and regenerative capacity after injury in plants.
Xu D, Belew ZM, Ferreira Dias NC … Yang L.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell reports · 2026-10-22
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, CPK*, signaling, salicylic acid, immun*, defense, root
abstract

Effective survival after injury requires the precise coordination of immune activation and tissue regeneration, yet the molecular mechanisms balancing these opposing processes remain poorly understood. Here, we identify proline transporters as key regulators of this trade-off in plants. Genetic disruption of two wound-induced proline transporters, ProT2 and ProT3, enhances regenerative capacity and abolishes salicylic acid (SA)-mediated suppression of regeneration while preserving SA-responsive gene expression and SA-mediated antibacterial resistance. Mechanistically, a ProT3-CPK1 complex modulates wound-induced reactive oxygen species (ROS), sustaining a ROS level that restricts de novo root regeneration (DNRR) after wounding. Notably, pharmacological inhibition of proline transport functionally separates immune activation from regenerative suppression and promotes regeneration across multiple plant species. These findings support the role of proline transporters as a regulatory hub integrating stress-induced proline metabolism, SA signaling, and ROS homeostasis to coordinate immunity and regeneration, and highlight chemical inhibition of proline transport as a strategy to improve regeneration without compromising immune defense.

10.0 ROS Equilibrium in the Digestive Fluid of the Nepenthes × ventrata Trap at Various Developmental Stages.
Wal A, Staszek P, Jusińska K … Krasuska U.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthInternational journal of molecular sciences · 2026-09-23
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, H2O2, hydrogen peroxide, signalling, development
abstract

Carnivorous plants, such as Nepenthes (Nepenthaceae), possess a unique feature: the external digestion of animal-origin food. Nepenthes × ventrata pitchers are filled with digestive fluid containing: enzymes, small regulatory compounds and reactive oxygen species (ROS), which also act as signalling molecules. The aim of this study was to investigate the pattern of ROS content and to characterise enzymatic and non-enzymatic ROS modulators in the digestive fluid of N. × ventrata at various developmental stages of the pitcher. Significant changes were observed in the levels of hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) and hydroxyl radical (•OH) throughout trap development. The highest concentration of H2O2 was found in aged pitchers, while the highest level of •OH was recorded in freshly opened immature traps. These findings corresponded with superoxide dismutase and class III peroxidase activities. After maturation of the trap, there was an increase in the activity of diamine and polyamine oxidases. We suggest that the production of ROS in the digestive fluid of pitcher plants at different stages of trap development is controlled by the redox modulatory system. These findings provide a basis for further research into how the regulation of ROS content supports trap development and digestive functions.

9.5 Jasmonate-Induced Metabolic Reprogramming Enhances Fruit Quality, Bioactive Compounds, and Stress Tolerance.
Iqbal S, Tariq K, Elatafi E … Fang J.

abstract

Jasmonates (JAs), including jasmonic acid (JA), methyl jasmonate (MeJA), jasmonoyl-isoleucine (JA-Ile), and cis-jasmone, have emerged as central integrators of plant stress adaptation, developmental regulation, and fruit quality formation in horticultural crops. This review comprehensively summarizes recent advances in JA biosynthesis, metabolic conversion, signal perception, and downstream regulatory networks governing abiotic stress resilience and quality-associated traits. We highlight the coordinated chloroplast-peroxisome biosynthetic pathway leading to the formation of bioactive jasmonates and discuss the molecular basis of JA signaling mediated through the SCF^COI1-JAZ co-receptor complex and key transcriptional regulators, including MYC, MYB, NAC, ERF, WRKY, and bHLH families. However, major knowledge gaps remain regarding context-dependent JA responses, hormone crosstalk, metabolic trade-offs, and field-scale validation. Future research should integrate multi-omics, spatial analyses, and standardized field trials to develop precise strategies for improving fruit quality and stress tolerance.

9.5 Multi-scale characterization of calcium hydroxide mediated cell wall deconstruction during mild pretreatment.
Chen X, Liang S, Lu M … Jin M.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthTrees and woody plantsInternational journal of biological macromolecules · 2026-10-20
matched: calcium, Ca2+, cell wall*, development, lignin*
abstract

The ultrastructural and catalytic mechanisms underlying the efficient depolymerization of lignocellulosic biomass into high-quality sugar platforms under mild conditions remain poorly understood. A promising room temperature and high solid state pretreatment was called densifying lignocellulosic biomass with calcium hydroxide (ch) chemicals (DLC(ch)). It synergistically integrated mechanical densification and alkaline catalysis to overcome cell wall recalcitrance via compression-driven reagent penetration and expansion-induced structure loosening. Herein, we performed multi-modal visualization and characterization of corn stover (CS) cell walls to elucidate the underlying DLC(ch) mechanism. DLC pretreatment initially extruded chemical reagents into cell wall interior via mechanical compression. During post-treatment storage of DLC(ch)-CS, OH- sustainably catalyzed lignin and carbohydrate bond cleavage, while Ca2+ chelated with soluble intermediates to form "cotton-like" calcium-organic deposits (~50% reduction in phenolics). Subsequent mild steam treatment induced cell wall swelling and the calcium-organic deposits filled the nanoscale interstices between cellulose microfibrils, forming a "sandwich-like" interlayer structure. This unique structure greatly enhanced cellulases accessibility to the otherwise occluded cellulose domains. Calcium-based DLC-CS pellets achieved a total sugar conversion of over 90% at a solid loading of 35% (w/w) via enzymatic hydrolysis, and the corresponding ethanol concentration reached 82.6 g/L. This study provided fundamental insights for the development of low-cost and high-efficiency lignocellulosic biorefinery technologies.

9.0 Effects of H2O2 Micropipette Application on Photosynthetic Electron Transport in Chara Chloroplasts With Unimpaired and Short-Circuited H+ Flows.
Krupenina NA, Bulychev AA, Cherkashin AA.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthMethodsPhysiologia plantarum · 2026-09-01
matched: ROS, H2O2, hydrogen peroxide, chloroplast*, polarity, development, microscop*
abstract

Hydrogen peroxide is a byproduct of oxygenic photosynthesis. While the ways of H2O2 disposal in chloroplasts are mostly known, the influence of H2O2 on photosynthetic electron transport received less attention. According to various reports, H2O2 indirectly promotes electron transport on the acceptor side of photosystem I and activates cyclic electron transport (CET). Here the microinjection technique and PAM microfluorometry were applied to study the influence of H2O2 on linear and cyclic electron flows in internodal Chara cells. First, the cells loaded with a fluorogenic ROS-sensitive probe DCF were used to show that microinjection of H2O2 is followed by rapid intracellular penetration of H2O2 and its distribution with the streaming cytoplasm. Next, the regulatory influence of H2O2 on electron transport was examined in chloroplasts with unimpaired and short-circuited H+ flows. Linear electron transport (LET) was assessed with a Microscopy PAM fluorometer, whereas the inference on CET was based on measurements of the post-illumination rise of the chlorophyll fluorescence yield. The local application of H2O2 was followed by a temporary development of non-photochemical quenching (NPQ). In chloroplasts uncoupled with nigericin, the NPQ development after H2O2 microinjection was eliminated, while the polarity and amplitude of H2O2-induced F' fluorescence changes were reverted and enhanced, respectively. Post-illumination fluorescence responses (PIFR) in Chara were large and comparable in extent to changes induced by intense light. The development of PIFR after preillumination was instantly collapsed upon H2O2 injection, indicating that the reaction product arising in the presence of H2O2 redirected electron flow from the cyclic route along the competitive linear pathway.

9.0 Fruit growth rate influences cortical xylem transport, calcium delivery, and bitter pit incidence of apple.
Griffith C, Einhorn TC.
GrowthTrees and woody plantsJournal of experimental botany · 2026-10-23
matched: calcium, stoma*, xylem, growth
abstract

Bitter pit of apple is commonly ascribed to calcium dilution because symptoms are positively associated with fruit mass. A dilution effect implies that calcium transport or supply cannot keep pace with fruit growth but does not identify the mechanism. Disparate sink:source ratios or transient photosynthetic inhibition were used to alter 'Honeycrisp' fruit growth rate (FGR) to study fruit xylem transport kinetics and peel calcium concentration. FGR and xylem dysfunction increased with decreasing sink:source ratio. Xylem transport declined to 50% of maximum 1.7 weeks earlier under Low than High sink:source ratios and peel calcium decreased concomitantly with xylem transport. Treatment effects on bitter pit incidence were explained by integrating time-dependent xylem transport curves into an empirical 'cumulative xylem' function. Regressing this function on FGR conflated treatment differences. When photosynthetic light reactions were inhibited by episodic limb shading, FGR was reduced and xylem transport increased; shade removal caused transient surges in FGR and concomitant reduction in xylem transport. Changes in calcium concentration were less responsive to shade due to stomatal limits to water transport. Combined, the results support that FGR compromises xylem transport, effectively limiting calcium supply and exacerbating bitter pit, the magnitude of which depends on the onset of xylem dysfunction.

9.0 Leaf-to-root ROS signaling recruits rhizosphere Bacillus: a synbiotic mechanism for rice adaptability under foliar Cd stress.

abstract

In paddy fields near mining sites or transport corridors, elevated foliar cadmium (Cd) exposure is an emerging threat to rice (Oryza sativa) production. While rice recruits beneficial rhizosphere microbes against environmental stress, how this recruitment is coordinated under foliar Cd stress remains elusive. We assessed physiological responses to foliar Cd exposure in 26 rice cultivars and characterized rhizosphere bacterial communities and root exudates in six representative cultivars. Foliar Cd reduced leaf biomass by 6-29% and induced pronounced oxidative stress. In parallel, this foliar stress reshaped the rhizosphere microbiota, with consistent enrichment of Bacillus. 8.5 Comparative Performance of Biosorption Pretreatments for Vinasse Phytoremediation Using Salvinia minima.
Tabernero NR, Chocobar-Ponce S, Gultemirian ML … Prado C.
Abiotic stressGrowthTrees and woody plantsPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-10-04
matched: H2O2, hydrogen peroxide, pH, salinity, growth, phytoremediation
abstract

Sugarcane vinasse is a liquid waste generated during bioethanol production. It is characterized by high organic load, acidity, elevated salinity, and recalcitrant compounds, including soluble phenolic compounds and melanoidins, which can hinder its treatment and limit biological processes. This study evaluated batch and fixed-bed column biosorption as pretreatments coupled with phytoremediation using Salvinia minima at laboratory scale. System performance was evaluated based on changes in pH, electrical conductivity (EC), dissolved oxygen (DO), chemical oxygen demand (COD), true color, soluble phenolic compounds (SPCs), and the non-dialyzable melanoidin fraction. Plant physiological responses were evaluated by measuring hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) and malondialdehyde (MDA) contents. Following phytoremediation, vinasse pretreated by fixed-bed column biosorption showed greater removal of COD, SPCs, true color, and the non-dialyzable melanoidin fraction. Plants exposed to column-pretreated vinasse also showed lower H2O2 and MDA contents. In contrast, batch-pretreated vinasse adjusted to pH 6 showed an increase in the non-dialyzable melanoidin fraction after phytoremediation, suggesting structural modifications of Maillard reaction-derived compounds rather than their removal. The results indicate that the physicochemical changes occurring during biosorption improved the conditions of sugarcane vinasse for the growth of *S. minima*. Under the conditions evaluated in this study, fixed-bed column biosorption followed by phytoremediation proved to be a more suitable treatment than batch biosorption followed by phytoremediation for the integrated treatment of sugarcane vinasse.

8.5 Overexpression of a Malus baccata NAC Transcription Factor Gene MbNAC40 Increases Cold Tolerance in Arabidopsis.
Jiao K, Li Y, Song P … Han D.
Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesGrowthInternational journal of molecular sciences · 2026-09-23
matched: ROS, drought, freezing, nucleus, growth, woody
abstract

As a primary apple rootstock extensively cultivated across Northeast China, Malus baccata (L.) Borkh. constantly suffers from low temperature, drought and salt damage throughout its growth cycle. NAC transcription factors take critical parts in coordinating plant adaptation to various adverse environments, yet relevant functional research on this woody rootstock species remains limited. In this work, a NAC family gene designated MbNAC40 was cloned from M. baccata, and its biological function was explored via heterologous transformation in Arabidopsis thaliana. Feng X, Pang Z, Huang X … Guo B.

matched: calcium, pH, cadmium, root, roots, shoot*
abstract

Calcium (Ca) amendments are widely used to restrict cadmium (Cd) accumulation in rice; however, the mechanisms governing competitive adsorption between Ca and Cd in iron plaque (IP) of rice roots remain poorly understood. Here, hydroponic and adsorption experiments using detached roots and goethite (Gt), a simulant for IP, were conducted to investigate these interactions. Results showed that exogenous Ca did not affect IP formation but decreased Cd concentrations in IP, roots, and shoots by 21%-28%, 11%-25%, and 17%-27%, respectively. Detached-root adsorption experiments showed that Ca reduced Cd adsorption in IP by 70% when the Ca: Cd ratio reached 20, with root Cd uptake declining by 50% compared with the Ca-free treatment. Gt experiments introduced competitive mechanisms through a dual-pathway model. First, Ca adsorption shifted the point of zero charge of Gt towards higher pH, weakening electrostatic attraction for Cd. Second, spectroscopic evidence supported that Ca bound to surface hydroxyl groups (Fe-OH) through ligand exchange to form inner-sphere complexes (Fe-O-Ca), competitively occupying surface binding sites. This study not only revealed molecular-scale mechanisms of Ca/Cd competition in roots IP, but also provided new insight into quantitatively estimating Ca detoxification when optimizing Ca compound strategies in Cd-contaminated paddy soils beyond pH effects.

8.0 Melatonin-mediated metabolic reprogramming and stress adaptation in plants.
Hussain B, Colgecen H, Karahan H, Fariduddin Q.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlant cell reports · 2026-10-17
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, signalling, phytohormone*, defence, stress respon*, nuclear, growth, development
abstract

Key messageMelatonin acts as a multifunctional regulator of plant stress adaptation by reprogramming primary and secondary metabolism and coordinating ROS, nitric oxide, and phytohormone signalling. Integrating metabolomics with transcriptomics and proteomics provides new insights into these mechanisms and highlights melatonin as a promising strategy for enhancing crop resilience and productivity under diverse environmental stresses. Plants, being sessile, are continuously exposed to diverse environmental challenges, which disrupt cellular homeostasis and reduce crop productivity. To survive under such conditions, plants have evolved complex defence mechanisms involving metabolic reprogramming and coordinated molecular signalling networks. Among the regulatory molecules involved in stress adaptation, melatonin (N-acetyl-5-methoxytryptamine) has emerged as a multifunctional signalling compound that plays a pivotal role in plant growth, development, and stress tolerance. Acting as a potent antioxidant and signalling regulator, melatonin modulates multiple metabolic pathways associated with energy metabolism, amino acid biosynthesis, carbohydrate metabolism, and secondary metabolite production. These metabolic adjustments enhance the accumulation of osmoprotectants, antioxidants, and defence-related metabolites, thereby improving plant resilience under stress. Recent advances in metabolomics, together with transcriptomics and proteomics, have provided deeper insights into melatonin-mediated metabolic regulation in plants. High-throughput analytical platforms such as liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC-MS), gas chromatography-mass spectrometry (GC-MS), and nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) enable comprehensive profiling of stress-responsive metabolites and reveal how melatonin orchestrates metabolic network reprogramming. Emerging evidence indicates that melatonin interacts with reactive oxygen species, nitric oxide, and multiple phytohormones to regulate gene expression, protein abundance, and metabolite accumulation, thereby integrating stress perception with physiological adaptation. This review summarises current knowledge on melatonin biosynthesis, signalling pathways, cross-talk and its role in regulating primary and secondary metabolism under diverse stresses. Emphasis has been laid on metabolomics-driven insights and multi-omics integration to elucidate the molecular mechanisms underlying melatonin-mediated stress tolerance and to support strategies to improve crop resilience and productivity.

Wang X, Ni X, Liu X … Li C.

abstract

Summer heat stress constrains ginger production. Although calcium (Ca) enhances stress tolerance in various crops, its effects on heat tolerance, yield, and quality in ginger remain unclear. A pot experiment was conducted using Zingiber officinale cv. 'Laiwu Big Ginger' to evaluate Ca effects on plant growth and quality under natural summer high-temperature conditions. Six Ca rates (0, 15.2, 30.5, 45.7, 60.9, and 76.2 mmol Ca2+ plant-1) were established using calcium nitrate tetrahydrate [Ca(NO3)2·4H2O]. Seedling-stage physiological and biochemical parameters were analyzed with linear mixed-effects models; harvest-stage growth, biomass, and quality parameters were analyzed with ANOVA or non-parametric tests. PCA, correlation analysis, and membership function analysis were used for multivariate evaluation. At Day 16, the 60.9 mmol Ca2+ plant-1 treatment significantly improved photosynthetic capacity (chlorophyll +23.3%, Pn +13.0%), antioxidant enzyme activities (SOD, POD, CAT), and proline content, while reducing oxidative damage (MDA, REC) and ROS (O2 ·-, H2O2). Multivariate analyses confirmed positive correlations among photosynthetic traits, antioxidant activity, biomass, and quality parameters, with the 60.9 mmolCa2+ plant-1 treatment ranking highest in comprehensive evaluation by membership function analysis. Calcium application at this optimal rate, as determined under our experimental conditions, was associated with enhanced heat tolerance and subsequent growth and quality formation. These findings provide practical insights for sustaining ginger productivity under heat stress within the scope of our experimental conditions.

7.5 Calcium silicate effects on wheat phosphorus nutrition depend on phosphorus-source chemistry
Karwa S, Halpern M.
preprintGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-22
matched: calcium, pH, root, root architecture, shoot*, growth
abstract

Abstract Purpose Silicon (Si) amendments can improve crop phosphorus (P) nutrition, but the effect is inconsistent. We tested whether wheat’s response to a calcium-silicate amendment depends on the chemical form of P supplied, in a factorial sand-culture experiment. Methods Wheat ( Triticum aestivum cv. Omer) was grown in sand culture with soluble KH 2 PO 4 , hydroxyapatite (HA), FePO 4 or no added P (NoP), each with or without CaSiO 3 . CaCl 2 balanced calcium in the NoSi treatments. Shoot and root biomass, the shoot ionome, root architecture and soluble phosphate in an abiotic sand-mineral assay were measured. Results CaSiO 3 effects depended strongly on P source, and the response mode differed among sources. On hydroxyapatite, the limiting source, CaSiO 3 drove growth and P uptake, increasing shoot biomass by 85%, root biomass by 86% and estimated shoot P content by 56%, with a larger, coarser root system. On soluble KH 2 PO 4 it drove growth, increasing shoot biomass by 65%. On FePO 4 , already high-biomass, CaSiO 3 produced only a weak, non-significant trend but raised shoot Fe and Mn by 49 and 119%. With no added P it had little effect. An abiotic assay showed CaSiO 3 raised pH and conductivity but decreased soluble PO 4 -P by 79 to 98%. Conclusion A single calcium-silicate amendment produced several P-source-dependent response modes rather than one generic effect. The abiotic assay showed that CaSiO 3 locked up rather than released phosphate, so the hydroxyapatite response points to plant- and rhizosphere-mediated acquisition rather than bulk phosphate release.

7.5 Exogenous riboflavin activates stress-responsive gene networks in Arabidopsis.
Tsitsekian D, Kamargiakis E, Mylona P … Daras G.

matched: ROS, signal transduction, signalling, stress respon*, mitochondri*, seed*, growth
abstract

Riboflavin is the precursor of flavin mononucleotide (FMN) and flavin adenine dinucleotide (FAD), vital coenzymes for a wide array of plant metabolic processes, supporting photosynthesis, mitochondrial electron transport and fatty acid oxidation. While exogenous riboflavin application has been reported to improve plant responses to a range of abiotic and biotic stresses, the molecular responses associated with these protective effects remain largely unknown. Here, we present a comprehensive transcriptomic analysis of riboflavin-treated Arabidopsis seedlings, revealing significant changes in gene expression related to stress responses, signal transduction and secondary metabolism. Riboflavin treatment altered the expression of genes within specific cellular functional categories, suggesting a broad reprogramming of plant metabolism. Exogenous riboflavin caused pronounced changes in the expression of stress-responsive transcriptional regulators, including DREB19 and DREB2C, which are involved in stress-responsive signalling pathways. The transcriptional changes were consistent with a shift from growth-associated to stress-responsive functions, including repression of photosynthesis-related genes and activation of pathways associated with oxidative stress responses. Collectively, these results indicate that an acute increase in riboflavin availability triggers coordinated transcriptional and redox responses associated with stress-related pathways and flavin metabolism, highlighting the importance of tightly regulated riboflavin homeostasis in plants.

matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, effector*, pathogen*, nuclear
abstract

The cytosolic iron-sulfur (Fe-S) cluster assembly (CIA) targeting complex mediates the maturation of cytosolic and nuclear Fe-S proteins and governs core eukaryotic cellular processes. Here, we show that the Magnaporthe oryzae avirulence effector AvrPi9 targets the host CIA targeting complex to suppress rice blast resistance. 7.0 Apoplast-Confined Nanozyme Catalysis for Selective 1O2 Generation and Host Defense in Plants.
Yan Y, Huang R, Kang S … Wang F.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesACS nano · 2026-09-01
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, apoplast*, immun*, pathogen*, defense, cell wall*
abstract

Reactive oxygen species (ROS)-based antimicrobial strategies in plants are often constrained by limited oxidant selectivity and collateral phytotoxicity. Here, we report an apoplast-confined nanozyme that integrates selective singlet oxygen (1O2) catalysis with host defense activation. The copper-iron Prussian blue analogue nanozyme CuFePBA exhibits oxidase-like activity under physiologically relevant conditions, converting molecular oxygen into 1O2 without detectable generation of superoxide or hydroxyl radicals. Following foliar application, CuFePBA is retained within the apoplast because of exclusion from the symplast by the plant cell wall. In this extracellular compartment, localized 1O2 production damages bacterial membranes, suppresses respiration, and eliminates apoplastic pathogens while minimizing intracellular oxidative stress. The resulting apoplastic redox perturbation also elicits a transient intracellular ROS burst, promotes cell wall reinforcement, activates pattern-triggered immunity, and induces sustained expression of defense-related genes. Through this catalytic-immunological coupling, CuFePBA enhances disease resistance in planta without detectable phytotoxicity. These findings establish spatially confined nanozyme catalysis as an effective strategy for combining antimicrobial activity with host-compatible immune activation and highlight microenvironmental confinement as a general design principle for biologically compatible nanomaterials in plant protection.

abstract

Environmental contamination with heavy metals severely impairs crop productivity, primarily by inhibiting growth and triggering oxidative damage. To counteract these adverse effects, plants undergo complex metabolic reprogramming. Among these adaptations, the accumulation of purine-derived ureides-particularly allantoin-has been proposed as a protective mechanism, though its role under copper (Cu) stress remains unexplored. These results reveal that the loss of ALN function promotes a multi-compartmental antioxidant response under Cu toxicity, highlighting the pivotal role of purine catabolism in plant redox homeostasis and pointing to ureide metabolism as a potential avenue for enhancing crop stress tolerance.

7.0 Transforming Cadmium in Paddy Soils into a Catalytic Driver for Chlorantraniliprole Degradation: Implications for an Integrated Strategy for Tackling Heavy Metal-Organic Cocontamination.
Yang L, Hou S, Zhou D … Zhang T.
Abiotic stressEnvironmental science & technology · 2026-09-01
matched: calcium, reactive oxygen species, heavy metal*, cadmium
abstract

Developing remediation strategies for heavy metal-organic cocontamination in paddy soils is imperative to ensure food safety. Current soil remediation strategies typically address these pollutant classes separately, often creating trade-offs in which mitigating one class of contaminants exacerbates the mobility or persistence of the other. Here, we demonstrate an integrated strategy that transforms heavy metal contaminants into a functional driver for organic contaminant degradation. Using cadmium (Cd)-chlorantraniliprole (CAP) cocontaminated paddy soil as a model system, we show that amendment with 0.5 wt % porous calcium-doped iron oxide (P-Ca@Fe2O3) achieves efficient Cd sequestration via Ca-Cd isomorphous substitution.
