By repurposing a soil pollutant into a functional component of the remediation process, our strategy provides a scalable, closed-loop catalytic process that restores contaminated croplands with minimal external inputs while safeguarding food safety.

6.5 Cold tolerance mechanisms in apple: integrative multi-omics reveals rootstock-mediated resilience via lipid remodeling and cryoprotectant-antioxidant flux.
Jahed KR, Saini AK, Sherif SM.
GrowthTrees and woody plantsPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-21
matched: ROS, H2O2, leaves, woody, phenolog*
abstract

Low temperatures threaten deciduous woody perennials, with floral tissues representing the most thermally vulnerable phenological stage. Although rootstocks are one of the primary determinants of scion thermal resilience, the systemic metabolic mechanisms conferring this protection remain poorly resolved. Here, we employed an integrated multi-omic framework combining metabolomics and transcriptomics, coupled with physiological validations of cellular redox status, to dissect the biochemical architecture of rootstock-mediated frost resilience in 'Gala' apple grafted onto cold-tolerant (B9) and cold-susceptible (M26) rootstocks. Spatiotemporal profiling of floral buds, scion leaves, and rootstocks revealed divergent metabolic reprogramming between genotypes. The B9 system orchestrated a coordinated adaptive response characterized by the selective accumulation of low-molecular-weight cryoprotectants (e.g., trehalose, melibiose, and Cinn-AraGlc), localized ROS accumulation, and hydroxyl-rich antioxidant flavonoids maintaining redox homeostasis. This was coupled with active membrane lipid remodeling evidenced by induction of glycerophospholipid DL-GPC and bioactive lysophospholipid LysoPA. Physiological quantification directly validated this compartmentalized oxidative environment, revealing that B9 rootstock tissues exhibited a sustained 4-fold elevation in basal O2•- and a 2.5-fold increase in H2O2 concentrations relative to M26, while scion floral buds maintained near-baseline levels (40% O2•- and 22% H2O2 elevations). Conversely, M26 exhibited a degradative lipid response marked by rapid declines in structural phosphatidylcholines, plasmalogen-type glycerophospholipids, and phenolic defenses, alongside pro-apoptotic ceramide induction. Intersectional network analysis highlighted a potential synchronized acropetal mobilization of cryoprotectants across the graft union exclusively in B9; however, this proposed vascular-mediated systemic priming mechanism necessitates functional validation. Multi-omic integration via DIABLO revealed that B9 resilience is mechanistically anchored in the co-activation of lipid metabolism and the TCA cycle, driven by linoleic acid metabolism and branched-chain amino acid catabolism. These findings suggest that genotype-specific membrane remodeling competency and cryoprotectant-antioxidant flux, rather than differential stress perception alone, play a critical role in modulating rootstock-mediated frost resilience in apple.

matched: Ca2+, H2O2, CNGC*, roots
abstract

Much like in animals, both the 3',5'- and the 2,'3'-cAMP isomers are present in plants. The former is the enzymatic product of adenylate cyclases (ACs), the latter is an RNA degradation product. While there is increasing evidence that both isomers can elicit or modulate a broad range of physiological responses, the question of the specificity of responses to isomers has remained, at least partially, unresolved. To delineate isomer-specific responses in Arabidopsis thaliana, we applied a comparative proteomics approach. 6.5 Mechanosensation of rice root border cells activates serotonin-mediated pre-invasion behaviors in Meloidogyne graminicola via DEG/ENaC channels.

abstract

This work uncovers a previously uncharacterized mechanosensory pathway in plant‑nematode interactions and identifies nematode DEG/ENaC channels as promising targets for disrupting parasitism, offering a new mechanosensory‑based strategy for nematode control.

6.0 Edge-deployable greenhouse tomato cluster harvesting robot integrating YOLOv8n-BiFPN-WIoU and ROS-based autonomous control.
Meng F, Wang Z, Wang H … Shi Y.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityMethodsFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-01
matched: ROS, effector*, deep learning
abstract

ObjectiveTo develop an edge-deployable greenhouse tomato cluster harvesting robot capable of accurate fruit detection, three-dimensional localization, autonomous navigation, and robotic harvesting under complex greenhouse conditions. The objective was to improve detection performance under fruit occlusion and variable illumination while maintaining real-time inference and stable robotic operation on embedded computing hardware.MethodsA lightweight YOLOv8n-based tomato detection model, termed YOLOv8n-BiFPN-WIoU, was developed by incorporating a bidirectional feature pyramid network and Wise-IoU loss to improve multiscale feature fusion and localization accuracy. An RGB-D vision system based on the Intel RealSense D435 was used for three-dimensional fruit localization. The robotic system integrated a rail-guided mobile platform, a six-axis manipulator, a dual-claw harvesting end-effector, and a ROS-based distributed control architecture. The vision model was optimized and deployed on an NVIDIA Jetson Nano using ONNX and TensorRT. Robot kinematics, hand-eye transformation, trajectory planning, and autonomous harvesting procedures were integrated to enable station-to-station operation and fruit harvesting.ResultsThe proposed YOLOv8n-BiFPN-WIoU model achieved a precision of 88.534%, recall of 89.377%, F1-score of 88.954%, mAP@0.5 of 92.338%, and mAP@0.5:0.95 of 71.368%. The proposed model increased the parameter count from 3.2 million to 3.6 million and computational complexity from 19.6 to 22.4 GFLOPs. After ONNX and TensorRT optimization, the model achieved an inference speed of 21.1 frames per second at an input resolution of 640 × 640 with batch size 1 on the Jetson Nano. The robotic system achieved a mean positioning error of 3.70 ± 0.32 mm across five representative targets, demonstrating the feasibility of accurate visual localization and robotic positioning for greenhouse tomato harvesting.ConclusionThe proposed edge-deployable greenhouse tomato cluster harvesting robot integrates lightweight deep learning, RGB-D three-dimensional localization, ROS-based autonomous control, rail-guided mobile operation, and robotic harvesting into a unified system. The YOLOv8n-BiFPN-WIoU model improved tomato detection performance while maintaining real-time inference on embedded hardware, and the integrated vision and manipulation system demonstrated accurate positioning suitable for autonomous harvesting. The results indicate that the proposed architecture provides a practical foundation for intelligent greenhouse tomato harvesting under complex visual and operational conditions.

Magiera A, Marchelak A, Kołodziejczyk-Czepas J … Olszewska MA.

abstract

Prunus serotina Ehrh. (Rosaceae) is one of the most widespread invasive tree species in Europe. Its abundant floral biomass remains understudied, despite its potential as a source of valuable bioactive compounds. Our study provides a comprehensive phytochemical characterization of P. serotina flower extracts and the first evaluation of their biological activity in models related to inflammation and oxidative stress. Phytochemical composition was analyzed using UHPLC-PDA-ESI-MS3, HPLC-PDA, and spectrophotometric techniques. Over 60 specialized metabolites were fully or partially identified, primarily polyphenolic compounds (flavonols, phenolic acids, proanthocyanidins), and the content of major constituents was quantified using authentic reference standards, providing the most detailed profiling data on P. serotina flowers to date. The total phenolic content of the extracts, obtained using water, 70% ethanol-water, ethyl acetate, and n-butanol, ranged from 119.05 to 144.97 mg/g dw. Biological potential of the extracts was evaluated by assessing their effects on pro-inflammatory cytokine release (TNF-α), the inflammation-related enzyme elastase (ELA-2), and reactive oxygen species (ROS) level in stimulated human immune cells (neutrophils), as well as their ability to enhance plasma antioxidant capacity (NEAC) and protect human plasma proteins and lipids from peroxynitrite-induced damage. At concentrations of 1-100 µg/mL, the extracts decreased TNF-α, ELA-2, and ROS levels by up to 46%, 43%, and 74%, respectively, and reduced 3-nitrotyrosine by up to 72%. These findings highlight the potential of P. serotina biomass as a sustainable source of bioactive compounds for inflammation- and oxidative stress-related disorders, particularly cardiovascular diseases (CVDs). However, further in vivo studies are needed to verify these effects under physiological conditions and assess their pharmacological potential.

6.0 Molecular Mechanism of AtCAX3-Induced BPA Tolerance in Plants.
Shin H, Kim D, Lee J … Hwang S.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-10-05
matched: Ca2+, ROS, defense, vacuol*
abstract

Cation/H+ Exchangers (CAXs) are involved in the sequestration of cations such as Ca2+, Mn2+, Li+, Zn2+, and Cd2+ from the cytosol into the vacuole using proton gradients. Our previous studies demonstrated the involvement of AtCAX3 in Cd2+ tolerance. In this study, we investigated whether AtCAX3 is also involved in tolerance to the endocrine disruptor Bisphenol A (BPA). Furthermore, to examine the role of the autoinhibitory domain of AtCAX3 in BPA tolerance, the empty vector (pYES5), AtCAX3, Δ36-AtCAX3 (deletion of the N-terminal regulatory region; NRR), and Δ90-AtCAX3 (deletion of both the NRR and regulatory-dependent region; RDR) were overexpressed in Saccharomyces cerevisiae and the atcax3 knockout mutant of Arabidopsis thaliana. In Arabidopsis, BPA tolerance, Ca2+ levels, and antioxidant enzyme activities were highest in Δ90-AtCAX3-overexpressing plants, followed by Δ36-AtCAX3, AtCAX3, Col-0, and atcax3. Conversely, ROS levels followed the opposite pattern. Interestingly, the expression levels of Ca2+ importer genes (AtCNGC10 and AtMCA1) were increased, whereas those of Ca2+ exporter genes (AtACA10) were decreased in the order of Δ90-AtCAX3 > Δ36-AtCAX3 > AtCAX3 > Col-0 > atcax3. These results suggest that elevated Ca2+ levels may contribute to ROS reduction. Taken together, deletion of the autoinhibitory domain of AtCAX3 enhances BPA tolerance by reducing BPA-induced ROS accumulation through increased Ca2+ accumulation and activation of antioxidant defense systems. Furthermore, enhanced Ca2+ homeostasis may interfere with the positive feedback loop between BPA-induced ROS generation and Ca2+ spikes. These findings can be applied to develop BPA phytoremediators and BPA-resilient crops.

6.0 Radical-Mediated integrated sludge treatment Strategy: Simultaneous enhancement of dewatering Performance, attenuation of antibiotic resistance Genes, and sustainable resource reutilization.
Ma M, Cong L, An N … Li Q.
Bioresource technology · 2026-10-24
matched: calcium, ROS, reactive oxygen species
abstract

This study presents a systematic investigation of the performance, underlying mechanisms, and resource recovery potential of a calcium oxide-activated persulfate (PS/CaO) system for sludge treatment. Following PS/CaO conditioning, sludge dewaterability was markedly enhanced, with the capillary suction time (CST) reduced by 45.5%. This enhancement was primarily through structural disruption of extracellular polymeric substances (EPS) and subsequent release of bound water. The PS/CaO process also yielded marked attenuation of antibiotic resistance genes (ARGs), reducing their absolute abundance by over 1.5 log units across all monitored ARG targets. Compositional analysis revealed that sludge organic matter, such as proteins and carbohydrates, underwent progressive degradation and concurrent humification, yielding humic-like and fulvic-like acids at concentrations of 5.7% and 4.2% (w/w), respectively. Reactive oxygen species (ROS) identification confirmed sulfate radicals (SO4·-) and hydroxyl radicals (·OH) as the dominant reactive species responsible for dissolved organic matter (DOM) transformation with oxygenation, Maillard condensation, and dealkylation identified as key reaction pathways. Pot experiments further demonstrated that application of the treated sludge at a 2% (w/w) dosage significantly increased wheat fresh biomass by 87.83% relative to the control. Life-cycle assessment further confirmed its superior environmental compatibility relative to aerobic composting. This study presents a technically viable approach and a robust theoretical basis for achieving enhanced sludge dewatering, effective risk mitigation, and safe resource reutilization.

18.5 Pantoea ananatis- triggered systemic resistance requires root sensing through the LORE receptor kinase in Arabidopsis
Duchateau S, Crouzet J, Cordelier S … Dorey S
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthJournal of Experimental Botany · 2026-10-26
matched: kinase*, receptor kinase*, signaling, receptor*, ethylene, jasmon*, salicylic acid, immun*, MAMP*, pathogen*, Pseudomonas syringae, Botrytis
abstract

Abstract Systemic immunity induced by beneficial bacteria enhances plant resilience to subsequent pathogen attacks. However, the mechanisms by which plants perceive these bacteria and translate this perception into a systemic response remain poorly understood. The Brassicaceae LIPOOLIGOSACCHARIDE-SPECIFIC REDUCED ELICITATION (LORE) receptor kinase is the direct sensor of bacterial medium-chain-length 3-hydroxy fatty acids (mc-3-OH-FAs) and 3-(3-hydroxyalkanoyloxy)alkanoic acids (HAAs), both of which activate local Brassicaceae immunity. Here, we show that the HAA-producing bacterium Pantoea ananatis BRT175 can colonize Arabidopsis thaliana roots independently of the production of HAAs, but these compounds induce a lore-dependent local immune response in roots. P. ananatis triggers induced systemic resistance (ISR) against the necrotrophic fungus Botrytis cinerea, but not against the hemibiotrophic bacterium Pseudomonas syringae pv. tomato DC3000. This ISR can be mimicked by HAAs or 3-hydroxy-decanoic acid (3-OH-C10), the strongest agonist of LORE. Both bacterium- and 3-hydroxylated fatty acid MAMP-induced ISR against B. cinerea rely on LORE-mediated sensing, involve salicylic acid, jasmonic acid/ethylene signaling pathways, and activate similar defense gene expression profiles in infected leaves. Our results demonstrate that lipidic microbe-associated molecular patterns (MAMPs) produced by beneficial microorganisms are necessary and sufficient to trigger rhizobacteria-mediated ISR and could differentially activate systemic resistance against biotrophic and necrotrophic pathogens.

16.5 The interactome of the receptor kinase THESEUS1 reveals how cell wall integrity is linked to hormone signalling in Arabidopsis
Zwartkruis, S. T. W., Haeger … T.
preprintCa2+HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunitybioRxiv · 2026-10-23
matched: calcium, kinase*, receptor kinase*, signaling, signalling, receptor*, phytohormone*, hormone*, immun*, cell wall*, endoplasmic reticulum
abstract

The receptor kinase THESEUS1 (THE1) activates responses to impairment of cell wall integrity (CWI). However, the interaction partners enabling THE1 to mediate the responses remain underexplored, impeding a mechanistic understanding of THE1. We produced a set of THE1 GFP fusion constructs in which THE1 is either wild type, mutated in its kinase domain, has a loss of function mutation in its ectodomain, or lacks the kinase or extracellular domain. We used these constructs to perform localization studies and immunoprecipitation liquid chromatography tandem mass spectrometry under control and stress conditions (isoxaben induced cell wall damage and sorbitol induced hyperosmotic stress) to identify the THE1 interactome. The interactome of THE1 changed in a context dependent manner, linking THE1 to known signaling components in CWI maintenance and phytohormone response (such as proton pumps, calcium pumps, other receptor kinases or cellulose synthesis regulators). Moreover, we identified novel interactors, associating THE1 with hitherto unexplored signaling pathways in response to CWI impairment. Our results also suggest that the THE1G37D protein (corresponding to the the1 1 allele) is retained in the endoplasmic reticulum, explaining its loss of function phenotype. The THE1 interactome and localization data provide the foundation to build a mechanistic understanding of THE1 mediated CWI maintenance.

14.5 A phosphorylation-gated GAPDH-MAPK6-LBD1 module controls drought-responsive transcription.
He Z, Wang Z, Gao C … Wang Y.
Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesTrees and woody plantsScience advances · 2026-10-21
matched: kinase*, MAPK*, phosphoryl*, signaling, drought, stress respon*, nucleus, nuclear, birch, Betula
abstract

Drought adaptation requires rapid spatial reorganization of signaling proteins to reprogram stress-responsive transcription, yet how phosphorylation coordinates this process remains unclear. Here we aimed to define how phosphorylation-gated protein relocalization regulates a BpLBD1-centered drought pathway in Betula platyphylla (birch). Drought activated two kinase branches: BpAGC phosphorylated BpMAPK6 at Ser318 within a cryptic nuclear localization signal, promoting importin α recognition, whereas BpCKII phosphorylated BpGAPDH at Thr109, Thr270, Ser279 and Thr298, promoting importin β recognition. Dual phosphorylation enabled coordinated nuclear accumulation of BpMAPK6 and BpGAPDH. In the nucleus, phosphorylated BpGAPDH acted as a scaffold-like adaptor coupling BpMAPK6 to BpLBD1, enabling BpMAPK6-mediated phosphorylation of BpLBD1 at Ser29. This weakened BpLBD1 binding to the BpMYB123 promoter, reduced expression of the drought-sensitivity regulator BpMYB123, and de-repressed drought-responsive genes such as BpRD29B. Disrupting BpGAPDH or BpLBD1 Ser29 phosphorylation compromised drought tolerance. Thus, a phospho-spatial GAPDH-MAPK6-LBD1 switch couples nuclear protein assembly to drought-responsive transcription.

Zhu W, Huang M, Li X … Li B.

matched: kinase*, receptor kinase*, RLK*, phosphoryl*, signalling, receptor*, immun*, pattern recognition receptor*, nuclear
abstract

The perception of damage-associated molecular patterns and phytocytokines by pattern recognition receptors activates danger-triggered immunity, thereby amplifying plant immune responses. However, how phytocytokine receptor complexes are assembled, activated and regulated remains poorly understood. Here we show that all six members of the nuclear-shuttle-protein-interacting kinase (NIK) family, a clade within LRRII-RLK subfamily, contribute redundantly but distinctively to immune responses triggered by Peps and SCOOP12 in Arabidopsis. 14.0 CRK5 preserves antioxidant homeostasis and prevents cell death during dark-induced senescence through inhibiting the salicylic acid signaling pathway.
Kamran M, Burdiak P, Rusaczonek A … Karpiński SM.
Ca2+HormonesAbiotic stressBMC plant biology · 2026-07-25
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, kinase*, receptor kinase*, receptor-like kinase*, signaling, receptor*, salicylic acid, stress respon*, autophagy, leaf
abstract

BackgroundDark-induced senescence (DIS) is a widely used model for dissecting the regulatory mechanisms that manage leaf aging, redox imbalance, and cell death (CD) in plants. Salicylic acid (SA) is a central hormonal regulator of these processes. However, the mechanism involving upstream components, which integrate SA-dependent pathways with antioxidant homeostasis during DIS, remains unresolved. CYSTEINE-RICH RECEPTOR-LIKE KINASE 5 (CRK5) is a membrane-localized protein that plays a role in developmental and stress-responsive pathways. Its promoter contains multiple W-box cis-elements, indicating regulation by WRKY factors in SA-mediated pathways. This study investigates how CRK5 modulates SA-dependent CD and antioxidant dynamics during DIS.ResultsIn this study, SA-accumulating mutant crk5 exhibited accelerated senescence, elevated electrolyte leakage, enhanced micro-lesion formation, and markedly increased reactive oxygen species (ROS) accumulation under both control and dark conditions. These phenotypes were accompanied by a substantial reduction in carotenoid and xanthophyll pools, enhanced accumulation of phenolic compounds, and increased free radical scavenging capacity, including ascorbate peroxidase, catalase, and superoxide dismutase activities. Importantly, crk5 phenotype was fully reverted in crk5sid2 and crk5NahG double mutants, confirming that crk5 DIS phenotype is induced by activation of the SA signaling pathway. Transcriptome profiling revealed extensive deregulation of senescence-, CD-, and redox-associated genes in crk5 during darkness, including strong induction of SAGs, metacaspases, autophagy, and antioxidant-related transcripts. The line with constitutively enhanced SA level (cpr1), used as a control, showed similar phenotypes to crk5, although transcriptional reprogramming was largely absent in cpr1 after darkness, highlighting CRK5 as a key upstream negative regulator of SA-mediated CD and positive regulator of antioxidant homeostasis.ConclusionOur work presents CRK5 as a central regulatory hub that inhibits the SA signaling, ROS burst, and CD activation during DIS. Loss of CRK5 function is associated with the activation of SA signaling, altered antioxidant systems, increased ROS burden, and ROS-driven CD acceleration, resulting in accelerated senescence. Conversely, suppression of SA-biosynthesis or catabolism in a crk5 background restores the wild-type phenotype. These findings position this receptor kinase as a key mediator that coordinates hormonal, metabolic, and oxidative pathways to maintain leaf viability, providing mechanistic insight into the control of stress-induced senescence and CD in Arabidopsis (Arabidopsis thaliana).

13.5 MAKR6 integrates TMK and CAMEL/CANAR signalling for auxin canalization in Arabidopsis.
Ge Z, Koczka L, Mazur E … Friml J.
HormonesCell biology and organellesGrowthNature plants · 2026-10-20
matched: kinase*, receptor-like kinase*, RLK*, signalling, receptor*, auxin*, IAA, PIN\d+, phytohormone*, trafficking, leaf, development
abstract

Adaptive plant development is orchestrated, among other means, by directional, intercellular transport of the phytohormone auxin. Self-organizing phenomena such as flexible vasculature formation depend on auxin canalization, which manifests as the gradual formation of auxin-transporting channels through feedback between auxin signalling and transport. Here we identify MAKR6 as a component of this feedback. MAKR6 transcripts accumulate in vascular cells and are tightly regulated by auxin via the Aux/IAA-ARF-WRKY23 transcriptional cascade. MAKR6 is required for auxin canalization-dependent processes, including leaf venation, vascular regeneration and de novo channel formation from local auxin sources. Mechanistically, MAKR6 interacts with the PIN1 auxin transporter and modulates its trafficking and polarization. MAKR6 also associates with and integrates TMK1/4 and CAMEL-CANAR, two key receptor-like kinase complexes involved in canalization. Together, our study establishes MAKR6 as a multifaceted regulator that couples transcriptional auxin signalling to PIN1 repolarization and coordinates multiple RLK-mediated pathways into a concerted process. This provides mechanistic insight into auxin canalization and exemplifies a framework for exploring similar regulatory nodes in other developmental contexts.

10.0 The BIK1-WRKY9 module activates jasmonate signaling to confer apple resistance to Alternaria blotch.
Bai Y, Shan D, Wang C … Kong J.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthJournal of integrative plant biology · 2026-10-15
matched: kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, receptor*, jasmon*, JA, immun*, PTI, Botrytis, leaves
abstract

Apple Alternaria blotch, caused by Alternaria alternata f. sp. mali (A. alternata), is a devastating fungal disease threatening global apple production. However, the early signaling pathway underlying apple resistance to A. alternata remains unclear. In this study, through transcriptomic analysis, we identified that MdWRKY9 is a key positive regulator of apple resistance to A. alternata, whose expression is induced in the resistant "Hanfu" while being reduced in the susceptible "Gala" following A. alternata infection. Stable overexpression of MdWRKY9 enhanced apple resistance to A. alternata, whereas RNA interference (RNAi)-mediated knockdown of MdWRKY9 increased susceptibility. Together, our findings define a complete PTI-associated signaling module in which the MdBIK1-MdWRKY9 complex enhances JA-mediated immunity against A. alternata by directly repressing MdJAZ8. This study refines the regulatory mechanism by which the BIK1 family kinases modulate JA signaling, providing new insights into the plant immune signaling network.

9.5 WAK This Way: The Roles of Wall-Associated Kinases in Plant Disease Resistance.
Zhong T, Ojiambo PS, Rellán-Álvarez R, Balint-Kurti P.

matched: kinase*, receptor-like kinase*, RLK*, signaling, receptor*, immun*, pathogen*, cell wall*
abstract

Plant wall-associated kinases (WAKs) and WAK-like kinases (WAKLs) are closely related subfamilies of receptor-like kinases (RLKs) that bridge the plant cell wall and cytoplasm and mediate essential signaling functions. Beyond their developmental roles, accumulating evidence highlights their important contributions to plant immunity. Initially identified in Arabidopsis thaliana, these proteins have emerged as important regulators of plant responses to biotic stresses. Notably, recent genetic evidence has challenged the long-held view that Arabidopsis WAKs serve as bona fide receptors for oligogalacturonides (OGs), highlighting unresolved questions about how WAKs and WAKLs perceive pathogen invasion. 8.5 Foundational characterization of tomato fruit RALF peptides reveals structural and functional specialization within the SlRALF family.
Montano JA, Carrera M, Wang X … Doblas VG.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesGrowthPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-22
matched: signaling, RALF*, immun*, cell wall*, root, root growth, growth, development
abstract

Rapid Alkalinization Factor (RALF) peptides regulate plant growth and cell wall signaling, but their roles in fruit development remain unclear. Here, we characterized tomato (Solanum lycopersicum) fruit-associated RALF peptides and their interactions with leucine-rich repeat extensins (LRXs). Expression analyses identified SlRALF5, SlRALF7, and SlRALF10 as the main fruit-expressed RALFs. 7.0 CaSNF1 interacts with and phosphorylates CaHD-ZIP to enhance cold tolerance in pepper.
Ullah I, Pei Y, Wang M … Chen R.
Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesGrowthTAG. Theoretical and applied genetics. Theoretische und angewandte Genetik · 2026-10-23
matched: kinase*, phosphoryl*, cold stress, stress respon*, nucleus, nuclear, growth
abstract

Cold stress severely restricts pepper (Capsicum annuum L.) growth and productivity by disrupting cellular homeostasis and inducing oxidative damage. Although HD-ZIP transcription factors play important roles in plant abiotic stress responses, the molecular mechanisms regulating their function during cold stress remain largely unknown. Here, we characterized the pepper HD-ZIP transcription factor CaHD-ZIP and demonstrated that it is a nuclear-localized, cold-responsive protein that positively contributes to cold stress tolerance. To further investigate the regulatory mechanism underlying CaHD-ZIP function, a yeast two-hybrid (Y2H) screen identified the sucrose non-fermenting 1-related protein kinase CaSNF1 as a previously unrecognized interacting partner of CaHD-ZIP. Complementary loss- and gain-of-function analyses, including virus-induced gene silencing and transient overexpression in pepper together with stable overexpression in Arabidopsis, demonstrated that CaSNF1 positively regulates cold tolerance. This study advances our understanding of the molecular mechanisms underlying pepper cold tolerance and provides potential genetic targets for improving cold resilience in pepper and other Solanaceae crops.

7.0 Plant Responses to Heavy Metal Stress: Molecular Mechanisms, Transcriptomic Regulation, and Multi-Omics Integration in Crop Improvement.
Sapakhova Z, Daurov D, Daurova A … Shamekova M.
reviewPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-29
matched: MAPK*, signaling, defense, heavy metal*, vacuol*, growth, single-cell
abstract

Heavy-metal contamination poses a serious threat to plant growth, productivity, and resilience, as it causes complex changes in cellular homeostasis, redox balance, element transport, and metabolism. This review summarizes current understanding of the molecular mechanisms by which plants adapt to heavy-metal stress, with a particular focus on transcriptional and omics approaches. The main detoxification and defense mechanisms are discussed, including chelation and vacuolar sequestration, antioxidant regulation, hormonal signaling, and ion transport. Particular attention is paid to the role of MAPK cascades and transcription factors in coordinating the expression of genes involved in metal transport, maintenance of redox homeostasis, and detoxification. Analysis of transcriptomic studies shows that the plant response is characterized by pronounced temporal, tissue-specific, and genotypic specificity and involves the reprogramming of genes involved in transport, antioxidant defense, secondary metabolism, and signaling pathways. At the same time, transcript levels do not always reflect the functional state of proteins and metabolic processes. Therefore, the integration of transcriptomics with genomics, epigenomics, proteomics, metabolomics, and single-cell and spatial approaches combined with functional validation is proposed as a key direction that may facilitate the identification of robust molecular markers and functionally validated genes for improving crop tolerance to heavy-metal contamination.

6.0 An AGC kinase cascade regulates ROP signaling for the correct timing of pollen germination.
Zhao T, Lepper A, Anzenberger F … Denninger P.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthThe New phytologist · 2026-10-21 · also: New Phytologist
matched: kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, plasma membrane, flowering, seed*, germination, growth
abstract

In flowering plants, the timing of pollen germination is crucial for successful fertilization and an important trait for seed and fruit crops. Environmental factors tightly regulate pollen germination, with germination-suppressing conditions on the pollen-producing anther and germination-promoting conditions on the receiving stigma. Using genetic, biochemical, and cell biological methods, we show that this environmental sensitivity of pollen germination in Arabidopsis thaliana is regulated by a 3-PHOSPHOINOSITIDE-DEPENDENT KINASE 1 (PDK1)-AGC1.5/AGC1.7 kinase cascade. We show that PDK1 hyperphosphorylates downstream AGC1.5/7 kinases to enhance their activity. Pollen-specific phosphoproteomics identified multiple proteins involved in pollen growth whose phosphorylation is controlled by AGC1.5/7. Two germination-promoting RHO OF PLANTS GUANINE NUCLEOTIDE EXCHANGE FACTORS (ROPGEFs) are phosphorylated by AGC1.5/7 at a novel site, reducing ROPGEF plasma membrane polarization. Loss of PDK1-AGC kinase signaling resulted in precocious pollen germination on the anther, which was rescued by high AGC1.5 kinase activity. Conversely, AGC1.5/7 overexpression or a phosphomimic ROPGEF version inhibited precocious pollen germination. Distinct subcellular localization of PDK1 and AGC1 kinases upon pollen germination is likely to alleviate their inhibitory effect. Our data indicate that the correct timing of pollen germination requires the fine-tuned activity of a PDK1-AGC1 kinase cascade inhibiting ROPGEFs on the anther, preventing precocious pollen germination.

6.0 Cross-species transcriptomic plasticity and root architectural adaptations in Brachypodium (Brachypodium distachyon) and barley (Hordeum vulgare) genotypes under different ammonium and nitrate availability.
Rouina H, Singh D, Arlt C … Stich B.
Abiotic stressGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-09
matched: signaling, FERONIA, nitrate, root, lateral root*
abstract

Nitrogen (N) form and availability shape the root phenotype by altering the root transcriptome and modulating root system architecture. However, it remains unclear whether these N-responsive architectural traits and their underlying regulatory networks are conserved or divergent between closely related grass species. Here, we compared root architectural and transcriptomic responses to nitrate and ammonium supply in Brachypodium distachyon and eight barley (Hordeum vulgare) genotypes under N starvation, optimal N supply, and high-N conditions. High-resolution root phenotyping revealed contrasting species-specific responses to N form. Barley generally showed enhanced lateral root elongation under nitrate but reduced total root length and limited lateral root proliferation under ammonium, indicating ammonium sensitivity. In contrast, B. distachyon maintained greater root architectural plasticity under ammonium, producing a highly branched and thinner root system. Cross-species module preservation and gene regulatory network analyses identified conserved hub genes, including FERONIA, peroxidase-like genes, CuAO-like genes, aspartyl proteases, and PEPC1-associated modules, as potential integrators of N signaling and root architectural remodeling in barley and B. distachyon. Taken together, our findings indicate that the two Pooideae species share conserved regulatory components but deploy them within distinct network contexts, leading to divergent root strategies in response to nitrate and ammonium. Ultimately, these results provide candidate genes and regulatory modules for improving root plasticity and N use efficiency in cereals.

6.0 Omics-based characterization of physiological and transcriptomic resilience during cold de-acclimation and re-acclimation in GSK-like kinase mutants.
Ahmar S, Rapacz M, Mahmood T … Pociecha E.
HormonesAbiotic stressGrowthBMC plant biology · 2026-07-22
matched: kinase*, signalling, brassinosteroid*, freezing, leaf, growth
abstract

BackgroundCold acclimation, de-acclimation, and re-acclimation are critical adaptive phases that determine plant survival under fluctuating temperatures. Although the physiological and molecular basis of initial cold acclimation is well studied, the regulatory mechanisms governing the loss and re-acquisition of freezing tolerance remain poorly understood. Brassinosteroid (BR) signalling, mediated by glycogen synthase kinase-like (GSK-like) kinases, modulates both stress tolerance and plant growth; however, its role under fluctuating cold-related temperature conditions has not been explored in detail. This study investigated the physiological and transcriptomic responses of Arabidopsis thaliana wild-type (Col-4) and two BR-signalling mutants carrying T-DNA insertions in GSK-like kinase genes BIL1, (At2g30980); GSK3 (At1g06390) across cold acclimation, de-acclimation and re-acclimation phases.ResultsAll genotypes exhibited reduced leaf water content (~ 55%) and suppressed photosystem II efficiency during cold acclimation, with partial recovery during de-acclimation and re-acclimation. Notably, the genotypes showed divergent roles in re-acclimation responses: Col-4 showed the greatest freezing tolerance (LT₅₀ = -20 °C), whereas BIL1 and GSK3 mutants showed more moderate re-hardening (~-15 °C and - 13 °C, respectively). In contrast, both mutants showed higher post-freeze canopy regrowth (headroom recovery ~ 80%) compared with Col-4 (~ 20%) during de-acclimation, revealing a trade-off between maximal freezing tolerance and vegetative recovery. Chlorophyll fluorescence analysis showed that GSK3 achieved the strongest photosynthetic recovery, while BIL1 displayed persistently impaired electron transport efficiency. Transcriptomic profiling revealed a genotype-specific reprogramming switch: during de-acclimation, GSK3 produced the largest transcriptional response (1,433 differentially expressed genes), whereas during re-acclimation this pattern reversed, with BIL1 exhibiting the most extensive but poorly coordinated reprogramming (1,876 differentially expressed genes). The co-expression network analysis revealed a dense, highly connected re-acclimation module in GSK3, contrasting with a fragmented network architecture in BIL1, which indicates qualitatively different transcriptional coordination capacities between the two mutants.ConclusionsOur findings suggest that GSK-like kinase signalling may contribute to cold resilience, potentially influencing the balance between freezing tolerance, photosynthetic recovery, and transcriptional coordination under fluctuating cold temperature conditions. The BIL1 mutant enhances post-freeze regrowth but compromises re-hardening and coherent transcriptional reprogramming, whereas GSK3 supports stronger photosynthetic and transcriptomic recovery. These findings identify GSK-like kinases as candidate regulators that warrant further functional validation as potential targets for engineering cold resilience in crops exposed to increasingly variable thermal environments.

17.0 Light modulates organ-specific gibberellin-abscisic acid crosstalk during drought response in Arabidopsis thaliana.
Miksteinová T, Szecówka M, Petřík I … Hedden P.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressGrowthPlanta · 2026-10-17
matched: signalling, abscisic acid, ABA, gibberellin*, GA, phytohormone*, hormone*, drought, root, roots, root architecture, leaf
abstract

Main conclusionContinuous blue light enhances drought tolerance in Arabidopsis by modulating ABA and GA pathways, whilst controlled GA catabolism, especially via GA2ox7/8, is important for stomatal regulation and drought adaptation. In response to drought, plants undergo physiological and developmental adjustments to reduce water loss and survive the stress, including reduced shoot growth, altered root architecture, and stomatal closure. These processes are regulated by phytohormones, including gibberellins (GAs), which mediate growth adjustments, and abscisic acid (ABA), which promotes stomatal closure. In addition, light signalling plays a crucial role in plant adaptation to drought. Here, we investigated how progressive water restriction affects hormone levels, gene expression, and leaf temperature in Arabidopsis thaliana shoots and roots under two contrasting light regimes: white light under short-day and continuous low-intensity blue light, with a focus on GA and ABA signalling pathways. The hormonal contribution to the drought response was investigated by analysis of ABA and GA mutants. Plants grown under continuous blue light experienced less-drought stress compared with white light as reflected in growth performance and differential gene expression. Drought increased ABA levels in leaves and roots, with stronger accumulation in roots under both light conditions. By contrast, GA production was decreased in leaves and increased in roots under drought in white light, but with little change in blue light. The change in GA status in leaves was associated with down-regulation of GA-biosynthetic 20-OXIDASE genes and up-regulation of GA-catabolic 2-OXIDASE genes (AtGA2ox6, AtGA2ox9). ABA- and GA-deficient mutants were unaffected by water restriction, whilst GA-overproducers lacking GA2ox genes or overexpressing AtGA20ox1 were hypersensitive to drought, with the C20-GA2ox genes GA2ox7 and GA2ox8 proving particularly critical with compromised stomatal regulation.

11.5 Uncovering Physiological Responses of Idesia polycarpa 'Yuji' Under Alkali Stress via Transcriptomics
Chen C, Liu C, Dang Q … Zhen L.
preprintKinases and signal transductionGrowthTrees and woody plantsResearch Square · 2026-10-21
matched: MAPK*, signal transduction, signaling, IAA, abscisic acid, ABA, GA, hormone*, root, root tip*, leaves, seed*
abstract

Abstract This study aimed to investigate the growth, physiological, and molecular responses of Idesia polycarpa ‘Yuji’ under different intensities of alkali stress, thereby providing a theoretical basis for its wider cultivation and application in saline-alkali areas. Two-year-old I. polycarpa ‘Yuji’ seedlings were used in a pot experiment. A mixed alkali stress solution of NaHCO₃:Na₂CO₃ = 9:1 molar ratio was applied at three concentration gradients: J1, 60 mmol·L − 1 ; J2, 90 mmol·L − 1 ; and J3, 120 mmol·L − 1 . Deionized water was used as the control treatment (CK). Our integrated analysis revealed that I. polycarpa ‘Yuji’ exhibited heightened sensitivity during the later stages of alkali stress, which progressively inhibited growth, damaged the microstructure of leaves and root tips, and severely impaired photosynthetic function, as evidenced by reduced pigment content and decreased net photosynthetic and transpiration rates. Physiological analysis revealed that as stress intensity and duration increased, soluble sugars (SS), soluble proteins (SP), proline (Pro) accumulated significantly, while malondialdehyde (MDA) content rose continuously, indicating severe oxidative damage to the cell membrane system. Peroxidase (POD) and superoxide dismutase (SOD) activities showing distinct activation patterns. Hormonal analysis revealed that under J1 to J2 stress, abscisic acid (ABA) levels fluctuated, while indole-3-acetic acid (IAA), zeatin riboside (ZR), and gibberellic acid-3 (GA₃) levels increased, whereas under J3 stress they were suppressed. A total of 9,995 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) were identified in the transcriptome sequencing of the control group (CK) and the J3 group. KEGG enrichment analysis revealed that DEGs were significantly enriched in pathways such as photosynthesis-antenna proteins. Transcriptome analysis identified 9 995 DEGs. KEGG enrichment analysis indicated that photosynthesis-antenna proteins, photosynthesis, and the plant MAPK signaling pathway played key roles in the response to alkali stress. In addition, hormone signal transduction-related genes, including ARR , ABF , PYR / PYL , and AHP , were significantly DEGs. This study clarified the physiological and molecular mechanisms underlying alkali tolerance in I. polycarpa ‘Yuji’ and identified candidate genes and key metabolic pathways associated with alkali stress resistance. These findings provide a theoretical foundation and genetic resources for stress-resistance improvement, efficient utilization of saline-alkali land, and the large-scale cultivation of this tree species.

11.5 Valorization of Agri-Food Industry Waste as a Source of Phytohormones and Evaluation of Its Biostimulatory Potential in Oat (Avena sativa L.) Cultivation.
Sienkiewicz A, Kowczyk-Sadowy M, Obidziński S … Kulus I.
Abiotic stressGrowthMaterials (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-27
matched: auxin*, abscisic acid, gibberellin*, cytokinin*, salicylic acid, brassinosteroid*, phytohormone*, hormone*, nutrient*, roots, leaf, leaves
abstract

The valorization of agri-food industry waste is an important approach to obtaining value-added materials for agricultural applications while supporting the principles of a circular economy. Such waste may contain valuable nutrients and biologically active compounds, including plant hormones. This study aimed to evaluate the phytohormone content of post-production residues from the agri-food industry and to assess the biostimulatory potential of the material with the highest total phytohormone concentration that was also available in sufficient quantities as a post-production residue in oat (Avena sativa L.) cultivation. Phytohormones were determined in 37 samples using liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC-MS) analysis. A total of 35 compounds representing six groups of plant hormones were identified: auxins, cytokinins, gibberellins, brassinosteroids, abscisic acid, and salicylic acid. Five samples-apple pomace, senna leaves, sage leaves, chicory roots, and tobacco stalks-contained all 35 compounds, whereas coffee grounds contained only eight. Brassinosteroids and auxins were the predominant hormone groups. Lemon balm leaves exhibited the highest total phytohormone concentration (511.26 ng·g-1 dry weight), while coffee grounds had the lowest (0.05 ng·g-1 dry weight). Dried and ground lemon balm leaves were therefore selected for a pot experiment conducted under controlled phytotron conditions. High proportions of lemon balm leaf waste reduced shoot growth, biomass accumulation, relative chlorophyll content, and nitrogen balance index (NBI), while increasing flavonoid and anthocyanin accumulation. In contrast, lower amendment promoted biomass production and maintained physiological and photosynthetic performance at levels comparable to or higher than those of the control plants. Most chlorophyll a fluorescence parameters remained relatively stable, although PIABS decreased at the highest amendment proportions. These findings indicate that lemon balm tea-processing waste may represent a potential source of biologically active compounds for agricultural applications. Its use at appropriate proportions may contribute to the valorization of plant-derived waste while supporting sustainable crop production and the principles of a circular economy.

11.0 Symbiosis with Penicillium sp. D7 rewires molecular modules to boost plant growth and As(III) stress tolerance.
Rodríguez-Dobreva E, Patel MK, El Wahabi O … Pollmann S.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-14
matched: signalling, auxin*, abscisic acid, ABA, hormone*, symbio*, endophyt*, vacuol*, root, roots, shoot*, growth
abstract

Arsenic (As) contamination severely compromises plant growth and agricultural productivity, particularly in environments affected by mining or industrial activities. Microbial symbionts adapted to metalliferous soils represent a promising yet underexploited resource for enhancing plant tolerance to As toxicity. In this study, we isolated root-associated fungal endophytes from three plant species thriving on As-rich tailings of the Mónica mine (Bustaviejo, Spain) and identified Penicillium sp. strain D7 as a highly As-tolerant symbiont with pronounced plant growth-promoting effects. When co-cultivated with Arabidopsis thaliana, D7 significantly enhanced shoot biomass under As(III) stress by five-fold and mitigated characteristic toxicity symptoms. Transcriptome profiling revealed that D7 reprograms host hormone signalling in a tissue-specific manner: the fungus suppresses abscisic acid (ABA) biosynthesis and ABA-responsive stress pathways in roots, while simultaneously inducing auxin biosynthesis and signalling in shoots, consistent with improved growth performance. Furthermore, D7 elicited the coordinated induction of key components of the plant's intrinsic As detoxification machinery, including ARQ1, PCS1|CAD1, and the vacuolar transporters ABCC1 and ABCC2. Functional analyses using the cad1-3 mutant and the abcc1 abcc2 double mutant demonstrated that vacuolar sequestration of As(III) in roots is essential for D7-mediated stress tolerance, indicating that fungal colonization enhances the host's capacity for intracellular detoxification rather than reducing As uptake. Together, these results demonstrate that Penicillium sp. D7 reinforces plant resilience to As(III) by simultaneously attenuating stress perception, promoting growth-related hormonal programs, and stimulating vacuolar sequestration of As. This study highlights D7 as a promising microbial tool for supporting vegetation establishment and phytostabilization efforts in As-contaminated environments.

11.0 Transcriptome analysis and hormone metabolic profiling provide molecular insights into flowering process in petunia.
Chen Y, Gao X, Chen J … Zhang W.
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthBMC plant biology · 2026-07-25
matched: signal transduction, signaling, auxin*, abscisic acid, gibberellin*, cytokinin*, jasmon*, salicylic acid, phytohormone*, hormone*, cell wall*, flowering
abstract

BackgroundThe flowering process is a complex biological event regulated by coordinated transcriptional programs and hormone signaling. Petunia hybrida is an important ornamental plant, yet the integrative regulatory mechanisms between hormones and gene expression during flowering remain poorly understood. In this study, flowers of the petunia cultivar '112' were classified into three developmental stages based on days before anthesis: 7 days before flowering (DBF7), 4 days before flowering (DBF4), and the day of flowering (F). Integrated transcriptomic and hormone profiling analyses were conducted to systematically investigate gene expression dynamics and hormone metabolism during the flowering process.ResultsHigh-quality RNA sequencing generated over 483.36 million clean reads, with mapping rates exceeding 83.9% against the petunia reference genome. Comparative transcriptome analysis identified 7,160, 8,687, and 11,161 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) in DBF4 vs. DBF7, F vs. DBF4, and F vs. DBF7, respectively. K-means clustering divided DEGs into four expression modules associated with primary metabolism, secondary metabolite biosynthesis, hormone signal transduction, and floral organ morphogenesis. Transcription factor analysis revealed that bHLH, bZIP, MADS, TCP, and WRKY families exhibited distinct stage-specific expression patterns during the flowering process. Hormone profiling revealed dynamic changes in auxin, gibberellin, jasmonate, salicylic acid, and abscisic acid contents. Integrated transcriptomic and hormone analyses showed that auxin, gibberellin, and jasmonate biosynthesis pathways were predominantly active during early developmental stages, in accordance with the expression dynamics of major biosynthetic genes (PhYUC, PhTAR, PhGA20ox, PhGA3ox, PhLOX, PhAOS, and PhOPR3). Gene-hormone network analysis revealed interconnections among phytohormone signaling pathways, particularly between jasmonate-gibberellin and abscisic acid-cytokinin, which are associated with representative genes involved in jasmonate-related lipid metabolism (PhPLA2A and PhOPR11), phenylpropanoid metabolism (PhPAL1 and PhCAMT3), and cell wall modification (PhPGLR4 and PhGUN25) during the flowering process.ConclusionsThis study provides a comprehensive overview of the transcriptional and hormonal regulatory networks governing the flowering process in petunia. Our results reveal a stage-specific regulatory framework in which coordinated gene expression and phytohormone interactions contribute to flower development and opening, providing potential genetic resources for the improvement of ornamental traits in petunia.

10.5 Integrative molecular mechanisms of lodging resistance in rice (Oryza sativa L.) culms: from DELLA-OsGSK2-BZR1 signaling to cell wall engineering and QTL-based breeding.
Sherif A, Tang C, Dong C … Yang Y.
reviewKinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-02
matched: signaling, auxin*, gibberellin*, ethylene, jasmon*, brassinosteroid*, strigolactone*, phytohormone*, cell wall*, stem, development, lignin*
abstract

Rice lodging, permanent culm displacement from the vertical position, reduces grain yield and quality, potentially causing up to ~80% yield loss. Lodging resistance is a multifactorial trait shaped by stem morphology, internal anatomy, and cell wall chemistry. A central unresolved question in the field concerns the relative contribution of lignin versus cellulose to culm mechanical strength, a debate that has significant implications for targeted breeding and cell wall engineering. This review offers a comprehensive integration of two key questions: (1) What are the current molecular regulatory mechanisms underlying lodging resistance-associated culm traits in rice? (2) How can these mechanisms be translated into effective breeding strategies?. How can these mechanisms be translated into effective breeding strategies? Unlike earlier reviews that focused on individual mechanisms, this synthesis integrates hormonal crosstalk, hierarchical NAC-MYB cascades, emerging evidence for epigenetic regulation, and QTL-based breeding into a unified conceptual framework centered on the DELLA-OsGSK2-BZR1 module. Our synthesis reveals that plant architecture is governed by extensive phytohormone crosstalk centered on the proposed DELLA-OsGSK2-BZR1 module, where gibberellin and brassinosteroid synergistically modulate elongation and thickening, whereas auxin, strigolactone, ethylene, and jasmonic acid fine-tune plant height and tillering. Major-effect loci, such as SCM2/APO1 and SCM3/OsTB1, enable the concurrent improvement of strength and yield, with QTL pyramiding reported to increase culm diameter by up to ~39% in specific genetic backgrounds. Vascular bundle development and cell wall biosynthesis (lignin, cellulose, and hemicellulose) are coordinated by hierarchical NAC-MYB transcriptional cascades, with recent advances suggesting epigenetic regulation through DNA methylation, histone modification, and DELLA-mediated chromatin silencing in rice. We further discuss how these mechanistic insights may guide modern breeding through marker-assisted selection, genome selection, CRISPR/Cas9-mediated precision engineering, and ideotype design, providing a roadmap for developing next-generation lodging-resistant rice varieties.

Megha D, Rajalakshmi S, Vignesh S, Manickavasagam M.

Platinum nanoparticles (PtNPs) were evaluated for their role in enhancing in vitro regeneration and phytohormone-related gene expression for the first time in Cicer arietinum. 9.5 Generation and characterization of a barley strigolactone mutant collection: from plant architecture to drought stress response.
Fontana IM, Buchcik W, Kumlehn J … Marzec M.

matched: signalling, abscisic acid, ABA, strigolactone*, drought, stress respon*, shoot*, development
abstract

Strigolactones (SLs) are known to regulate shoot architecture and to be involved in plant responses to environmental stress, whereas their specific contributions to drought adaptation in barley remain incompletely defined. In this study, we analysed transcriptional, hormonal, and physiological responses to water deficit in barley SL mutants affected in early biosynthesis (Hvd10 and Hvd17), late biosynthesis (Hvmax1a), or signalling (Hvd14). The Hvd10, Hvd17, and Hvd14 mutants exhibited the typical high-tillering phenotype of SL deficiency, whereas Hvmax1a displayed characteristics similar to the wild type (WT), indicating functional differences within the SL biosynthetic pathway. Notably, Hvmax1a resembled the mutant-like metabolic profile, despite maintaining a wild-type-like architecture. Furthermore, the mutants generated in this study using Cas9-mediated genome editing represent a valuable genetic collection for future research into SL-mediated development and stress responses in barley.

9.0 Nuclear NPR1 Modulates Salicylic Acid/Jasmonic Acid Crosstalk via Transcriptional Control of WRKY Transcription Factors
Aerts, N., Caarls … S. C. M.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesbioRxiv · 2026-10-21
matched: ethylene, jasmon*, JA, salicylic acid, hormone*, defense, nucleus, nuclear
abstract

ABSTRACT The plant hormones salicylic acid (SA) and jasmonic acid (JA) are major regulators of plant defense. They can antagonize each other’s activity, enabling an optimized defense response. The SA master regulator NPR1 is a key player in this SA/JA crosstalk, although the mechanisms of NPR1-mediated SA/JA crosstalk remain only partly understood, especially regarding the ethylene-coregulated ERF branch of the JA pathway. Here, we investigated the function of NPR1 in SA/JA crosstalk using several Arabidopsis npr1 mutant variants. Assays using the NPR1-HBD and npr1-nls lines, expressing only cytosolic NPR1, revealed that NPR1 has a nuclear role in SA/JA crosstalk. RNA-seq analysis of lines expressing NPR1 mutated in residues Cys 82 or Cys 216 , in which repression of the JA pathway was impaired while SA pathway induction remained mostly intact, identified 32 SA-responsive genes with reduced expression compared to Col-0, including four WRKY transcription factor genes. Of these, WRKY53 and WRKY70 could suppress expression of the JA marker gene PDF1.2 induced by ORA59, the master regulator of the ERF branch of the JA pathway, in a protoplast system. Additional SA-inducible WRKYs (WRKY50, WRKY54, WRKY63, and WRKY67) similarly suppressed ORA59-induced PDF1.2 expression, implicating WRKY63 and WRKY67 for the first time in SA/JA crosstalk and confirming earlier reports on the role of the other WRKY TFs. WRKY53 and WRKY67 were further shown to reduce accumulation of the ORA59 protein in protoplasts. Thus, our study indicated that NPR1 can regulate SA/JA crosstalk in the nucleus via transcriptional activation of genes encoding specific JA-repressing WRKY transcription factors. MAIN CONCLUSION NPR1 has a nuclear function in regulating salicylic acid/jasmonic acid crosstalk by activating specific WRKY transcription factors that suppress the jasmonic acid pathway, likely in part by reducing ORA59 accumulation.

abstract

Inappropriate fertilization has led to adverse environmental effects and food-safety issues. Biostimulants have been widely used to improve the growth, yield, and quality of crops. 8.5 Methyl jasmonate reduces the damage caused by low temperature combined with low light: By regulating photosynthetic capacity and hormone levels of pepper seedlings (Capsicum annuum L.).

abstract

Low temperature combined with low light (LL) stress is a major issue restricting the yield and economic benefits of protected vegetables. As a typical thermophilic vegetable, the growth and yield of Peppers (Capsicum annuum L.) are easily limited by LL conditions. Methyl jasmonate (MJ), a signal molecule, plays a vital role in plant stress responses. This study is expected to improve pepper yield and quality in facility cultivation and provide insights for pepper resistance breeding.

8.5 Multi-Omics Analysis Uncovers the Salicylic Acid and Ethylene-Responsive Transcription Factor-Mediated Defence Mechanism of Panax notoginseng to Panax notoginseng Virus Y.
Li W, He Z, Qu Y, Liu D.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityMolecular plant pathology · 2026-10-01
matched: reactive oxygen species, signal transduction, signalling, ethylene, salicylic acid, defence
abstract

Panax notoginseng is an edible traditional medicinal plant, but it is highly susceptible to Panax notoginseng virus Y (PnVY). This study revealed that salicylic acid (SA) treatment decreases this susceptibility. The underlying molecular mechanism was elucidated by integrating physiological, multi-omics and genetic approaches. Transcriptional regulatory network analysis revealed that the ethylene-responsive transcription factor PnERF120 may positively regulate the biosynthesis of phenolic acids including SA. Overexpressing PnERF120 enhanced the resistance of P. notoginseng to PnVY, activated SA biosynthesis and signal transduction pathway, upregulated the RNA-silencing-related genes and improved phenolic acid accumulation. However, silencing PnERF120 in P. notoginseng had the opposite effect. Additionally, yeast one-hybrid and dual-luciferase assays indicated that PnERF120 binds to the promoter of PnPAL1 and activates PnPAL1 expression. In conclusion, PnERF120 positively regulates defence against PnVY infection by modulating SA biosynthesis and the SA signalling pathway.

8.0 Genome-Wide Identification and Expression Profiling of the GATA Gene Family Reveals Candidate Genes Associated with GA3-Induced Shoot Elongation in Citrus grandis 'Wanbai'.
Ma C, Xu Y, Han J … Guo L.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressGrowthInternational journal of molecular sciences · 2026-09-24
matched: signal transduction, signalling, gibberellin*, GA3, phytohormone*, stress respon*, leaves, stems, shoot*, seed*, growth, development
abstract

Plant GATA transcription factors are zinc-finger proteins involved in signal transduction, growth and development. However, their potential involvement in plant height and gibberellin-associated shoot growth in pummelo insufficiently characterised. In this study, members of the CgGATA gene family were identified genome-wide and characterised through analyses of physicochemical properties, chromosomal distribution, phylogenetic relationships, gene structures, conserved motifs, collinearity and predicted cis-acting elements in putative upstream regulatory regions. 8.0 Small but big player: the important role of microRNAs in rice.
Zhou S, Wu T, Wang S … Hu B.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-05
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, auxin*, abscisic acid, gibberellin*, phytohormone*, hormone*, growth, development
abstract

Rice is a staple food for more than half of the global population. Research on rice crop establishment and tolerance to biotic and abiotic stresses helps enhance rice productivity and safeguard food security. MicroRNAs (miRNAs) regulate diverse biological processes in rice, including growth, development, and stress resilience by modulating different target genes. They coordinate rice growth and development primarily through phytohormone signaling (abscisic acid, auxin, and gibberellin) and metabolic pathways (cell proliferation and fatty acid metabolism). Furthermore, they regulate rice stress tolerance by modulating hormone signaling pathways and metabolic pathways such as reactive oxygen species (ROS) scavenging. Understanding these regulatory networks is crucial for optimizing rice yield and enhancing stress tolerance. This review synthesizes current understanding of the pivotal roles of miRNAs in regulating rice development and adaptive responses to environmental stimuli. It further discusses the emerging prospects of miRNA applications in rice breeding, laying a conceptual foundation for harnessing miRNA pathways to improve rice productivity and stress resilience, thereby contributing to global food security and agricultural sustainability.

7.5 Chicken-manure digestate contains phytohormones with potential biostimulant activity in lettuce.
Qiong Y, Kaijun W, Kuo F … Yanchen L.
Abiotic stressGrowthBioresource technology · 2026-10-24
matched: auxin*, abscisic acid, gibberellin*, cytokinin*, jasmon*, salicylic acid, phytohormone*, nutrient*, seed*, germination
abstract

Liquid digestate from anaerobic digestion is a potential liquid fertilizer, but its high water content complicates storage, transport, and application. Beyond basic nutrients, its profile of biostimulants, particularly phytohormones, remains insufficiently characterized. This study used widely targeted metabolomics and targeted UPLC-MS/MS to compare chicken-manure digestate collected before membrane pretreatment (BK) with the final concentrate produced by an industrial U-NRO system (MC). BK and MC represented one composite industrial sample pair, with three independently prepared analytical replicates per matrix rather than independent process replicates. In total, 1465 metabolites were annotated and 49 phytohormone-related analytes were quantified. MC contained higher measured concentrations of several auxin-, salicylic acid-, jasmonic acid-, gibberellin-, cytokinin-, and melatonin-related compounds, whereas the summed abscisic acid-related pool decreased markedly. Pathway annotation based on the detected metabolites suggested several possible metabolic routes associated with the occurrence of phytohormone-related compounds in the digestate. The co-detection of tryptophan and several indole-related metabolites was consistent with possible tryptophan-associated transformations, while phenylalanine- and salicylic-acid-related metabolites suggested a possible association with phenylalanine- and phenylpropanoid-related pathways. In germination assays, 0.33% MC increased seedling length without reducing germination speed, whereas 3.3% MC was phytotoxic. In the pot experiment, no harvest-stage phenotypic trait differed significantly between the full-fertilizer control and the selected 70% fertilizer plus MC. Overall, the study establishes a molecular inventory of an industrial digestate concentrate and defines a preliminary concentration-dependent plant-response range.

7.5 Integrated biochemical, metabolomic, transcriptomic, and phytohormone analyses reveal cultivar- specific metabolic regulation in Chaenomeles speciosa fruit during harvest
Fang Q, Fang Y, Fang Y … Peng D.
preprintKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-19
matched: signaling, auxin*, abscisic acid, gibberellin*, GA, cytokinin*, ethylene, jasmon*, JA, salicylic acid, phytohormone*, hormone*
abstract

Abstract Background Chaenomeles speciosa is a dual-purpose fruit with high value for both medicinal and food applications. Its fruit quality varies substantially among cultivars and harvest stages, yet the molecular basis underlying metabolic divergence between cultivars during the harvest window remains poorly understood. Results Here, we analyzed cultivars ‘P’ and ‘L’ at 105 and 120 days after full bloom in terms of morphology and chemical composition, integrating transcriptomics, metabolomics, and hormone profiling to investigate the quality variation. A total of 896 metabolites were identified, with significant variety among the cultivars in carbohydrates (stachyose, D-glucose), phenolic acids (caffeic acid and chlorogenic acid) and flavonoids. Phytohormone profiling revealed that during harvesting, auxin, salicylic acid (SA), jasmonic acid (JA), ethylene, abscisic acid, and cytokinin declined, whereas gibberellin (GA) exhibited the opposite trend. Through integrated correlation analysis of differentially accumulated metabolites, differentially expressed genes, and phytohormones, we predicted a potential GA- JA- SA crosstalk network that may coordinate the balance between fruit growth and defense during the harvest period. Notably, the persistent-calyx ‘L’ variety exhibited greater metabolic stability and higher accumulation of functional saccharides, while the deciduous-calyx ‘P’ variety showed higher peak phenolic content but rapid decline in the later stage of the harvest window. Conclusions Our study provides the first integrated multi-omics and phytohormone profiling of cultivar-specific metabolism in Ch. speciosa fruit during harvest and establish a regulatory framework linking hormone signaling to variety-specific metabolic divergence. The findings offer new insights into the molecular mechanism of cultivar-specific fruit development in multi-purpose medicinal plants.

abstract

Exogenous gibberellin (GA) efficiently induces seeded grape parthenocarpy and improves berry quality, with VvGAMYB serving as a key response factor. 7.0 Glucose amendments are associated with structural and hormonal shifts in a fungal-algal-bacterial (FAB) consortium.
Kaszecki E, Emery RJN, Saville BJ.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressGrowthFEMS microbes · 2026-10-07
matched: signalling, gibberellin*, cytokinin*, jasmon*, hormone*, heavy metal*, stress respon*, development, microscop*
abstract

The fungal-algal-bacterial (FAB) consortium composed of Euglena mutabilis, Talaromyces, and Acidiphilium acidophilum is a natural acid-tolerant assemblage whose heavy metal handling may depend on interspecies cooperation rather than a single-organism uptake alone. Here, glucose was used as a simplified laboratory model of labile carbon availability to test whether carbon input reorganizes consortium structure and stress responses under CdCl2 exposure. Glucose supplementation promoted fungal hyphal development, more extensive visible bacterial colonization, pronounced flocculation, and higher Euglena cell densities under CdCl2 stress. CdCl2 accumulation relative to Euglena cell abundance increased over time in all CdCl2-treated cultures but did not differ significantly among glucose treatments. Hormone profiling revealed distinct extracellular responses under glucose + CdCl2, including elevated bioactive trans-Zeatin (tZ), gibberellin GA7, methyl-thiolated cytokinins, and jasmonic acid, consistent with coordinated consortium-level signalling under metal stress. Scanning electron microscopy provided visual evidence of the FAB physically restructuring when grown in glucose, while transmission electron microscopy (TEM) identified previously uncharacterized electron-dense intravacuole bodies within E. mutabilis, although their identity could not be determined. Together, these findings show that glucose availability reorganizes structural and biochemical signalling of the FAB consortium and is associated with changes in consortium organization and physiological responses under heavy metal stress. They also highlight coordinated structural and biochemical responses associated with consortium persistence under acidic, metal-rich conditions and identify directions for future mechanistic investigations.

6.5 A HAT7/ATHB13/Brassinosteroid signaling module mediates growth of laticifer cells in Euphorbia lathyris.
Escaray FJ, Antonelli CJ, Balaguer B … Vera P.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesThe Plant cell · 2026-10-17
matched: signaling, brassinosteroid*, immun*, cell wall*, embryo*, growth
abstract

Laticifers, the coenocytic cells responsible for latex production, represent one of the most extreme examples of continuous and polarized growth in plants. Their unique architecture, extending over large distances, requires tight coordination between wall extensibility, cytoplasmic organization, and hormonal control. Here, we characterized the poor in latex 13 (pil13) mutant of Euphorbia lathyris which, similar to the previously described pil1 and pil6 mutants, is defective in laticifer expansion during post-embryonic phases of plant growth. Through comparative transcriptomic analyses of these three pil mutants, we identified a core set of 266 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) that define a transcriptomic signature associated with laticifers. We further identified a regulatory network linking two HD-Zip I transcription factors, HAT7 and ATHB13, with brassinosteroid (BR) signaling in the mediation of laticifer growth. This HAT7/ATHB13-BR module regulates the expression of laticifer DEGs involved in cell wall remodeling. Consequently, we found that laticifer cell walls are highly enriched in low-esterified homogalacturonan (HG) and branched rhamnogalacturonan-I (RG-I) pectins. Whole-mount immunolocalization revealed that laticifer cell walls exhibit a peculiar spatial segregation of pectic polysaccharides. These pectins follow a characteristic complementary, non-overlapping deposition pattern along the laticifer longitudinal axis, presumably conferring both elasticity and mechanical integrity. This modular pectin assembly appears compatible with the high flexibility and mechanical sinuosity required for growing of laticifers through host tissues, while accommodating internal turgor pressure changes derived from latex accumulation. Our findings define the laticifer as a cellular model in which wall remodeling, cell growth, turgor pressure, and hormonal signaling are mechanistically coupled.

6.5 Plant cytokinin ortho-topolin riboside suppresses the proliferation of multiple myeloma cells by promoting β-catenin degradation.
Kim M, Kang W, Oh S.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionTrees and woody plantsBMB reports · 2026-10-21
matched: annexin*, phosphoryl*, signaling, cytokinin*, Populus
abstract

Multiple myeloma involves the aberrant activation of the Wnt/β-catenin signaling pathway, resulting in β-catenin accumulation. Targeting this pathway presents a promising therapeutic approach for multiple myeloma. A cell-based chemical assay revealed that ortho-topolin riboside (oTR), a cytokinin naturally produced by Populus robusta, effectively suppressed the activation of β-catenin response transcription induced by Wnt3aconditioned medium. The mechanism underlying this suppression involved oTR-triggered phosphorylation of β-catenin at Ser33, Ser37, and Thr41, leading to its proteasomal degradation and a reduction in intracellular β-catenin levels. Furthermore, oTR consistently decreased β-catenin levels and inhibited the expression of Axin2, cyclin D1, and c-Myc-genes regulated by β-catenin/T cell factor signaling in multiple myeloma cells. Additionally, oTR inhibited the proliferation of multiple myeloma cells and promoted apoptosis, as evidenced by an increase in Annexin V-FITC-positive cells and elevated caspase-3/7 activity. These findings suggest that oTR exerts its antiproliferative effects by attenuating the Wnt/β-catenin pathway, indicating its potential as a therapeutic agent for preventing and treating multiple myeloma.

6.5 Transcriptome, metabolite profiling and functional genomics provide molecular insights into sweetpotato sprouting.
Patil AB, Vyawahare AB, Barvkar VT … Kondhare KR.

abstract

Short dormancy period of sweetpotato (Ipomoea batatas) storage roots causes significant economic losses. To unravel the molecular mechanism of sweetpotato sprouting, a comparative transcriptome was performed using storage roots incubated under non-sprout (15 °C) and sprout favouring conditions (25 °C). abstract

Gibberellins (GAs) are widely used in grape cultivation to promote berry enlargement and induce seedlessness, but their effects on seed development and the regulatory mechanism remain poorly understood. In this study, exogenous GA3 significantly reduced seed weight in grapevine, indicating impaired seed development. Bouzenita MH, Tahir RA, Fan M … Chibani K.

abstract

Thioredoxins (TRXs) are small central redox proteins involved in stress responses and plant development, but their hormone-dependent regulation remains poorly characterized.
