15.0 High Light Intensity Potentiates Bacillus amyloliquefaciens PMB05-Associated Plant Immunity and Bacterial Soft Rot Control in Arabidopsis thaliana.
Li AT, Tsai YC, Tsai YC … Lin YH.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionGrowthPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-22
matched: reactive oxygen species, signaling, immun*, PTI, PAMP*, pathogen*, elicitor*, defense, leaf, leaves, stoma*, growth
abstract

Bacterial soft rot, caused by Pectobacterium carotovorum subsp. carotovorum, affects numerous vegetable crops. Beneficial microbes like Bacillus amyloliquefaciens PMB05 offer a sustainable alternative to traditional copper- or antibiotic-based controls, which risks the development of pathogen resistance. Bacillus amyloliquefaciens PMB05 enhances disease resistance to soft rot by intensifying PAMP-triggered immunity (PTI). Although our previous research showed that PMB05-intensified PTI correlates with a reduced photosynthetic electron transport rate under high light conditions, it remains unclear whether increasing light intensity can strengthen PMB05-mediated immune signaling to further enhance disease resistance in plant leaves. In this study, Arabidopsis plants were grown under varying light intensities to address this question. Furthermore, expression of the type III elicitor, HrpN protein, was applied to evaluate the impact of PMB05 treatment on immune activation. HrpN acts as a PAMP that can trigger plant defense responses. The results revealed that higher growth light intensity was associated with PMB05-intensified PTI response by increasing reactive oxygen species generation but suppresses callose deposition. In addition, PMB05-facilitated PTI-mediated stomatal closure occurred more rapidly and tightly under high light, thereby restricting pathogen invasion into leaf tissues. Disease assays further confirmed that higher light intensity significantly reduced soft rot severity in A. thaliana, while the combination of high light and PMB05 treatment achieved superior control efficacy. Furthermore, PMB05-associated disease protection under high growth light was not detected in sid2, npr1, and nahG plants, indicating that intact SA-associated functions are required for the detectable protective phenotype under the conditions tested. These findings demonstrate that environmental light management can strategically potentiate immunity-intensifying microbes, establishing a novel paradigm for sustainable disease control through the coordinated manipulation of light conditions and microbial application.

14.5 RXLR effectors of Phytophthora infestans: delivery, host targets and immune suppression.
Saini O, Arora A, Kaur A, Saini S.
reviewKinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesMolecular biology reports · 2026-10-21
matched: signalling, immun*, PTI, ETI, effector*, pathogen*, Phytophthora, defense, chloroplast*, trafficking, vesicle*, autophagy
abstract

Phytophthora infestans, the pathogen responsible for the devastating potato disease known as late blight, poses a significant threat to plant health. A major determinant of its pathogenic success is the deployment of cytoplasmic effector proteins, particularly the RXLR class of effectors, which accumulate within the host cells and manipulate plant immune responses. These effectors function as key virulence factors that interfere with multiple layers of plant defense, including pattern-triggered immunity (PTI) and effector-triggered immunity (ETI). Recent studies have revealed that RXLR effectors target diverse host cellular processes such as transcriptional regulation, post-translational modifications, vesicle trafficking, autophagy, chloroplast-mediated immune signalling, and protein stability. By converging on these critical immune hubs, RXLR effectors reprogram host cellular networks to promote pathogen colonization and disease progression. Advances in genomics, structural biology, and host-interactor screening have significantly expanded our understanding of RXLR effector diversity, their molecular mechanisms, and their interactions with host susceptibility factors. In this review, we synthesize current knowledge on the functional roles of RXLR effectors of P. infestans, highlighting their subcellular localization, host targets, and mechanisms of immune suppression. We also discuss emerging concepts in effector biology and their implications for developing durable resistance strategies against late blight disease.

14.0 Stimulation of growth in Piper betle L. via induced systemic resistance (ISR) under combined pathogenic stress through priming of Jasmonic acid.
Chaurasia SK, Acharya C, Pandey JK.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPhysiology and molecular biology of plants : an international journal of functional plant biology · 2026-09-11
matched: H2O2, signaling, jasmon*, JA, immun*, pathogen*, Xanthomonas, Phytophthora, defense, leaf, leaves, stoma*
abstract

Plant tolerance to biotic stress is significantly regulated by Jasmonic Acid (JA), which orchestrates induced systemic resistance (ISR) through synergistic signaling pathways. This study evaluates the efficacy of exogenous JA priming in conferring resistance to Piper betle L. against a broad spectrum of pathogens, including Xanthomonas campestris, Colletotrichum piperis, Fusarium semitectum, and Phytophthora parasitica. Experimental groups comprised a uninfected-untreated control (N), a pathogen-infected control (P), and a JA-primed infected group (T3). Our findings demonstrate that JA treatment significantly mitigates pathogenic damage by restoring morphological integrity, including leaf turgidity and stomatal architecture, which were severely compromised in the P group. Quantitatively, T3 plants exhibited superior physiological performance, with significantly higher relative water content (RWC), chlorophyll, and carotenoid levels compared to both N and P groups. Biochemical profiling revealed that JA priming suppressed oxidative stress markers, specifically reducing electrolyte leakage, H2O2 accumulation, and malondialdehyde (MDA) content to near-basal levels. Simultaneously, JA significantly enhanced the antioxidant defense system, as evidenced by elevated levels of proline, glutathione, ascorbic acid, and total protein. Transcriptome analysis further elucidated the molecular basis of this resistance, revealing a robust upregulation of ISR-related, growth-regulating, and metabolic genes in T3 leaves. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) confirmed a strong positive correlation between ISR traits and biomass accumulation. Furthermore, GO and KEGG enrichment analyses highlighted the optimization of defense-priming pathways and the strategic downregulation of redundant metabolic genes in JA-primed plants validated by metabolic profiling using GCMS analysis. Collectively, these results establish JA priming as a potent strategy for enhancing broad-spectrum immunity and growth vigor in P. betle.

13.0 Disruption of OsMAPKKK24 activation by the Xanthomonas oryzae effector XopY leads to compromised disease resistance in rice.
Chen L, Chen Y, Xu W … Wang W.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthJournal of integrative plant biology · 2026-10-26
matched: kinase*, MAPK*, phosphoryl*, signaling, receptor*, immun*, effector*, pathogen*, Xanthomonas, leaf
abstract

Mitogen-activated protein (MAP) kinase (MAPK) cascades are highly conserved signaling modules that play crucial roles in plant disease resistance. Emerging evidence shows that MAPK cascades are potential targets of plant pathogen effectors. Bacterial leaf blight (BLB), caused by Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae (Xoo), is a devastating disease in rice. Xoo secretes multiple effectors into host rice plants to suppress immune activation. XopY is a conserved effector across diverse Xoo strains; however, how XopY manipulates disease resistance and what its host targets are in rice remain largely unknown. 12.5 A novel Phytophthora-specific putative lipid-binding protein triggers plant immunity.
Seo YE, Kim S, Kong J … Choi D.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionPlant physiology · 2026-10-23
matched: reactive oxygen species, receptor*, immun*, PTI, pathogen*, Phytophthora, oomycete*, elicitor*, defense
abstract

Plants recognize conserved pathogen-derived molecules to activate pattern-triggered immunity (PTI), yet many secreted proteins from oomycete pathogens remain functionally uncharacterized. Here, we identified PcCF16, a previously uncharacterized secreted protein from Phytophthora capsici, through activity-guided fractionation of the pathogen secretome. PcCF16 induced cell death and PTI-associated gene expression in Nicotiana benthamiana, and purified recombinant PcCF16 triggered reactive oxygen species production across multiple plant species. PcCF16-induced cell death was independent of the co-receptors BAK1 and SOBIR1, while defense gene activation remained SOBIR1-independent but was partially dependent on BAK1. Recombinant PcCF16 pretreatment enhanced resistance to P. capsici, while P. capsici lines constitutively expressing PcCF16 exhibited reduced virulence. Structural analyses revealed similarity between PcCF16 and members of the tubular lipid-binding protein (TULIP) superfamily and predicted internal hydrophobic cavities. Consistent with these features, PcCF16 preferentially associated with phosphatidic acid in vitro. Despite substantial sequence divergence, PcCF16 homologs retained similar predicted protein folds but differed in cell death-inducing activity, with surrounding protein context influencing the activity of the conserved C-terminal region. PcCF16 was preferentially expressed during the necrotrophic phase of infection, suggesting a biological function distinct from that of typical early-stage immune elicitors. Together, these findings identify PcCF16 as a Phytophthora-specific secreted immune elicitor with potential lipid-associated functions and reveal an unusual link between late-stage pathogen biology and plant immune activation.

12.0 Arabidopsis NLR Immune Receptors: Structural Diversification, Signaling Networks, and Emerging Principles of Immune Activation.
Huang J.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-30
matched: Ca2+, signaling, receptor*, immun*, ETI, NLR*, effector*, pathogen*
abstract

Plant intracellular nucleotide-binding leucine-rich repeat receptors (NLRs) are central to effector-triggered immunity (ETI), but the rapid expansion of structural data has outpaced an integrated explanation of how receptor architecture, oligomerization, and network context determine immune output. Recent structural and biochemical studies, particularly in Arabidopsis, have revealed that NLRs operate as dynamic signaling assemblies rather than simple pathogen-recognition switches. Here, we synthesize recent evidence across coiled-coil NLRs (CNLs), Toll/interleukin-1 receptor NLRs (TNLs), and RPW8-like helper NLRs (RNLs), with emphasis on mechanisms that are broadly conserved and those that remain receptor- or pathway-specific. ZAR1 (HopZ-Activated Resistance1) and selected helper RNLs form membrane-associated Ca2+-permeable channels, whereas TNLs use Toll/interleukin-1 receptor (TIR)-domain enzymatic activity to generate signaling molecules that are decoded through branch-specific modules formed by ENHANCED DISEASE SUSCEPTIBILITY1 (EDS1)-PHYTOALEXIN DEFICIENT4 (PAD4)-ACTIVATED DISEASE RESISTANCE1 (ADR1) or EDS1-SENESCENCE-ASSOCIATED GENE101 (SAG101)-N REQUIREMENT GENE1 (NRG1). Paired NLRs partition recognition and signaling, while recent work on ADR1-L1 indicates that higher-order spatial organization can further tune immune execution. We also distinguish structurally resolved complexes from inferred higher-order assemblies, examine crosstalk with cell-surface immunity and transcriptional regulation, and assess NLR-engineering strategies according to their experimental validation and potential fitness costs. Together, the evidence supports a modular view in which oligomerization is a recurrent activation principle, but biochemical output, partner dependence, and membrane execution are not interchangeable across NLR classes.

abstract

Pantoea stewartii subsp. indologenes is an emerging cereal phytopathogen with unclear virulence mechanisms. HR3-48 harbors a conserved plant-pathogenic PSI-1 type III secretion system (T3SS) and a single flagellar locus (flag-1) with multiple fliC genes. 11.0 Pathogenesis versus symbiosis: a dual perspective on plant-microbe interactions.
Dong Y, Shamsy R, Ahammed GJ.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant cell reports · 2026-10-22
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, receptor*, phytohormone*, immun*, pathogen*, symbio*, microbiome*
abstract

Plants function as integral components of soil ecosystems, embedded within diverse microbial consortia that engage in continuous chemical and informational exchange. This molecular dialog governs how plants distinguish harmful, neutral, and beneficial microbes, transforming the rhizosphere into a dynamic signaling interface. Microbial interactions operate along a chemical continuum in which metabolites, volatiles, peptides, and other small molecules act across aqueous, lipidic, and chemically heterogeneous microenvironments to shape recognition and response. The physicochemical organization of rhizosphere metabolites may influence their diffusion, stability, and accessibility, although direct evidence for these effects remains limited. Within this framework, plant recognition is reinterpreted as a fluid, context-dependent communication process rather than a binary separation of immunity and symbiosis. This review synthesizes recent advances in the molecular architecture of this continuum, from receptor-mediated signaling to metabolite-driven ecological filtering, and examines how these overlapping systems determine host specificity, community stability, and adaptive resilience. Special attention is given to the regulatory roles of reactive oxygen species (ROS), phytohormones, and redox metabolites as both stress indicators and inter-organismal communicators. By integrating biochemical, ecological, and systems perspectives, this review highlights implications for developing microbiome-based, climate-smart, and sustainable agricultural practices.

10.5 Nodulation Signaling Pathway 2: A Proposed Multifunctional Signaling Hub for Coordinating Plant Architecture, Rhizosphere Chemistry, and Symbiotic Associations Under Nutrient Stress.
Wang Y, Zhao Y, Liu R … Li Y.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressBiology · 2026-09-22
matched: signaling, gibberellin*, strigolactone*, karrikin*, rhizobi*, mycorrhiz*, symbio*, nutrient*, root, shoot*, growth
abstract

Under nutrient stress, plants adjust shoot branching, root remodeling, and symbiotic associations to optimize survival and growth. The GRAS-type transcription factor NODULATION SIGNALING PATHWAY 2 (NSP2) participates in these responses through context-dependent interactions with partners: NSP1, MYB40, and SLR1, and is indirectly repressed by SMAX1 via NSP1 and SLR1. NSP2 functions differ among species: it directly binds DNA in rice (Oryza sativa) strigolactone (SL) biosynthesis, whereas in M. truncatula it acts as a co-regulator in nodulation and flavonoid regulation, with NSP1 or MYB40 providing DNA-binding activity; the NSP2-MYB module also appears conserved in barley (Hordeum vulgare). Through these partnerships, NSP2 is proposed to coordinate three interconnected modules influencing plant architecture, rhizosphere chemistry, and symbiotic associations. The NSP1-NSP2 heterodimer activates SL biosynthesis under phosphate deficiency, remodeling root/shoot architecture and balancing N/P uptake in rice. The NSP1-NSP2-SLR1-SMAX1 quaternary module integrates phosphate, gibberellin, and karrikin signals to orchestrate arbuscular mycorrhizal symbiosis in rice. Under nitrogen limitation and rhizobial infection, the NSP2-MYB40 module enhances flavonoid biosynthesis to promote rhizobial nodulation in Medicago truncatula. These findings suggest that the NSP2-centered network could be targeted to improve nutrient-use efficiency, although further field validation is required.

abstract

Germination of urediniospores marks the onset of infection by Neophysopella tropicalis, the causal agent of Asian grapevine leaf rust, yet the molecular mechanisms enabling this obligate biotrophic fungus to establish infection remain largely unknown. We combined transcriptomics, histological analyses, and functional assays to identify and characterize candidate effector proteins deployed during the earliest stages of pathogenesis. Together, these findings provide the first comprehensive characterization of the N. tropicalis effector repertoire, demonstrating that early infection involves coordinated, sequential deployment of evolutionarily diverse effectors associated with plant immune suppression. This work establishes an integrated framework for discovery and functional prioritization of rust fungus effectors and provides a valuable molecular resource for future studies on grapevine-rust interactions.

matched: signaling, immun*, effector*, pathogen*, symbio*, microbiota, defense, leaf, germination, willow*
abstract

The increasing frequency and intensity of heatwaves-extreme thermal events characterized by prolonged supra-optimal temperatures-exert profound impacts on global ecosystem stability and species interactions. While the disruptive effects of thermal stress on host physiology and gut microbiota are well documented, the role of cuticular microbiota in host defense under heatwaves remains poorly characterized. Here, we show that the cuticular microbiota of the willow leaf beetle Plagiodera versicolora may serve as a thermostable protective barrier against the entomopathogenic fungus Beauveria bassiana. Using microbiota-manipulated larval models, we found that cuticular microbiota significantly bolstered host survival under both control (27°C) and simulated heatwave (32°C) regimes. Recolonization experiments confirmed that cuticle-isolated Enterobacter gergoviae and Pantoea anthophila are sufficient to reduce the increased fungal susceptibility of germ-free larvae. These findings highlight the resilience of cuticular defensive symbioses in the face of climate change and provide new insights into the adaptive mechanisms that sustain host-microbe-pathogen dynamics in a warming world.

9.5 Development of Approaches and Identifying Limitations toward Functional Yeast Surface Display of FLS2.
Dolgikh B, Schulte S, Woldring DR.
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthACS synthetic biology · 2026-09-01
matched: receptor*, immun*, flg22, pattern recognition receptor*, pathogen*, ER, trafficking, development
abstract

Pattern recognition receptors such as FLAGELLIN SENSING 2 (FLS2) are central to plant immunity and attractive targets for engineering broader detection of bacterial phytopathogens for application in pest management (and diagnostics) in food crops and sustainable agriculture practices. However, evaluating numerous FLS2 variants for altered pathogen sensing specificity directly in plants is slow and has low throughput and has been seldom optimized for heterologous display systems. Here, we established conditions that enabled Arabidopsis thaliana FLS2 ectodomain expression on the surface of Saccharomyces cerevisiae and evaluated binding to its cognate ligand, flg22. We show how yeast high-mannose glycosylation of the FLS2 ectodomain contributes to inefficient folding and loss of detectable flg22 binding in standard yeast surface display conditions. Substitutions at all N-glycosylation motifs compromised surface expression, indicating that some glycosylation is required for trafficking. We tuned the extent of glycosylation using tunicamycin, an N-linked glycosylation inhibitor, in combination with thermal stress to modulate ER quality control. Under these conditions, we observed a reproducible subpopulation of cells with improved flg22 binding despite reduced overall expression, and we confirmed flg22 selectivity against non-FLS2 proteins using both flow cytometry and magnetic bead-based enrichment. Guided by structural modeling of high-mannose glycans on the FLS2 ectodomain, we then substituted asparagines at selected N-glycan sites with serine. We identified a key glycan-site variant, N388S, which lies proximal to the flg22 binding interface and increased the binding population size under stress conditions. Binding assays against FLS2 variants and a reported non-binding variant affirmed that FLS2 selectivity was specific to FLS2 display, showing that all variants maintained a low affinity interaction with flg22. Together, these results point to FLS2 display conditions, not only glycosylation state, as an underlying limitation to detect true flg22 interactions, which will require more sensitive approaches to confidently resolve true binding populations.

Qi, R., Glavincheska … S.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-23

abstract

Plants recruit beneficial microbiota in their rhizosphere to alleviate stresses. In Arabidopsis thaliana, foliar infection by Hyaloperonospora arabidopsidis (Hpa) has been shown to establish a disease-suppressive microbiome that promotes protection against Hpa infection of the subsequent plant generation grown in this conditioned soil - a phenomenon known as soil-borne legacy (SBL). The root responses involved in SBL establishment have remained largely unclear. In this study, time-series RNA-seq of roots from Hpa-infected plants grown in natural soil was conducted over seven days post-inoculation. 8.0 Deep Learning and Computational Modeling for Epitope-Focused Immunogen Design Targeting Monkeypox
Selvaraj G, Kaliamurthi S, Peslherbe GH, Wei D.
preprintTrees and woody plantsMethodsResearch Square · 2026-10-23
matched: immun*, pathogen*, tree, trees, deep learning, machine learning
abstract

Abstract Monkeypox (MPX), now widely called mpox, is a zoonotic orthopoxvirus infection that has moved from a neglected tropical disease to a global public health concern. Live-attenuated smallpox vaccines offer cross-protection, but they are produced in limited quantities, are unevenly distributed, and are not suited to every recipient. These constraints motivate the search for rationally designed, epitope-focused immunogens that can be produced at scale and tailored to emerging viral variants. This article describes an in silico framework that combines amino acid feature engineering, classical machine learning (ML) benchmarking, and a deep neural network (DNN) to predict potential T-cell and B-cell epitopes from the MPX virus proteome (VP). Epitope and non-epitope sequences, together with antigen information, were collected from public immunology and virology databases, encoded numerically, and described by a panel of 84 amino acid features. Hierarchical heat-map clustering based on Pearson correlation identified 44 of the 84 features as redundant, and these were removed before modeling. Twenty-eight ML algorithms were then screened; the five best performers, namely Random Forest, Label Spreading, Decision Tree, Label Propagation and Extra Trees, were compared using accuracy, F1 score and the Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC), the last two being appropriate for imbalanced data. A DNN with fully connected dense layers, trained by back-propagation with the Adam optimizer, was evaluated with two and four hidden layers. The four-layer network converged faster in terms of loss, while accuracy did not differ significantly between architectures across training epochs. According to the held-out test data, the DNN outperformed the traditional ML models and other deep learning approaches. Because the framework is not specific to one pathogen, it can in principle be retrained to support vaccine design against other emerging infectious diseases. We discuss the biological rationale, methodological choices, limitations, and the experimental validation steps required to move from computational candidates to a multivalent MPX immunogen.

8.0 MycorrhizaFinder: a user-friendly machine-learning tool to quantify endomycorrhizal colonisation of nursery and field roots.
Kowal J, Buckley S, Upham R … Suz LM.
GrowthMethodsMycorrhiza · 2026-10-23
matched: mycorrhiz*, endophyt*, root, roots, development, microscop*, machine learning
abstract

Root colonisation by endomycorrhizal fungi has long been a widely used metric in mycorrhizal studies. However, due to the significant time required to assess colonisation using traditional microscopy techniques, studies of colonisation at large scales are impractical. Machine-learning-based approaches may increase output and facilitate ecosystem assessments. We trained a machine-learning-based tool (MycorrhizaFinder) on 42 field roots (132 unique 1 cm sections, with > 100,000 tiles) and validated on a further 37 sections from grasslands and heathlands hosting common Northern European plants with arbuscular (AM) and ericoid mycorrhizal (ErM) fungal structures, and dark septate endophytes (DSE), also common in field-sourced roots. We incorporated a user-customized confidence threshold to encourage engagement with morphological ambiguities, in e.g. ErM and DSE. A Macro F1 statistic was used to assess the tool's development performance. We provide a sample workflow from root processing and microscope slide scanning to semi-automated model training and performance evaluation. Our model performance combining all classes, the Macro F1, is conservatively 66% for arbuscular and 57% for ericoid mycorrhizal colonisation assessment. MycorrhizaFinder is user-friendly, requires no programming skills and offers flexibility for advanced agronomists or ecologists to train the tool using their own labelled mycorrhizal root datasets, including images acquired from different instruments or staining protocols. This adaptability allows users to customize the model for specific ecosystems or experimental designs. Leveraged with molecular identification and/or functional traits of fungi (e.g. arbuscules), MycorrhizaFinder supports scalable and repeatable nursery experiments and monitoring across ecosystems to assess mycorrhizal status and track land-use changes over time.

Singh P, Singh RK, Guo DJ … Li YR.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-30
matched: signaling, hormone*, immun*, PTI, ETI, effector*, pathogen*, microbiome*, nutrient*
abstract

The rhizosphere is a living physical space that is constantly evolving and is characterized by a complex interplay between beneficial microbes, pathogens, and plants, mediated by molecular and ecological interactions. These three-way relationships constitute a rhizosphere "triangular warfare system," which controls plant health, nutrient uptake, disease resistance, and plant resilience. This review provides a mechanistic overview of the molecular, metabolic and ecological processes underlying these tripartite interactions. We discuss how plants integrate pattern-triggered immunity (PTI), effector-triggered immunity (ETI), microbiome exudation, and hormone signaling to select beneficial microbes to exclude pathogen entry. Beneficial microbes deploy volatile organic compounds, induction of systemic resistance (ISR), secretion systems, phytotoxins, siderophores, lytic enzymes, and microbiome-disrupting strategies to enhance plant fitness, whereas pathogens use virulence effectors, lytic enzymes, secretion systems, phytotoxins, siderophores, and microbiome-disrupting strategies to overcome host defenses and restructure rhizosphere communities. Recent advances in multi-omics have revealed the intricate signaling networks, metabolic interactions, and microbial competition shaping these relationships under various environmental conditions. We further explore emerging opportunities in microbiome engineering, synthetic microbial consortia and disease-suppressive soil management for sustainable crop protection. This review synthesizes existing mechanistic knowledge and introduces the concept of 'rhizosphere triangular warfare' as a stepping stone toward the next generation of microbiome-based agriculture and precision rhizosphere engineering under environmental changes.

8.0 There are cores, indicators, and differences - a high-throughput and quantitative PCR study of the Ambrosia artemisiifolia L. leaf, root, and seed endophytic bacterial microbiomes within the European invasive range
Grzyb, T., Hrabovsky … J.
preprintGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-24
matched: pathogen*, microbiome*, endophyt*, root, roots, leaf, leaves, seed*
abstract

Ambrosia artemisiifolia L. is among the most harmful invasive plants in Europe, a major agricultural weed and a source of severe allergenic pollen. Endophytes may affect invasion, but this species' endophytic bacteria were profiled by high-throughput sequencing only in a single study from China. We aimed to characterize the leaf, root, and seed endobacteriome and test whether tissue type, habitat, or region shape its composition within the European invasive range. Plants were sampled from six sites in Poland and Slovakia across three habitat types. 16S rRNA gene amplicons from surface-sterilized tissues and soil were sequenced and bacterial load quantified by qPCR. Analyses included alpha and beta diversity, core microbiome, indicator analysis, differential abundance, and functional prediction. Tissue type, not region or habitat, structured communities. Gammaproteobacteria exceeded Alphaproteobacteria in most plant tissue samples, but in soil the reverse was true. Mean bacterial load in plant tissues ranged from 1.73-3.49 × 10 8 16S rRNA gene copies g dry weight -1 . The pan-tissue core (80% prevalence) contained 13 genera, 9 of them absent from the soil core. Seeds had significantly lower alpha diversity, the smallest core and no seed-exclusive predicted functional indicators. Leaves and roots differed significantly in taxonomic composition but not in predicted functions. Tissues shared a core that overlapped only partly with the soil core. Seeds carried a reduced subset of the endobacteriome that, in this annual invader, may pass across generations. Further studies should compare these communities with native-range populations and test the enhanced mutualism and pathogen spillback hypotheses.

7.5 A planthopper-secreted miRNA facilitates viral infection by disrupting plant growth-defense balance in rice.
Zhang B, Jia S, Zhu Y … Qiu L.
HormonesGrowthMolecular plant · 2026-10-22
matched: auxin*, jasmon*, JA, immun*, effector*, pathogen*, defense, growth
abstract

Insect vectors can enhance the pathogenicity of plant arboviruses, yet the molecular mechanisms driving this cross-kingdom manipulation remain elusive. Here, we demonstrate that white-backed planthoppers secrete a specific microRNA, miR-133, into rice to facilitate southern rice black-streaked dwarf virus (SRBSDV) infection. Mechanistically, insect-derived miR-133 functions via cross-kingdom RNA interference to directly repress the GOLDEN2-LIKE transcription factor 31 (OsGLK31). OsGLK31 acts as a dual regulator that promotes growth via OsYUCCA-mediated auxin biosynthesis and confers antiviral immunity through OsLOX11-mediated jasmonic acid (JA) biosynthesis. In the absence of infection, the plant repressor OsJAZ10 binds to OsGLK31 to specifically inhibit its immune function, thereby prioritizing growth. However, insect infestation disrupts this balance: insect-secreted miR-133 represses OsGLK31 expression, while OsJAZ10 blocks the transcriptional activity of residual OsGLK31 protein on OsLOX11. This coordinated blockade suppresses both auxin and JA biosynthesis, causing severe dwarfism and dampened viral resistance. Our findings reveal miR-133 as a cross-kingdom effector that disrupts the host plant growth-defense balance to pave the way for viral invasion, offering potential targets for antiviral crop improvement.

7.5 Pseudomonas avellanae: From Specific Pathogens to a Species Complex.
Scortichini M, Popović Milovanović T, Iličić R.
reviewGrowthTrees and woody plantsMolecular plant pathology · 2026-10-01
matched: effector*, pathogen*, endophyt*, leaf, stoma*, shoot*, woody
abstract

TaxonomyBacteria; Proteobacteria, gamma subdivision; Order Pseudomonadales; Family Pseudomonadaceae; Genus Pseudomonas; Pseudomonas avellanae species complex, which includes different pathogenic lineages previously classified within the P. syringae complex, namely the pathovars avellanae, theae, actinidiae, and morsprunorum race 2.Microbiological propertiesAs a whole, the P. avellanae species complex genome comprises 19.4% conserved genes and 80.6% flexible genome. Mobile genetic elements are present within the complex. Strains associated with bacterial canker of European hazelnut possess two plasmids, whereas multiple plasmids and megaplasmids can occur in the lineages associated with bacterial canker of kiwifruit and cherry. Fourteen type III secretion system effectors are shared by all lineages of the species, and each lineage apparently possesses specific effector proteins. Phytotoxins, such as phaseolotoxin, coronatine, and tabtoxin, can be produced by some lineages of P. avellanae. Siderophores are present in the P. avellanae species complex.Host rangeCorylus avellana, Camellia sinensis var. sinensis, Actinidia chinensis var. chinensis, A. chinensis var. deliciosa, Prunus avium, P. cerasus, P. domestica. A strain of P. avellanae has also been isolated from river epilithon.Disease symptomsPseudomonas avellanae can cause severe symptoms, including bud necrosis (kiwifruit), leaf necrosis and wilting (hazelnut, tea, kiwifruit, cherry/plum), shoot and twig blight (hazelnut, tea, kiwifruit, cherry/plum), flower necrosis (kiwifruit), fruit necrosis (cherry/plum), trunk canker (hazelnut, kiwifruit, cherry/plum), and plant death (hazelnut, tea, kiwifruit).EpidemiologySome P. avellanae lineages are favoured by air temperatures below 0°C. Penetration mainly occurs through stomata, leaf scars, and wounds induced by frost events. P. avellanae species complex exhibits both epiphytic and endophytic phases. During summer, very high temperatures can reduce the multiplication and dispersal of the pathogen. The global circulation of latently infected propagative plant material largely favours the spread of P. avellanae lineages across different cultivation areas.DetectionPathogens belonging to this complex can be detected in both symptomatic and asymptomatic plant tissues (e.g., twigs, buds, and cankers) using various conventional techniques and molecular tools (standard-, duplex-, TaqMan-, real-time PCR, LAMP, MLSA). Depending on the targeted symptoms, pathogenicity tests may be conducted on leaf, leaf scars or woody tissues.Disease controlDisease management is based on spray treatments to the crown using copper compounds applied in spring and autumn, as well as after harvest, during leaf fall, and following hail, frost events, and pruning. Balanced plant nutrition is important to reduce the risk of severe infection. Alternative promising strategies include the application of biocontrol agents, natural products, and bacteriophages.

7.0 A Gnotobiotic Rice Pathosystem Reveals How the Microbiome Suppresses Rice Blast Disease
Johnson, T. R., Kemmerer … J.
preprintCa2+GrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-21
matched: ROS, immun*, chitin, pathogen*, microbiome*, endophyt*, defense, growth
abstract

Rice blast disease caused by the hemibiotrophic fungal pathogen Magnaporthe oryzae poses a major threat to global rice production, creating an urgent need for sustainable disease management strategies. Because plant-associated microbiomes can contribute to pathogen suppression and host defense, their role in rice blast resistance is of considerable interest. However, interactions among M. oryzae , rice, and the rice microbiome remain poorly understood. Using a gnotobiotic rice pathosystem, we investigated how microbiome colonization influences host responses to infection and how bacterial community structure changes following pathogen challenge. Microbiome colonization significantly reduced blast disease severity, while differential abundance analyses identified infection-associated shifts in bacterial community composition, including enrichment of Brevibacillus -associated taxa. Functional characterization of cultured endophytes revealed plant growth-promoting traits and antagonistic activity against M. oryzae . Two synthetic communities assembled from healthy- and infected-derived isolates significantly reduced disease severity, demonstrating that disease suppression can be recapitulated using simplified microbial communities. Interestingly, axenically grown plants exhibited elevated basal expression of multiple defense-associated genes but greater disease severity and reduced chitin-induced ROS production compared with holoxenic plants. In contrast, microbiome-colonized plants displayed enhanced ROS responsiveness and reduced disease, suggesting that the rice microbiome promotes a more effective and regulated immune state rather than simply increasing defense activation. Together, these findings advance our understanding of rice-microbiome- M. oryzae interactions and establish a tractable experimental platform for mechanistic studies of microbiome-mediated disease suppression.

7.0 Colonization, growth-promoting effect, control efficacy against tobacco black shank, and mechanism of action of the tobacco endophyte Microbacterium foliorum 5SY32.
Zheng W, Du H, Wu D … Kang Y.
HormonesCell biology and organellesGrowthFrontiers in microbiology · 2026-10-08

abstract

Tobacco black shank (TBS) severely restricts sustainable tobacco cultivation. Endophytic bacteria serve as promising biocontrol agents due to strong host colonization ability. Microbacterium foliorum 5SY32, isolated from healthy tobacco, inhibits Phytophthora nicotianae growth by over 60% and synthesizes 15.13 μg/mL indole-3-acetic acid. This study assessed its colonization, growth-promoting capability and disease suppression efficacy via greenhouse and field trials, and explored functional mechanisms through transcriptome sequencing. Re-isolation and molecular verification proved that strain 5SY32 stably colonizes tobacco roots, stems, and leaves, with the highest population detected in roots. The strain significantly optimized root morphology and physiological activity, and improved aboveground agronomic traits, leaf chlorophyll and nitrogen content. Field yield was elevated by more than 20%. It achieved 58.58% protective and 30.31% curative efficacy in the greenhouse, and sustained 48.99%-52.31% control efficacy in the field against TBS. 5SY32 boosted antioxidant and defense enzyme activities, and activated key genes related to jasmonic acid, ethylene and abscisic acid pathways. A total of 12,485 differentially expressed genes participating in cell wall formation, oxidative response and secondary metabolism were modulated, ultimately inducing systemic resistance in tobacco. This study first clarifies the integrated mechanism of colonization, growth promotion and disease resistance induction mediated by M. foliorum 5SY32, offering a valuable microbial resource for green management of TBS.

7.0 Culturomics and amplicon-based insights into the endophytic microbiome of Kalmegh (Andrographis paniculata (Burm.f.) Nees) and its implications in plant growth promotion.
Bagul SY, Sarkar R, Suthar MK … Reddy NRR.
GrowthWorld journal of microbiology & biotechnology · 2026-10-16
matched: pathogen*, microbiome*, endophyt*, leaf, leaves, stem, flowering, seed*, germination, growth
abstract

Leaf and stem endophytic microorganisms play an important role in plant physiology and secondary metabolite biosynthesis of plants, particularly in medicinal plants. Kalmegh (Andrographis paniculata (Burm.f.) Nees) is an important medicinal plant with liver-protective, anti-inflammatory, ovicidal, larvicidal, and anti-urolithatic properties. The medicinal activities are due to presence of andrographolides. This study investigated the culture-independent diversity of endophytic bacteria associated with Kalmegh at nursery, vegetative, and flowering stages using a metagenomic approach. The results revealed that Sphingomonas was the dominant bacterial genus during nursery and vegetative stages, whereas Halobacterium predominated during the flowering stage. Microbial abundance was highest in the nursery stage, followed by flowering and vegetative stages. Functional analysis showed that flowering stage endophytes were enriched with specialized carbon metabolism and aromatic compound degradation pathways, potentially influencing andrographolide biosynthesis. Chemical analysis supported these findings, showing the highest andrographolide content in leaves during flowering (3.1%). Gene expression analysis further demonstrated that increased andrographolide accumulation was associated with upregulation of methyl erythritol phosphate pathway (MEP) pathway and diterpene biosynthetic genes. Bacterial endophytes isolated from flowering-stage leaves exhibited plant growth-promotion, antifungal, and seed germination-enhancing activities. In vitro assays against Fusarium wilt and Rhizoctonia disease showed that Bacillus cereus strains KL11, KL12, and KL5 inhibited pathogen growth by 60-65%. Biopriming with strains KL3 and KL5 of Bacillus cereus improved seed germination and plant growth. Pot trials with Micrococcus luteus KES1 increased herbage yield, plant height, and andrographolide content by 43%. These findings highlight the potential of leaf and stem endophytes for sustainable improvement of Kalmegh productivity and andrographolide content in the plant.

6.5 Antibacterial mechanism of hypocrellin against Ralstonia solanacearum in tobacco and its modulation of the endophytic microbiome.
Du W, Sun C, Wang J … Wang C.
Cell biology and organellesMicrobiology spectrum · 2026-10-22
matched: pathogen*, microbiome*, microbiota, endophyt*, defense, cell wall*
abstract

The photosensitive compound hypocrellin, derived from the traditional Chinese medicinal fungus Shiraia bambusicola Henn., shows potential as a novel pesticide. To evaluate its efficacy against bacterial wilt disease caused by Ralstonia solanacearum in tobacco, this study examined its antibacterial mechanisms through in vitro experiments and analyzed their impact on tobacco endophytic microbiota. An optimized hypocrellin offering a promising sustainable formulation-with a 2:3 ratio of hypocrellin A to hypocrellin B-was developed for enhanced antibacterial activity, and its interaction with bacterial DNA, intracellular components, and membrane integrity was systematically investigated. Hypocrellins exhibited strong antibacterial effects by disrupting the cell wall and membrane integrity of R. solanacearum, increasing membrane permeability, and inducing the leakage of intracellular K+, soluble proteins, and reducing sugars. Dual DNA-binding mechanisms were suggested: electrostatic adsorption to phosphate groups, followed by groove insertion, and intercalation between base pairs. In tobacco plants, hypocrellin treatment significantly altered the endophytic microbial community, increasing microbial richness and diversity, increasing the proportion of Bacillota and Pseudomonadota, and significantly reducing pathogen abundance. Predictive functional analysis based on PICRUSt2 suggested that hypocrellin may have influenced metabolic pathways associated with microbial basal metabolic activity and proliferation potential. Hypocrellins mitigate R. solanacearum infection through multi-target mechanisms: physical disruption of bacterial membranes and DNA, restoration of beneficial endophytic microbiota, and activation of metabolic pathways critical for plant defense.IMPORTANCEThis study highlights the multi-target antibacterial mechanisms and microbiome-modulating effects of hypocrellin, offering a promising candidate for a sustainable strategy for controlling soil-borne diseases. Unlike conventional chemical pesticides, hypocrellin acts through physical disruption of bacterial membranes and DNA interaction, while simultaneously restoring beneficial endophytic microbiota and activating metabolic pathways critical for plant defense in tobacco. Its ability to suppress Ralstonia solanacearum and enhance microbial ecological balance positions hypocrellin as both an effective antibacterial agent and a microbiome regulator. These findings provide a scientific basis for developing hypocrellin-based bio-pesticides and support further research into its application in eco-friendly agricultural practices.

6.5 Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungal community abundance, functions, and symbiotic interactions revealed by root metatranscriptomes.
Chen P, Taylor JW, Gao C.
Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesGrowthiMetaOmics · 2026-02-21
matched: mycorrhiz*, symbio*, drought, nutrient*, cytoskelet*, root, development
abstract

Arbuscular mycorrhizal (AM) fungal communities are often profiled by metabarcoding, but this approach can bias abundance estimates and provide limited insight into community function and interactions with the host. Here, using weekly, field-based multi-omics, we find metatranscriptomes and metagenomes increase estimates of AM fungal relative abundance 6-15-fold over rDNA metabarcoding and that root AM fungal relative abundance correlates strongly with sorghum forage yield (R > 0.9) and moderately with grain yield/quality. AM fungal relative abundance is impacted directly by sorghum development and indirectly by drought via suppressed sorghum fatty acid gene expression. Gene co-expression network revealed coordinated plant and AM fungal functions in structural reorganization, resource exchange, and signal communication between the symbiotic partners. We found that early-season co-transcriptions of AM fungal and host genes feature cytoskeletal genes essential for arbuscule formation, the fungal-plant organs of nutrient and mineral exchange. Late-season plants downregulate cytoskeletal genes, but AM fungi continue their upregulation, which is associated with fungal sporulation and putative mating. Our study identifies previously ignored massive underestimation of AM fungal relative abundance, highlights the predictive role of AM fungal relative abundance for crop productivity, and uncovers symbiotic life history dynamics underpinned by coupled and decoupled co-expression of plant and AM fungal genes.

6.5 Mulberry-soybean intercropping reshapes rhizosphere metabolites and microbiota alongside enhanced soybean nodulation.
Liu C, Li J, Zhu K … Huang G.
GrowthTrees and woody plantsPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-17
matched: symbio*, microbiota, root, roots, leaves, stems, shoot*, growth, woody
abstract

Woody perennial-legume intercropping may improve nitrogen-use efficiency, but the belowground links among root-zone configuration, soybean nodulation, rhizosphere chemistry, microbial assembly and nitrogen movement remain unclear. We conducted a greenhouse pot experiment with soybean monoculture (Mono-S), mulberry monoculture (Mono-M), full-contact mulberry-soybean intercropping (Int), and root-separated intercropping with a 30 μm nylon-mesh barrier (Int + B). Physiological and N-related traits were assessed with six pot-level biological replicates, whereas untargeted rhizosphere metabolomics, bacterial full-length 16S rRNA profiling and fungal full-length ITS profiling used three pot-level replicates per treatment-plant component. Relative to Mono-S, Int increased soybean total nodule number by 12.3%, nitrogenase activity by 21.6%, whole-plant N accumulation by 9.1% and shoot dry weight by 26.5%. GmNIN and GmENOD40 transcript abundance was 2.60- and 2.22-fold higher, respectively, in Int-S than in Mono-S. Metabolomics revealed broad, host-specific treatment-associated shifts across multiple chemical classes, while selected putatively annotated soybean isoflavonoids and mulberry flavonoids showed contrasting response patterns. Host-specific PERMANOVA revealed significant differences in bacterial and fungal community composition. 15N label applied to soybean-side soil was recovered in mulberry roots, stems and leaves under both intercropping configurations, indicating recovery of soil-applied 15N label in mulberry. Mean land equivalent ratios exceeded 1 in both treatments and differed significantly. Cross-omics associations were largely treatment-structured, highlighting the need for targeted validation of specific metabolite-microbe relationships. Overall, full root-zone continuity was associated with the strongest coordinated soybean symbiotic and growth responses, while both intercropping configurations produced distinct rhizosphere restructuring. These findings provide a framework for understanding rhizosphere-mediated interactions in woody perennial-legume intercropping systems.

6.5 The Fraxinus mandshurica genome reveals a dual adaptive strategy linking rapidly evolving immune receptors and structural fortification.
Yan J, Yang S, Feng Q … Zeng F.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthTrees and woody plantsForestry research · 2026-08-31
matched: receptor*, immun*, NLR*, defense, xylem, development
abstract

Fraxinus mandshurica exhibits high tolerance to ash dieback and superior wood mechanical properties, yet the genomic basis of these dual traits remains incompletely understood. Here, we present a chromosome-scale genome assembly of the elite clone 'M8' and integrate comparative genomics with temporal transcriptomics to investigate its adaptive architecture. We show that recent tandem and proximal duplications have contributed to the diversification and candidate positive selection of the NLR immune repertoire. Transcriptomic profiling reveals a stage-associated deployment pattern, in which expressed NLR genes are enriched during the Transition Stage, consistent with reinforced immune surveillance during rapid xylem expansion. As development progresses, the Latewood Stage shifts toward phenylpropanoid metabolism, lignification-related programs, and structural reinforcement. We identify and experimentally validate a latewood-recruited FmNAC104-FmPRX1 candidate regulatory module, in which FmNAC104 binds to and activates the FmPRX1 promoter and promotes endogenous FmPRX1 expression. Formal NAC/PRX gene family analysis further supports an enlarged NAC/PRX repertoire relative to P. trichocarpa and clarifies the orthogroup status of the FmNAC104-FmPRX1 module among related ash species. Collectively, our findings support a 'fortified defense' working model in which F. mandshurica temporally coordinates immune-receptor diversification with latewood-associated structural reinforcement. This study provides a genomic resource and candidate targets for breeding programs aimed at improving wood quality and disease resilience in Fraxinus.

6.0 Benzoic Acid Secreted From the Chickpea Root Promotes Persistent Colonisation by Specific Soil Microbes.
Zhou Y, Fuentes D, Merchant A … Denton MD.
HormonesGrowthPlant, cell & environment · 2026-10-21
matched: IAA, microbiome*, microbiota, endophyt*, root, root tip*, growth
abstract

The plant root tip is highly sensitive to environmental cues and exerts sensory, chemical and movement responses. However, the microbial diversification at the root tip, and the nature of root-selection for microbes, are poorly understood. This study investigated root exudation and microbiome colonisation in the chickpea (Cicer arietinum L.) root tip and root base, in diverse soils. For the endosphere microbiome, deterministic effects were more important than stochastic processes in shaping the root tip microbiome, indicated by the stable endophyte composition independent of the soil microbiota background. A genotype with high secretion of benzoic acid (BA), was consistently colonised by 3 core ASV (Amplicon Sequence Variant) from the Burkholderia clade. Two of 98 isolated strains were confirmed as the culturable candidates of the core ASV, both of which were characterised by superior BA tolerance and the ability to utilise BA as a sole carbon source, demonstrated through high expression of BA degradation genes. Additionally, one IAA-producing coloniser specifically promoted the growth of its original host genotype across different soils, but provided no consistent benefits for other genotypes. Our results reveal the mechanisms by which soil microbes interact with root exudates of a host genotype to establish colonisation.

6.0 Context-dependent immunomodulation by Astragalus polysaccharides: Structural determinants, structure-activity relationships, mechanisms, and applications: A review.
Wang M, Li Y, Xu H … Liang Z.
reviewKinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthInternational journal of biological macromolecules · 2026-10-16
matched: kinase*, signaling, receptor*, immun*, microbiota, nuclear, development
abstract

Astragalus polysaccharides (APS), derived mainly from Astragalus membranaceus (Fisch.) Bunge and Astragalus membranaceus var. mongholicus (Bunge) P. K. Hsiao, have attracted considerable attention as plant-derived immunomodulatory macromolecules. Accumulating evidence suggests that APS-mediated immunomodulation is context-dependent rather than unidirectional. Raw-material characteristics, processing methods, extraction procedures, fractionation, and purification can generate APS fractions with different structural characteristics, including molecular weight distribution, monosaccharide composition, glycosidic linkages, branching patterns, chain conformation, solubility, and aggregation behavior. This review summarizes recent progress in APS structural characterization, structure-activity relationships, immunomodulatory effects, mechanisms of action, and immune-related applications. Under immunosuppressive conditions, APS may help restore impaired immune defenses, whereas during immune hyperactivation, APS may suppress excessive inflammatory responses and support immune homeostasis. Mechanistically, APS may modulate innate and adaptive immune responses, gut microbial composition and metabolite production, mucosal barrier function, and gut-organ communication. Toll-like receptor 4-associated nuclear factor-κB and mitogen-activated protein kinase pathways are among the most frequently reported mechanisms, whereas nuclear factor erythroid 2-related factor 2/heme oxygenase-1 signaling, regulation of the NOD-like receptor family pyrin domain-containing 3 inflammasome, interleukin-22/signal transducer and activator of transcription 3 signaling, and microbiota-mediated pathways may contribute in specific settings. In addition, APS-based interventions have been investigated for supportive clinical care, vaccine adjuvants, veterinary use, functional foods, immune-support products, and pharmaceutical or topical formulations. By linking structural variation to immune effects, mechanistic pathways, and application contexts, this review provides a basis for the structural standardization of APS, quality control, activity optimization, and immune-related product development.

6.0 Genomic Signatures of Endemic Pathogen Exposure in a Declining Amphibian.
Wendt AS, Berger L, Skerratt LF, Brannelly LA.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthTrees and woody plantsMolecular ecology · 2026-10-01
matched: signalling, immun*, pathogen*, differentiation, tree, dendro*
abstract

Endemic wildlife diseases can impose strong selection on threatened populations, yet whether persistence is associated with adaptive genomic responses remains poorly understood. We tested whether long-term exposure to Batrachochytrium dendrobatidis (Bd) following population declines has left detectable selection signatures in the genome of the alpine tree frog, Litoria verreauxii alpina. Because prior work suggests limited immunity to Bd within the species, we did not expect persistence to be explained primarily by repeated shifts at canonical immune genes. Instead, we expected pathogen-associated differentiation, if present, to be diffuse and weakly shared across contrasts, potentially involving regulatory, signalling or reproduction pathways consistent with tolerance, compensatory recruitment or demographic turnover. Using genome-wide SNP data, we compared Bd-naïve and Bd-endemic populations and two 11-year temporal resampling contrasts. Candidate SNPs were annotated to identify genomic context, nearby genes, putative functions and overlap with previously reported Bd-associated transcriptomic responses. Using an empirical 99th-percentile XtX threshold, scans identified high-differentiation candidate SNPs in all contrasts. Most occurred in noncoding and putatively regulatory regions, with relatively few canonical immune genes. Candidate-associated genes were more often linked to signalling, transcriptional regulation and cell-cycle processes. A descriptive comparison with previous work in the species identified 64 candidate-associated genes matching Bd-associated transcriptomic annotations, supporting several immune-adjacent, regulatory, cellular and reproduction candidates. Persistence under endemic Bd might reflect pathogen-associated selection, compensatory recruitment and demographic change following recurrent adult mortality rather than uniform adaptive rescue through immune resistance.

6.0 Multimodal Plant-Animal Communication: Signaling Mechanisms, Coevolution, and Environmental Disruption.
Jasim HA, Khudair MY, Jasim SA, Abdulateef SM.
reviewKinases and signal transductionGrowthTrees and woody plantsPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-28
matched: signaling, effector*, elicitor*, defense, seed*, phenolog*
abstract

Plant-animal communication involves diverse forms of information transfer that can mediate ecologically important interactions, including pollination, seed dispersal, herbivory, and plant defense. This review synthesizes current knowledge of the chemical, visual, gustatory, electrical, acoustic, and vibrational information involved in these interactions, with particular emphasis on volatile organic compounds, herbivore-derived elicitors and effectors, multimodal signaling, sensory perception, learning, and evolutionary processes, including coevolution where reciprocal evidence is available. A central distinction is made between detectable traits or cues and functional biological signals, emphasizing the need for evidence linking signal production or modification to receiver detection, behavioral or physiological responses, and ecologically meaningful outcomes. Acoustic and vibrational phenomena are considered critically because their communicative significance remains less firmly established than that of chemical and visual signaling. The review further examines how climate change and other anthropogenic pressures may alter signal production, transmission, perception, and phenological synchrony, thereby reorganizing plant-animal interactions. Genetic manipulation of hormonal, volatile, and specialized-metabolite pathways is also evaluated as a potential tool for modifying plant signaling, together with its possible unintended ecological consequences. By integrating plant physiology, sensory ecology, animal behavior, evolution, and environmental change, this review identifies major evidence gaps and highlights the importance of plant-animal communication for biodiversity conservation, pollination, biological control, and sustainable agriculture.

6.0 Oak gall wasps lack conserved microbial symbionts
Naeem S, Rojas-Guerrero D, Łukasik P.
preprintTrees and woody plantsEcoEvoRxiv · 2026-10-23
matched: symbio*, microbiota, oak
abstract

Herbivorous insects frequently rely on microbial partners to exploit their host plants, yet for gall-inducing insect taxa, there were few attempts to comprehensively resolve their microbial associations. To address this gap, we characterised microbial abundance, composition and specificity in three Polish populations of cherry oak gall wasps (Cynips quercusfolii), a highly specialized phytophagous lineage. Using quantitative sequencing of bacterial and fungal marker region amplicons, we tested whether these insects harbour conserved microbiota likely associated with gall induction or host specialisation. Cynips quercusfolii and their associated parasitoid Torymus auratus harboured notably low bacterial loads, with a median of ca. 2.28 × 10⁴ 16S rRNA gene copies per insect. Their bacterial communities, primarily comprising genera with broad environmental distributions, were highly inconsistent among individuals and populations, and no bacterial lineage was consistently abundant or broadly conserved across the sampled gall wasps. In turn, fungal communities showed a stronger population-specific pattern. These results indicate oak gall wasps do not maintain abundant, conserved microbial symbionts despite their specialised herbivorous lifestyle. Instead, their bacterial communities appear sparse, variable, and dominated by environmental or transient taxa. This contrasts with symbiont-dependent lifestyles in many herbivores and suggests gall induction is unlikely to rely on stable bacterial mutualisms.

6.0 Plant endophytic fungi against plant-parasitic nematodes: from direct antagonism to plant-mediated biocontrol.
Giachero ML, Marquez N, Declerck S.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-09
matched: signaling, hormone*, immun*, pathogen*, endophyt*, defense, development
abstract

Plants have evolved sophisticated and tightly regulated immune systems to defend themselves against pathogen, whereas pathogens have developed diverse strategies to evade or manipulate host defenses. Plant-parasitic nematodes pose a particular challenge because of their intimate and prolonged interactions with host tissues, during which they actively manipulate plant physiology and immune responses. The limitations of conventional nematode control strategies have increased interest in plant-associated microorganisms as sustainable alternatives. Among these, endophytic fungi are emerging as promising allies because they can act through complementary mechanisms, combining direct antagonism of nematodes with the ability to modulate and prime plant immunity. This review integrates current knowledge on plant-nematode interactions with emerging insights into the role of endophytic fungi as modulators of plant defense. We examine how fungal endophytes influence hormone signaling, defense-related pathways, systemic resistance, and other components of plant immunity, while also considering their direct effects on nematode survival, development, and reproduction. We demonstrate that the protective potential of endophytic fungi extends beyond direct biocontrol and involves a broader reprogramming of plant responses that can enhance resistance to nematode attack. Finally, we discuss the factors that determine the consistency of these interactions, including fungal and plant genotype, environmental conditions, and the physiological and ecological context of the plant-fungus-nematode interaction. Understanding these mechanisms will be critical for translating endophyte-mediated plant protection into reliable and sustainable strategies for nematode management.

6.0 The effect of Rhizobium-field pea symbiosis on host plant quality and subsequent herbivory by the pea leaf weevil, Sitona lineatus (Coleoptera: Curculionidae)
Fatukasi OI, Thilakarathna MS, Evenden ML.
preprintGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-22
matched: rhizobi*, symbio*, defense, root, leaf, growth, development
abstract

Abstract Legumes develop symbiotic relationships with rhizobia bacteria, enabling biological nitrogen fixation and providing nitrogen for plant growth and development. This nitrogen availability influences plant nutrition and chemical defense that affect herbivores. The pea leaf weevil (PLW), Sitona lineatus L. is an herbivore of legumes that depends on nutritious foliage and root nodules for food in different parts of its lifecycle. Adult PLW overwinter and emerge in spring to seek hosts and mates. Following host colonization, reproductively active weevils mate and oviposit in the soil. Larval development occurs in root nodules followed by pupation in the soil. Adults that emerge in the fall prepare to overwinter in a reproductively inactive state. We tested the hypothesis that Rhizobium -field pea interactions influence plant traits and subsequent herbivory by adult PLW in different physiological states. Field pea plants were inoculated with different Rhizobium leguminosarum strains that either fix nitrogen (wild-type; nif+ ) or do not fix nitrogen (mutant-type; nif- ) and were compared to nitrogen and water-treated plants as positive and negative controls. The nif+ positively affected field pea height, nitrogen and chlorophyll content compared to the nif- inoculated plants. Rhizobium -mediated plant traits only affected adult PLW in the fall when weevils were preparing to overwinter. The fall weevils preferred the nif+ over the nif- , nitrogen or water-treated plants. Fall weevil preference for nif+ treated plants suggests that host nutritional quality, rather than plant defense, drives weevil feeding behaviour before winter. Understanding the effect of rhizobia-mediated plant-herbivore interactions will help develop sustainable management strategies for pests of legumes.

6.0 Unveiling the resistance risk and molecular mechanism of Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzicola to zhongshengmycin.
Luo J, Sun J, Tian Y … Liu X.
GrowthPesticide biochemistry and physiology · 2026-09-12
matched: pathogen*, Xanthomonas, leaf, growth
abstract

Zhongshengmycin, an N-aminoglycoside agricultural antibiotic, has been widely used to control bacterial leaf streak (BLS) caused by Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzicola (Xoc) in China. However, the resistance risk and its underlying molecular mechanisms remain poorly understood. In this study, we determined the sensitivity of 101 Xoc isolates collected from major rice-growing regions in China, with a mean EC50 value of 0.7005 ± 0.2407 μg/mL; no naturally occurring resistant strains were detected. Five resistant mutants were obtained through laboratory adaptation, and these mutants exhibited significantly reduced growth rate, motility, biofilm formation, and pathogenicity, indicating substantial fitness costs. No cross-resistance was detected between zhongshengmycin and kasugamycin, zinc thiazole, tetramycin, or xinjunan acetate. Whole-genome resequencing combined with genetic transformation verified that the I162S mutation in the ribosomal protein S3 (rpsC) conferred low-level resistance to zhongshengmycin. Transcriptome analysis revealed significantly upregulated amino acid biosynthesis pathways and two GNAT family acetyltransferase genes in resistant mutants. Collectively, these findings suggest that zhongshengmycin resistance in Xoc may be co-mediated by the rpsC mutation, upregulation of key amino acid biosynthesis pathways, and overexpression of GNAT family acetyltransferases. This work provides a scientific basis for field monitoring of resistance to zhongshengmycin and sustainable management strategies against BLS.

14.0 Nitric Oxide Signaling in Photosynthetic Resilience: Linking Photoprotection and Carbon Gain in Tropical Crops.
Islam MS, Methela NJ, Cho YB … Mun BG.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionHormonesInternational journal of molecular sciences · 2026-09-25
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, ABA, pathogen*, salinity, drought, hypoxia, flooding, chloroplast*, stoma*
abstract

Tropical crops are increasingly exposed to climate-driven, recurrent combinations of heat, excessive irradiance, high vapor pressure deficit (VPD), drought, flooding, salinity, and pathogen pressure that collectively impair photosynthesis and reduce crop productivity. Unlike individual stress events, these interacting stresses simultaneously disrupt photoprotection, electron transport, carbon assimilation, stomatal regulation, and chloroplast redox homeostasis, ultimately limiting whole-plant carbon gain under fluctuating environments. Nitric oxide (NO) has emerged as a central signaling molecule coordinating these adaptive responses, yet its roles in photosynthetic regulation remain dispersed across molecular, physiological, and crop-specific studies. This review presents an integrated framework linking chloroplast-level NO signaling with whole-plant photosynthetic performance in major tropical crops, including maize, sugarcane, banana, cassava, and oil palm. We propose that NO regulates photosynthetic resilience through three interconnected axes: (i) reversible protein S-nitrosylation that modulates photosynthetic complexes and Calvin-Benson cycle enzymes; (ii) maintenance of chloroplast redox homeostasis through coordinated reactive oxygen species (ROS) and reactive nitrogen species (RNS) signaling, antioxidant networks, and S-nitrosothiol metabolism; and (iii) ABA-mediated stomatal regulation that optimizes the trade-off between carbon assimilation, stomatal conductance, and water-use efficiency under environmental stress. We further discuss the emerging roles of S-nitrosothiols (SNOs) and S-nitrosoglutathione (GSNO) as central regulators of NO homeostasis and potential biochemical indicators linking chloroplast signaling with whole-plant carbon performance. By synthesizing current evidence across climate-relevant stress combinations, including heat + high light, drought + high VPD, flooding + hypoxia, salinity + pathogen interactions, this review provides a conceptual framework for understanding NO-mediated photosynthetic resilience and identifies key research priorities for developing NO-based strategies to enhance productivity and climate resilience in tropical cropping systems.

12.5 Identification of LsSLR1 gene in Leymus secalinus and validation of its function in Arabidopsis thaliana.
Li J, Zhang X, Miao Y … He W.

abstract

Leymus secalinus is a rhizomatous perennial high-quality gramineous forage that expands its habitat and adjusts its ecological niche through underground rhizome differentiation. Rhizomes are its strategy to adapt to drought, salinity and other adversities, and have important ecological and breeding value. Although tillering regulation has been well studied in gramineous crops, the molecular mechanisms underlying rhizome differentiation in forage grasses remain largely unexplored, hindering molecular breeding for stress tolerance. This study cloned a growth-related gene LsSLR1 (SLENDER RICE1) from L. secalinus and characterized its biological function. These findings provide a theoretical basis for understanding rhizome tillering regulation in L. secalinus and offer new insights into the evolution of tillering traits in gramineous plants, with implications for forage molecular breeding.Supplementary informationThe online version contains supplementary material available at https://doi.org/10.1007/s12298-026-01821-4.

11.5 Genome-wide identification of the TIFY gene family in Sorghum bicolor and functional characterization of SbTIFY9 in salt stress tolerance.

matched: signaling, phytohormone*, hormone*, salt stress, NaCl, flooding, stress respon*, nucleus, root, root growth, leaf, seed*
abstract

The TIFY gene family is a plant-specific group of transcriptional regulators characterized by a conserved TIFY domain (TIF[F/Y]XG), participating in plant development, phytohormone signaling and abiotic stress responses. In this study, 21 SbTIFY genes were genome-widely identified in sorghum (Sorghum bicolor L.). Heterologous overexpression of SbTIFY9 in Arabidopsis improved true leaf expansion, fresh weight at germination, and root growth and biomass at seedling stage under 125 and 150 mM NaCl, demonstrating its positive role in salt tolerance. 11.0 Unveiling the adaptive strategies in Blumea Balsamifera roots under salt stress through integrated physiological and multi-omics analysis.
Mo Z, Guo C, Chen S … Pang Y.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-07
matched: signal transduction, signaling, jasmon*, hormone*, defense, salt stress, cell wall*, root, roots, leaf, growth, lignin*
abstract

BackgroundSoil salinization is a growing constraint on agricultural productivity, yet the adaptive mechanisms of many medicinal plants-particularly Blumea balsamifera, a species of significant economic and pharmacological value-remain poorly understood. In this study, five treatments-CK (control), LS (low salt), MS (medium salt), HS (high salt), and EHS (extremely high salt)-were established to systematically investigate the comprehensive effects of salt stress on the growth, physiological metabolism, and molecular regulatory networks of B. balsamifera.ResultsThe results showed that salt stress significantly inhibited the growth of B. balsamifera, causing leaf chlorosis and wilting. In roots, the activities of antioxidant enzymes (SOD, POD, CAT) and the level of malondialdehyde (MDA) initially increased and then decreased, while the accumulation of osmoregulatory substances (PRO, SS, SP) and lignin (LIG) progressively increased. Transcriptomic analysis identified 5,701 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) shared between the HS vs CK and EHS vs CK comparisons, which were significantly enriched in functional terms including transcription factor activity, cell wall organization, plant hormone signal transduction. Metabolomic analysis identified 590 and 647 differentially accumulated metabolites (DAMs) in the HS vs CK and EHS vs CK comparisons, respectively. Under moderate stress, flavonoid synthesis and cutin/suberin biosynthesis pathways were significantly enriched, whereas under extreme stress, the phenylpropanoid pathway, carbohydrate metabolism pathway, and oxylipin/jasmonate-related metabolism pathways were significantly activated. Integrated multi-omics analysis further revealed that cutin, suberin, and wax biosynthesis, phenylpropanoid biosynthesis, and oxylipin/jasmonate-related metabolism are the core synergistic pathways in the root response of B. balsamifera to salt stress, mediating physical barrier construction, chemical defense and structural reinforcement, and lipid signal transduction, respectively.ConclusionCollectively, these findings uncover a multilayered adaptive strategy in B. balsamifera roots under salt stress, namely physical barrier formation, chemical-structural defense, and membrane lipid signaling. These results provide theoretical insights and key molecular targets for breeding salt-tolerant varieties and developing stress-resilient cultivation strategies.

10.5 Biostimulant-Mediated Enhancement of Quinoa Tolerance to NaCl-Induced Salinity Stress Through Foliar Application of Ascorbic Acid, Saponins and Quinoa Seed Extract.
Ben Youssef R, Goussi R, Friji C … Manaa A.
Ca2+GrowthPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-28
matched: salt sens*, salt stress, salinity, NaCl, seed*, growth
abstract

Quinoa (Chenopodium quinoa Willd.) is widely recognized for its relatively high tolerance to salinity, but substantial genotypic variation exists, with some varieties remaining sensitive to severe salt stress. Valorizing quinoa biomass and processing-derived resources also offers opportunities for developing zero-waste value chains and producing value-added food and non-food products. This study evaluated foliar applications of saponins (SAP, 0.15%), quinoa seed extract (QSE, 3%), and ascorbic acid (AsA, 0.2 mM) to improve the physiological and biochemical performance of quinoa under salinity (300 mM NaCl). AsA showed the strongest growth-promoting effect, increasing total dry weight by 115% compared with salt-stressed plants. Overall, AsA, SAP, and QSE enhanced the resilience of salt-sensitive quinoa under severe salinity, highlighting a potential zero-waste strategy in which quinoa-derived resources, particularly saponins and quinoa seed extract, can be valorized as foliar biostimulants to support sustainable quinoa production in salt-affected environments.

10.5 Genome-Wide Characterization of the Wheat Pum Gene Family and Heterologous Functional Analysis of TaPum-III-5b in Arabidopsis Under Salt Stress.
Zheng W, Cui Z, Li P … Yan B.

matched: reactive oxygen species, abscisic acid, ABA, jasmon*, MeJA, salt stress, drought, stress respon*, nuclear
abstract

Pumilio (Pum)-family proteins are evolutionarily conserved RNA-binding proteins that regulate post-transcriptional processes; however, their specific functions in staple crops, for example, wheat (Triticum aestivum), remain poorly understood. We identified 56 TaPum genes and classified them into three phylogenetic subgroups (I-III). This study characterizes the Pum gene family in wheat and identifies the TaDREB2B-TaPum-III-5b module as a candidate regulatory pathway associated with salt-stress responses. Its potential application in wheat improvement requires further validation in wheat.

Salt stress limits plant growth, but the contribution of COP9 signalosome subunit 5 (CSN5) to salt tolerance in Salvia miltiorrhiza remains unclear.
