10.0 Molecular mechanisms and application potential of MYC2 transcription factor-mediated plant stress responses.
Yang LQ, Shu SL, Zhang YH, Huang G.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-05
matched: signalling, jasmon*, JA, hormone*, pathogen*, defence, salinity, drought, stress respon*, polarity, growth, CRISPR
abstract

Plant adaptation to drought, salinity, temperature extremes, pathogens, and herbivorous insects depends on coordinated hormone signalling, transcriptional control, and metabolic reprogramming. The jasmonic acid (JA) pathway is a major regulator of defence and the growth-defence trade-off, and the bHLH transcription factor MYC2 is a central node in JA output. Mechanistically, MYC2 is controlled through demonstrated protein-protein interactions with JAZ repressors, MED25, and selected transcriptional or chromatin regulators; it binds G-box-related cis-elements to regulate specific promoters, whereas many reported links to LOX/AOS, CBF, and antioxidant pathways are transcriptional or pathway-level rather than physical interactions. This integrative narrative review compares MYC2 orthologues across species, distinguishes MYC2-specific functions from those shared with MYC3/MYC4 or other clade IIIe bHLHs, and organises beneficial and detrimental stress phenotypes around pathogen lifestyle, target-gene polarity, tissue and developmental context, paralogue redundancy, and experimental design. We integrate genome-wide DNA-binding, transcriptomic, metabolomic, and protein-level studies, and critically evaluate demonstrated versus prospective applications of CRISPR/Cas genome editing. The synthesis identifies regulatory strategies that may exploit MYC2 networks whilst limiting growth, defence, and quality trade-offs.

10.0 Rhizosphere dynamics and microbial mechanisms for improving plant resilience to soil salinity.
Kaya C, Ashraf M, Shabala S.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityJournal of plant physiology · 2026-10-18
matched: signaling, abscisic acid, jasmon*, microbiome*, salinity, osmotic stress, nutrient*, root, roots, root architecture
abstract

The rhizosphere is a critical interface where salinity-induced ionic, osmotic, and oxidative stresses are shaped by reciprocal interactions between plant roots, soil properties, and microorganisms. The paper discusses how microbial traits governing ion transport, antioxidant capacity, hormonal signaling, and nutrient mobilization contribute to plant resilience to soil salinity and examines the physiological, molecular, and ecological basis of these interactions. Under saline conditions, changes in root architecture and exudation profiles influence the recruitment and activity of microbial communities, including halotolerant and plant-beneficial taxa capable of producing exopolysaccharides, 1-aminocyclopropane-1-carboxylate (ACC) deaminase, siderophores, and other functional metabolites. These interactions create reciprocal feedbacks that connect microbial community assembly with root physiology, nutrient mobilization, and stress signaling. Beneficial microorganisms alleviate ionic and osmotic stress through biofilm formation, improved rhizosphere water retention, regulation of sodium (Na+) and potassium (K+) transport, and modulation of key ion transport systems involved in plant K+/Na+ homeostasis. They also influence phytohormonal networks involving indole-3-acetic acid, abscisic acid, and jasmonate, while enhancing antioxidant defenses and redox homeostasis. By integrating these interconnected processes, the review highlights the rhizosphere as a dynamic functional system in which microbial activities can reinforce plant physiological adaptation to salinity. This framework provides a basis for understanding how rhizosphere processes can be harnessed to improve plant salinity resilience and develop more effective microbiome-informed strategies for sustainable crop production in salt-affected soils.

10.0 SDG26 integrates ABA-dependent and ABA-independent pathways to regulate cold stress responses in Arabidopsis via chromatin modification.
Zhang X, Lapierre J, Zumsteg J … Berr A.
HormonesGrowthTrees and woody plantsThe Plant journal : for cell and molecular biology · 2026-10-01 · also: The Plant Journal
matched: abscisic acid, ABA, drought, cold stress, stress respon*, stoma*, growth
abstract

Plants constantly face adverse environmental conditions, with temperature drops posing major threats to growth and productivity. To adapt, plants have evolved complex mechanisms of transcriptional reprogramming. While various cold-responsive pathways have been described, the contribution of chromatin-level regulation, particularly histone modifications, remains poorly understood. Here, we identify the histone methyltransferase SET DOMAIN GROUP 26 (SDG26) as a positive regulator of cold stress responses in Arabidopsis thaliana. We show that SDG26 is transcriptionally induced and posttranscriptionally stabilized by cold, and that its loss of function compromises several abscisic acid (ABA)-related phenotypes. Collectively, our results reveal that SDG26 integrates ABA-dependent and ABA-independent pathways to fine-tune Arabidopsis responses to abiotic stresses. We further establish SDG26 as a chromatin modifier contributing to stress-responsive H3K36me3 enrichment at specific loci. Together, our findings position SDG26 as a chromatin-based hub that coordinates cold acclimation and water conservation to enhance plant resilience.

9.5 Direction-aware graph attention modeling and co-expression integration reveal novel core regulators of shared drought and salinity stress responses in rice leaves.
Safarzadeh M, Tohidfar M.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-25
matched: signaling, hormone*, salinity, drought, stress respon*, leaf, leaves
abstract

Although differential expression analysis has identified numerous drought- and salinity-responsive genes in rice, it cannot resolve the directional, transcription factor (TF)-driven regulatory architecture underlying responses across independent drought and salinity datasets. To address this, we applied TF-Anchored Directed SiGaResNet, a signed, direction-aware graph attention residual network with a TF-anchored edge-retention rule and permutation-based validation, to RNA-seq profiles from 92 rice leaf samples (51 stress, 41 control) derived from independent drought and salinity studies. Unlike conventional correlation- or co-expression-based approaches, the framework constrains regulatory inference around TF nodes while learning directed, signed relationships through supervised graph representation learning. Differential expression analysis identified 719 genes (405 upregulated, 314 downregulated), with induced transcripts enriched in stress-response and hormone-signaling processes and repressed transcripts dominated by photosynthesis- and secondary-metabolism-related processes. The framework showed strong cross-validated classification performance (MCC = 0.78 ± 0.17; AUC = 0.95 ± 0.05). The resulting directed gene regulatory network comprised 137 nodes and 163 edges and exhibited a sparse, modular architecture (modularity = 0.77; 15 communities), with TFs significantly enriched among regulatory source nodes (P < 0.001). Hub regulators were distributed across multiple TF families, predominantly bZIP, MYB-related, and NAC. Eighteen of thirty-two prioritized TF-encoding genes were independently corroborated within stress-associated WGCNA modules, and four comparatively uncharacterized candidates (Os07g0176200, Os05g0569300, Os03g0231000, and Os02g0187700) received convergent computational support from promoter cis-element enrichment, protein functional-association topology, and leave-one-out cross-validation classification. Quantitative real-time PCR further confirmed stress-responsive expression of all four candidates under separate drought and salinity treatments, supporting their prioritization as putative candidate regulators. These findings indicate that direction- and sign-aware graph attention modeling can integrate transcriptomic information from independent drought and salinity studies to identify biologically coherent, TF-centered regulatory patterns and prioritize candidate regulators for further functional investigation, without establishing causal regulatory mechanisms.

9.0 Coordinated Changes in Water Relations, Antioxidant Defense, and Metabolic Enzyme Activities in Maize Seedlings Under Salt Stress
Aliyeva N, Aliyeva D, Gurbanova U … Huseynova I.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPreprints.org · 2026-10-21
matched: defense, salt stress, salinity, NaCl, leaf, seed*, growth, development
abstract

Salinity is a major abiotic stressor that restricts plant growth and development and impairs physiological and biochemical processes. This study aimed to investigate the effects of salt stress on the morphophysiological traits, oxidative status, antioxidant defense system, and associated metabolic processes of maize (Zea mays L.) seedlings. The results showed that increasing salinity exacerbated oxidative stress, impaired water status and photosynthetic activity, and markedly reduced growth in maize seedlings. Increasing NaCl concentrations progressively inhibited plant growth and leaf development, with the most pronounced effects observed at 250 mM NaCl. Salt stress enhanced antioxidant enzyme activity and induced substantial changes in the non-enzymatic antioxidant system. Glutathione (GSH) content declined progressively with increasing stress intensity, whereas ascorbic acid (AsA) content increased under moderate stress but decreased sharply at high salinity. 9.0 Halotolerant Brucella intermedia PM63 enhances growth, ion homeostasis, antioxidant defense, and salt stress tolerance in rice.
Sultan MA, Paker NP, Ali J … Chaudhary HJ.

matched: defense, salt stress, salinity, NaCl, osmotic stress, root, shoot*, growth
abstract

Soil salinity limits rice growth through osmotic stress, ionic imbalance, and oxidative damage. In this study, halotolerant plant growth-promoting bacteria were isolated from Peganum harmala collected at the Khewra Salt Mine, Pakistan, and one of the best isolates, Brucella intermedia PM63, was evaluated for its ability to improve salt tolerance in rice. Ten bacterial isolates were screened for salt tolerance and plant growth-promoting (PGP) traits, and three selected strains were characterized by phenotypic tests, FTIR, SEM, 16S rRNA sequencing, and PCR detection of functional genes. PM63 tolerated up to 3 M NaCl and, under non-saline conditions, produced indole-3-acetic acid (167.27 µg mL⁻¹) and siderophores (110.72 PSU) and solubilized phosphate (157.72 µg mL⁻¹). PCR detected nifH, acdS, ituC, sfp, and opd in PM63. FTIR and SEM analyses showed changes in cell-surface functional groups and cell morphology under salt stress. In a greenhouse experiment with three rice varieties exposed to 0-300 mM NaCl, PM63 inoculation improved root and shoot growth, biomass, photosynthetic pigment content, and osmolyte accumulation. It also modulated antioxidant responses, increased K⁺ retention, reduced Na⁺ accumulation, and lowered oxidative damage (H₂O₂ and malondialdehyde) and electrolyte leakage. The magnitude of the response differed among varieties, with KSK-434 responding most strongly across several traits. These findings identify PM63 as a halotolerant plant growth-promoting bacterial inoculant with potential for improving rice performance under saline conditions.

8.0 Differential physiological responses to sodium and potassium chloride in Salicornia europaea, a model euhalophyte.
Mohammadzadeh P, Hajiboland R, Haeili M.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityFunctional plant biology : FPB · 2026-11-01
matched: H2O2, signalling, defence, salt stress, salinity, NaCl, growth, lignin*
abstract

Sodium (Na) and potassium (K) have comparable crustal abundances and similar physicochemical properties. However, due to the predominance of NaCl over KCl in natural salinity, most studies on plant salt responses have focused on NaCl, leaving responses to KCl salinity largely unexplored. To compare plant responses to Na and K salts, the euhalophyte Salicornia europaea was grown under equimolar (100 mM) NaCl and KCl treatments. NaCl salinity enhanced plant dry biomass, succulence, water relations, and the accumulation of organic osmolytes, whereas KCl salinity induced defence responses, lignin deposition, and caused complete plant mortality within 7 weeks. The levels of signalling molecules (H2O2, NO, and H2S) showed distinct patterns between NaCl and KCl treatments. Genes involved in ion compartmentation (HKT1, NHX1, SOS1, and VHA-A) were upregulated only under NaCl salinity. Moreover, NaCl reduced cellular ammonium levels, whereas KCl led to its accumulation, accompanied by declines in amino acid and protein contents, suggesting a disruption of ammonium assimilation under KCl stress. Comparative studies involving two glycophytes and four halophytes revealed that whereas glycophytes exhibited similar or less growth inhibition under KCl than NaCl, halophyte growth was severely inhibited by KCl but stimulated by NaCl. Notably, ammonium accumulation patterns differed between the two plant types; being higher under NaCl in glycophytes, but higher under KCl in halophytes. Overall, despite the chemical similarity between Na+ and K+ ions, our findings demonstrate that K fails to activate adaptive signalling pathways to salt stress, highlighting ion-specific mechanisms underlying halophyte tolerance to NaCl salinity.

8.0 Integrative GWAS and Transcriptome Analysis Reveal Genetic Architecture and Candidate Genes for Drought Tolerance During Soybean Germination.
Xia Z, Du S, Zong J … Cao Y.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-10-04
matched: reactive oxygen species, defense, drought, stress respon*, cell wall*, germination
abstract

Drought stress severely limits soybean production, and understanding the genetic basis of drought tolerance at the germination stage is important for breeding tolerant varieties. In this study, a panel of 270 soybean accessions was used for phenotypic evaluation and a genome-wide association study (GWAS) of five drought tolerance indices under control and PEG-simulated drought conditions. Using single-locus and multi-locus GWAS models, we detected 109, 115, 121, 86, and 46 significant SNPs, and 14, 7, 9, 10, and 3 QTNs for the five indices, respectively. Combining the results from both models, 97 important QTNs consisting of those controlling multiple traits under the single-locus model and those detected by both models were identified. These QTNs were distributed across 14 genomic intervals, with qDT-4-2 and qDT-9-1 which were associated with all five indices, being the key loci. Transcriptome analysis revealed 2109 genes that were commonly differentially expressed in three tolerant accessions under PEG treatment compared with control conditions. GO and KEGG enrichment analyses showed significant enrichment of pathways related to cell wall metabolism, osmotic adjustment, and antioxidant defense, including amino sugar and nucleotide sugar metabolism, starch and sucrose metabolism, galactose metabolism, and ascorbate and aldarate metabolism. Integration of GWAS and transcriptome data predicted 12 candidate genes within these loci, which may function in drought stress responses through regulating cell wall-modifying enzyme activity, accumulating osmoprotectants, and scavenging reactive oxygen species. Our findings provide useful information for understanding the genetic regulatory network of drought tolerance during soybean germination and offer candidate gene resources for marker-assisted breeding and functional gene discovery.

8.0 Nitrogen Regulation Under Heavy Metal Stress in Soil-Plant Systems: Implications for Plant Growth and Phytoremediation.
Sun R, Farooq A, Chen S … Wang J.
reviewCa2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-10-08
matched: pH, microbiome*, nitrate, nutrient*, heavy metal*, root, growth, phytoremediation
abstract

Nitrogen (N) is a vital macronutrient that dictates soil fertility and plant productivity. Heavy metal contamination threatens plant health by disrupting nutrient uptake and photosynthesis. Specifically, heavy metals accumulate in soils and inhibit key N-assimilatory enzymes, such as nitrate reductase. This inhibition restricts N availability, which limits protein synthesis and reduces overall plant biomass. This review synthesizes the recent literature to evaluate the mechanisms of heavy metal-N interactions, highlighting a critical scientific insight: N exerts a dual role in heavy metal stress. Externally, the chemical form of N uptake alters rhizosphere pH, which can either mobilize or precipitate toxic metal cations. Internally, optimal N assimilation fuels the synthesis of metal-chelating proteins (e.g., phytochelatins) required for cellular detoxification. A key conclusion of this review is that isolated agronomic or physiological interventions are insufficient to manage this stress in the field. Effective mitigation requires a coupled "soil-to-gene" approach. By combining macroscopic soil management (e.g., pH buffering and biochar application) to restrict external metal bioavailability with targeted molecular interventions (e.g., regulating root ion transporters and leveraging microbiome-assisted multi-omics) to enhance internal detoxification, researchers can improve phytoremediation efficiency and restore soil health in multi-contaminated environments.

8.0 Roles and mechanisms of halophilic/salt-tolerant microorganisms in mitigating plant salt stress and promoting growth.
Cheng Z, Rong P.
reviewHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthFrontiers in microbiology · 2026-10-01
matched: auxin*, phytohormone*, microbiome*, endophyt*, defense, salt stress, salinity, nutrient*, germination, growth
abstract

Soil salinization is a major constraint on global agricultural productivity and food security, largely because excess salts disrupt osmotic balance, nutrient acquisition, ion homeostasis, and redox stability in plants. Halophilic and salt-tolerant microorganisms, including plant growth-promoting rhizobacteria, endophytes, and fungi, have emerged as promising biological tools for improving crop performance in saline environments. This review synthesizes current evidence on how these microorganisms mitigate salt stress and promote plant growth across diverse crop systems. The major mechanisms include modulation of phytohormone homeostasis, especially through auxin production and ACC deaminase activity; enhanced nutrient acquisition via nitrogen fixation, phosphate solubilization, and siderophore production; maintenance of ionic balance through reduced Na+ toxicity and improved K+/Na+ homeostasis; reinforcement of antioxidant defense systems; stimulation of osmolyte accumulation; and effective rhizosphere colonization through exopolysaccharide secretion and biofilm formation. Representative studies in rice, wheat, maize, tomato, soybean, cucumber, chickpea, common bean, quinoa, and banana consistently show improvements in germination, biomass, physiological resilience, and, in some cases, yield under salinity stress. However, important challenges remain, including strain specificity, inconsistent field performance, limited mechanistic resolution, ecological safety concerns, and the difficulty of translating laboratory strains into stable commercial products. Future progress will depend on multi-omics-guided mechanism dissection, precision microbiome engineering, improved formulation and delivery systems, and rigorous long-term field validation. Overall, halophilic/salt-tolerant microorganisms represent a promising strategy for saline agriculture and sustainable crop production.

7.5 A key tonoplast Na+/H+ antiporter gene VuNHX1 mediating salt tolerance in cowpea (Vigna unguiculata).
Su X, Zhu S, Gao J … Pan L.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-09-30
matched: salt stress, NaCl, stress respon*, tonoplast, root, roots, leaf, leaves, shoot*
abstract

Soil salinisation severely restricts the production of cowpea (Vigna unguiculata), and the VuNHX1 gene is a key candidate gene for regulating cowpea salt tolerance, yet its physiological and molecular regulatory mechanisms underlying salt stress response remain poorly elucidated. In this study, Agrobacterium rhizogenes K599-mediated hairy-root transformation was used to generate VuNHX1 overexpression (OE) and RNA interference (RNAi) hairy-root lines in three cowpea genotypes. Under 100 mmol L-1 NaCl stress, the VuNHX1 gene exhibited tissue-specific temporal expression, with its expression level peaking at 8 h in leaves and 12 h in roots. Compared with the control plants, the VuNHX1 OE lines showed higher leaf soluble sugar content and significantly lower cytoplasmic Na + content in roots; the K+ content increased in roots, the Na+/K+ ratio decreased, the root SOD activity and POD activity increased, and the root MDA content decreased. In contrast, the RNAi lines presented opposite changes in ion homeostasis and physiological indices. These results indicate that the VuNHX1 antiporter maintains Na+/K+ ion homeostasis in roots and mediates long-distance ion distribution between roots and shoots, which reduces Na+ translocation to aerial tissues, promotes leaf soluble sugar accumulation for osmotic adjustment, and modulates the antioxidant system in a tissue-specific manner. This study clarifies the physiological response of VuNHX1 to salt stress, providing a theoretical and experimental reference for the salt tolerance breeding of cowpea and other leguminous crops.

7.5 The effects of pseudophosphorylation on Vitis riparia dehydrin structure and function.
Murray MR, Boddington KF, Graether SP.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-08
matched: ROS, phosphoryl*, salinity, drought, nucleus, nuclear
abstract

Plants undergo a wide range of deleterious effects as a result of exposure to abiotic stress, such as cold, salinity, and drought conditions. Some of these effects include protein denaturation and aggregation, membrane leakage and phase transitions, and DNA damage. One protective measure includes the expression of dehydrins, which are members of the group 2 LEA protein family. Several studies have shown that the S-segment, a serine-rich tract found in many dehydrins, can be phosphorylated in vivo. The main functional role of this phosphorylation appears to recruit the dehydrin to the nucleus, though this is not an absolute requirement. Here, we investigate whether pseudophosphorylation (i.e. the substitution of serine residues with glutamate) had any effect on the protection of proteins, membranes and DNA using VrDHN1 as a model YSK2 dehydrin. The changes resulted in improved protection of lactate dehydrogenase from freeze-thaw damage and caused an increase in α-helicity after binding to SDS micelles. Pseudophosphorylation was found to reduce DNA binding but had no effect on the protection of DNA from ROS damage. In addition to a possible role in nuclear localization, phosphorylation may result in a tuning of dehydrin function toward improved protective ability.

abstract

BackgroundRING-type E3 ligases are key enzymes in protein ubiquitination modification and crucial regulators in plant growth and development. However, their roles in sugar beet (Beta vulgaris L.) remain unclear. 7.0 Non-coding RNA levels integrating analysis revealed the salt and drought stress response mechanism of miR395e-PvUXS1 pathway in switchgrass.

abstract

Soil salinization and drought are major constraints on global agricultural production, yet the shared molecular mechanisms by which forage crops respond to these stresses remain incompletedly understood. Switchgrass (Panicum virgatum L.) is a perennial forage and bioenergy crop with adaptation to saline, semi-arid, and drought-prone environments. 7.0 clf mutation affects cold stress response and development in Arabidopsis thaliana
Longo C, Fiorella GF, Lepri A … Vittorioso P.
preprintGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-21
matched: cold stress, freezing, stress respon*, leaf, growth, development
abstract

Abstract Background Plants acquire freezing tolerance through cold acclimation, a period at low, non-freezing temperature during which gene expression is reprogrammed. This response is driven by the C-repeat binding factors (CBFs) but unfolds on chromatin shaped by the Polycomb Repressive Complex 2 (PRC2), which silences genes through the histone mark H3K27me3. Several Polycomb components converge on the CBF loci; what the histone methyltransferase CURLY LEAF (CLF) alone contributes to the cold response is not known. Results To ask whether CLF controls the cold response, we compared the transcriptomes of wild type and clf-29 plants before cold, after three hours of cold, after four days of acclimation and after a further drop towards freezing temperature. The canonical pathway does not depend on CLF: the CBF genes and their COR targets were induced normally in the mutant. To see what the loss of CLF does instead, we compared the two genotypes within each condition. We found a broad misregulation that builds up with age at control temperature, from about one thousand to several thousand genes, and that acclimation almost abolishes, leaving fewer than two hundred. To establish whether any part of the cold response depends on CLF, directly or not, we tested the genotype-by-treatment interaction and scored the genes for H3K27me3. We found such a dependence almost only at acclimation, on genes that are depleted of H3K27me3 and relate to growth; the genes responding to short-term cold only in the mutant are enriched for H3K27me3 but not for the telobox motif that recruits PRC2. Finally, we measured survival to freezing: clf-29 survived better than the WT, most clearly after acclimation. Conclusions CLF is not required for the canonical cold pathway, consistent with redundant Polycomb control of the CBF loci. Its loss mainly affects a developmental programme whose deregulation builds up during growth at control temperature and is strongly attenuated by cold. Cold acclimation thus emerges as a condition in which the H3K27me3-dependent effects of CLF can be separated from secondary, growth-related ones, and the improved survival of the mutant suggests that CLF restrains, rather than supports, the acquisition of freezing tolerance.

sialer MMF, Piergiovanni AR, SABETTA W.
preprintGrowthTrees and woody plantsResearch Square · 2026-10-23
matched: osmotic stress, drought, stress respon*, root, roots, seed*
abstract

Abstract The ongoing climate changes are increasing the frequency and severity of drought events, thereby threatening the productivity of many crops in the Mediterranean basin. Chickpea (Cicer arietinum L.), a nutritionally valuable grain legume of global importance, is widely cultivated in Southern Italy and exhibits considerable local biodiversity. In this study, nine chickpea landraces were comprehensively characterized by an integrated approach combining microsatellite-based genetic analysis, seed biochemical profiling, and gene expression analysis under simulated water deficit. Genetic variability assessed by SSR markers resolved the landraces into three clusters that largely corresponded to seed coat colour (white, red, black). Seed quality was evaluated by quantifying key biochemical parameters, including protein content, total phenolic compounds and carotenoids; red and black seeds contained significantly higher levels of phenolics and carotenoids than white seeds. Drought tolerance potential was investigated by imposing polyethylene glycol (PEG)-induced osmotic stress and quantifying the transcript abundance of two Kunitz-type trypsin inhibitor genes by quantitative PCR. The CaTPI-1 transcript exhibited a marked up-regulation in roots of red and black landraces under stress, whereas white-coated landraces showed reduced root accumulation of CaTPI-1. Collectively, these results indicate that red and black landraces combine enhanced antioxidant-related seed chemistry with a stress-responsive transcriptional profile potentially associated with improved tolerance to water deficit. These locally adapted chickpea genotypes constitute valuable genetic resources for conservation and breeding programmes aimed at enhancing chickpea resilience and nutritional quality in semi-arid Mediterranean agroecosystems.

6.5 Alkaline salt impairs germination recovery more severely than neutral salt in the euhalophyte Suaeda salsa.
Liu X.
Ca2+GrowthTrees and woody plantsFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-07
matched: pH, salt stress, NaCl, drought, root, shoot*, seed*, germination, growth
abstract

IntroductionSoil salinization and alkalization increasingly threaten global ecosystem stability, necessitating a clearer understanding of how dominant halophytes respond to complex salt stresses.MethodsThis study examined the differential effects of neutral and alkaline salt stresses on seed germination and early seedling growth of Suaeda salsa, a dominant halophyte in the Yellow River Delta. A two-factor factorial experiment was conducted using three salt types (NaCl, KCl, NaHCO3) across six concentration gradients (0-500 mmol L-1). A two-stage germination trial, comprising an initial stress phase and a post-stress recovery phase, assessed root and shoot growth, fresh mass accumulation, water potential, and germination ratios.ResultsSalt concentration primarily determined germination traits. At comparable molar concentrations, alkaline salt (NaHCO3) exerted significantly stronger inhibition on growth than the neutral salts (NaCl, KCl). Low-concentration NaCl promoted fresh mass accumulation and sustained shoot elongation through efficient osmotic adjustment, whereas alkaline stress severely impaired this capacity, inducing acute physiological drought. Future ecological restoration in the Yellow River Delta should prioritize amelioration of high-pH alkaline conditions to ensure natural regeneration of halophyte communities.

6.5 Carboxymethyl cellulose-ferulic acid nanocomposite enhances salinity tolerance and phytochemical accumulation in Dracocephalum moldavica via coordinated redox and osmotic regulation.
Sarvi S, Razavi SM, Khalofah A … Ghorbani A.
Ca2+GrowthBMC plant biology · 2026-07-22
matched: H2O2, salinity, leaf, stoma*, growth
abstract

BackgroundSalinity is a significant constraint on medicinal and aromatic plants that limits their growth and photosynthetic activity, increases oxidative stress, and disrupts water balance.ResultsThe present paper prepared a carboxymethyl cellulose-ferulic acid nanocomposite (FA-NC), which is a bio-based nano-formulation to enhance the performance of Dracocephalum moldavica in the presence of salinity stress, and the effect was directly compared to free FA. 6.5 Regulatory architecture tunes gene expression noise in the plant heat shock response
Goswami, R., Cano Ramirez … J. C. W.
preprintHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesbioRxiv · 2026-10-25
matched: jasmon*, defence, heat stress, stress respon*, nucleus, root, reporter*
abstract

Cells within the same tissue can respond differently to the same stress, yet how gene regulatory networks generate and control this variability remains unclear. Here, we show that cell-to-cell variation in the Arabidopsis heat shock response is coordinated across the heat shock regulon and that the distribution of responses is tuned by the underlying regulatory network. Imaging of an HSP70 promoter reporter revealed basal activity in a subset of root cells and heterogeneous induction after heat shock, with some cells remaining near pre-stress expression levels. Dual reporters showed strong coordination between independently integrated HSP70 reporter loci, consistent with substantial shared upstream regulatory variation. Single-nucleus RNA sequencing confirmed heterogeneous but coordinated activation of heat shock genes across root cell types. Stronger heat shock states at both ambient and heat stress temperatures were associated with a recurrent thermoprotective programme across root cell populations, whereas weak responders after heat shock were associated with distinct cell-type specific transcriptional programmes, including defence- and jasmonate-associated states in some root cell types. Stochastic modelling suggested that HSFA2-dependent amplification could broaden the distribution of HSP70 expression across cells. Consistent with this prediction, experiments showed that loss of HSFA2 reduced HSP70 induction and lowered cell-to-cell variability after controlling for differences in mean expression. Together, these results show that the plant heat shock network controls both the magnitude and distribution of stress-responsive gene expression across cells.

6.5 Spent mushroom substrate mediates lead-cadmium immobilization and plant growth in tailings: Enhanced phosphorus availability and rhizosphere microbial modulation.
Zhang Y, Wan M, He X … Zhan F.
Ca2+GrowthBioresource technology · 2026-10-25
matched: calcium, pH, nutrient*, heavy metal*, cadmium, root, growth
abstract

Mining activities cause severe heavy metal dispersion in tailings, threatening regional water and food security. The global mushroom industry generates enormous spent mushroom substrate (SM), a promising organic material for mine restoration. However, mechanistic comparisons between SM and three specific inorganic amendments (calcium carbide slag, CS; phosphogypsum, PG; coal gangue, CG) remain limited. A 60-day pot experiment on heavily polluted lead-zinc tailings (total lead: 16733 mg/kg; total cadmium: 78.04 mg/kg) compared SM with CS, PG, and CG treatments. Higher bioavailable lead under SM still reduced root lead accumulation by 62.31 %. This study demonstrates that SM remediates tailings via integrated chemical and biological regulation, providing sustainable waste recycling for mine rehabilitation.

6.5 Transcriptome Profiling Reveals the Molecular Regulatory Network in Response to Salt Stress in Intervarietal Hybrids of Chrysanthemum × morifolium 'Yannong Jinxia' × 'Yannong Qiujin'.

Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionGrowthBiology · 2026-09-24
matched: salt sens*, signaling, salt stress, salinity, growth, development
abstract

Salinity stress is a major abiotic factor that significantly constrains plant growth and development. Hybridization within the genus Chrysanthemum produces hybrid offspring exhibiting salt tolerance levels that are intermediate between those of the parental. However, the molecular mechanisms underlying this phenomenon remain unreported. In this study, the salt-sensitive chrysanthemum variety 'Yannong Jinxia' (JX) and the salt-tolerant chrysanthemum variety 'Yannong Qiujin' (QJ) were selected as parents for hybridization, aiming to elucidate the molecular basis of this intermediate salt tolerance. Results showed that under salt stress, the hybrid XT exhibited intermediate levels of antioxidant enzyme activity and MDA accumulation relative to its parents. 6.0 Physiological and Antioxidant Responses of Rough Lemon (Citrus jambhiri L.) Rootstocks to Foliar-Applied Green-Synthesized ZnO Nanoparticles Under Drought Stress.
Tehseen M, Hou Y, Khan TA … Wang L.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthTrees and woody plantsPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-10-06
matched: defense, drought, leaves, seed*, growth, microscop*
abstract

Drought stress affects citrus growth and physiological responses by impairing photosynthesis and antioxidant homeostasis. In this study, we produced green-synthesized zinc oxide nanoparticles (ZnO NPs) using leaves from Calotropis gigantea L. We assessed their effects on drought-stressed rough lemon (Citrus jambhiri L.) seedlings. UV-Visible spectroscopy, X-ray diffraction (XRD), scanning electron microscopy (SEM), and dynamic light scattering (DLS) confirmed the formation of hexagonal wurtzite ZnO nanoparticles with predominantly spherical morphology and nanoscale particle size. 6.0 ZnO and SiO2 nanopriming enhancing seedling performance and changing volatile profiles of Melissa officinalis under drought and salinity stress.
Mokhtarzadeh S.
GrowthTrees and woody plantsFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-09
matched: salinity, drought, seed*, germination, growth
abstract

IntroductionMelissa officinalis L. is an important medicinal and aromatic plant whose germination, seedling establishment, and secondary metabolism can be adversely affected by drought and salinity. This study investigated whether ZnO and SiO2 nanopriming could mitigate these effects and modify the volatile profile of M. officinalis.MethodsTwo independent factorial experiments were conducted under drought and salinity stress. Seeds were hydroprimed as the control or nanoprimed with ZnO or SiO2 at 50 or 100 mg L-1. Drought treatments were 0, -0.2, -0.4, and -0.6 MPa, while salinity treatments were 0, 4, 8, and 12 dS m-1. Germination and early seedling traits were evaluated. Selected 50 mg L-1 treatments under moderate stress conditions were subsequently subjected to volatile profiling by GC/GC-MS.ResultsNanopriming significantly improved several germination and early seedling traits, with the strongest responses generally observed at 50 mg L-1. ZnO at 50 mg L-1 increased germination from 42% in the non-primed control to 56% under -0.4 MPa drought and from 36% to 52% under 8 dS m-1 salinity. Low-dose ZnO and SiO2 also improved radicle and plumule growth and seedling vigour, although their effects declined under severe stress. Eleven volatile compounds were detected in all analyzed samples, with geranial and neral as the predominant constituents. Alloaromadendrene showed the largest descriptive change, increasing from 2.4% in the control to 6.3 -6.8% in selected treatments. Because volatile analysis was performed once per selected treatment (n = 1), these compositional differences were interpreted descriptively.DiscussionLow-dose nanopriming, particularly ZnO at 50 mg L-1, improved early-stage tolerance of M. officinalis to independently imposed drought and salinity stress and was associated with changes in its volatile profile. Replicated chemical analyses and greenhouse and field experiments are required to confirm the observed volatile responses and agronomic applicability.

15.5 The Membrane Continuum Governing Cadmium Uptake, Partitioning, and Detoxification in Plants.
Li X, Zhang J, Qin Q … Sun Y.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressEnvironmental pollution (Barking, Essex : 1987) · 2026-10-23
matched: Ca2+, ROS, signaling, heavy metal*, cadmium, plasma membrane, vacuol*, endoplasmic reticulum, ER, Golgi, trafficking, vesicle*
abstract

Cadmium (Cd) contamination threatens agro-ecological safety and food security; thus, elucidating mechanisms of Cd uptake and accumulation in plants is key to breeding low-Cd crops. Within the defensive network plants deploy against Cd stress, the plasma membrane and endomembrane system act as physical barriers to metal influx and as the core regulatory hub governing signal perception, transmembrane transport, and vacuolar compartmentalization for detoxification. This review examines the physiological and molecular mechanisms by which the cell membrane system influences Cd transport and accumulation in plants, focusing on five key dimensions: (1) the gating roles of plasma membrane transporters (e.g., ZIP, NRAMP, and HMA families) in root Cd uptake, efflux detoxification, and long-distance translocation to shoots; (2) compensatory mechanisms in intracellular homeostasis remodeling mediated by endoplasmic reticulum (ER) stress-induced unfolded protein response (UPR) and Golgi vesicle trafficking; (3) the vacuole as a central detoxification depot, achieving compartmentalized sequestration of free Cd2+ and macromolecular chelates through transmembrane transport networks comprising ABC, MTP, and CAX proteins; (4) profound effects of Cd-triggered dynamic remodeling of membrane lipid composition, lipid peroxidation, and membrane microdomain changes on physicochemical states and spatial distribution of transport channels; (5) membrane-initiated signaling networks (lipid sensors, Ca2+, ROS, NO) and their post-translational fine-tuning of transporter abundance and activity. This review integrates dynamic plant response mechanisms to heavy metal Cd from a multi-dimensional membrane biology perspective, aiming to provide theoretical references and novel insights for the molecular design and safe production of low-Cd crop varieties.

13.0 Integrating cortical microtubules into plant gravity signaling: dynamics, tubulin phosphorylation, and growth regulation.
Islam MS, Wyatt SE.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-08
matched: phosphoryl*, phosphatase*, signaling, auxin*, cell wall*, cytoskelet*, microtubul*, cell expansion, gravitrop*, seed*, growth
abstract

Plant gravity responses depend on coordinated signaling and growth-control mechanisms that convert directional and mechanical stimuli into differential cell expansion. Cortical microtubules are central to the conversion: they pattern cellulose synthase trajectories, set cell-wall anisotropy, and are remodeled in altered-gravity and tubulin-mutant systems. Yet the cytoskeletal regulatory pathway remains only loosely connected to the canonical, auxin-centered picture of how plants perceive and respond to gravity. Physiological, genetic, and phosphoproteomic evidence linking microtubules to gravitropism and gravity resistance, obtained under gravistimulation by reorientation at 1 g, hypergravity, and real or simulated microgravity, is organized into an explicit, layered framework. Three regulatory layers are proposed to act on cortical microtubule organization on distinct time scales: a slow transcriptional layer that adjusts the tubulin and regulator pool, an intermediate regulator-mediated layer of nucleation and severing, and a fast post-translational layer that modifies tubulin directly. Within the framework, phosphorylation of α-tubulin at threonine 349 (Thr349) by PROPYZAMIDE-HYPERSENSITIVE 1 (PHS1) is the best-characterized candidate for a molecular link between stress signaling and control of microtubule polymerization. Whole-seedling spaceflight phosphoproteomic data show reduced Thr349 phosphorylation across four α-tubulin isoforms (TUA1, TUA3, TUA4, and TUA6) and highlight Thr349 phosphorylation as a candidate post-translational regulator of cortical microtubule responses under spaceflight conditions. Future studies should test whether PHS1 controls Thr349 phosphorylation during gravity responses and identify the activating signal and opposing phosphatase. Resolving links with γ-tubulin-dependent nucleation, katanin-mediated severing, auxin transport, and cell-wall remodeling will explain how cortical microtubules shape gravity-directed growth.

9.5 Membrane environment-dependent relocation of phloem-loading sucrose transporters.
Yin S, Zhang X, Burt P … Liesche J.
GrowthMethodsPlant & cell physiology · 2026-10-21 · also: Plant and Cell Physiology
matched: plasma membrane, endocytosis, membrane domain*, leaves, phloem, epiderm*, development, live-cell imaging, microscop*
abstract

Plasma membrane transporters play an essential role in plant development and all physiological processes. Their activity is tightly controlled by a variety of mechanisms, including the rate of transporter endocytosis. There is evidence that the local membrane environment, in particular the sterol content, influences transporter endocytosis. Here, we analyzed the sucrose transporters StSUT1 (potato) and AtSUC2 (Arabidopsis) transiently expressed in Nicotiana benthamiana epidermal cells. Using live-cell imaging of fluorescent protein fusions combined with extracellular quenching by Trypan Blue, we quantitatively distinguished plasma membrane-localized from internal transporter pools. Pharmacological perturbation of sterols and sphingolipids with methyl-β-cyclodextrin, fenpropimorph and myriocin reduced sucrose transporter abundance at the plasma membrane and decreased uptake of the fluorescent sucrose analogue esculin. In Arabidopsis leaves, methyl-β-cyclodextrin treatment reduced endogenous AtSUC2 protein levels, confirming sterol-dependent regulation in planta. Single-molecule localization microscopy of AtSUC2-reporter constructs revealed its enrichment in ∼50 nm plasma membrane domains, with the localization becoming more homogeneous upon sterol depletion. Together, our results demonstrate that plasma membrane lipid composition modulates sucrose transporter localization and abundance, with likely relevance for the regulation of phloem loading and other membrane proteins.

9.0 WHIRLY1 Interactome Profiling Reveals the Chloroplast Fate Correlation with Stromule-like Morphologies in Arabidopsis Leaves Under Dark and DBMIB Stress Conditions.
Li M, Huang D, Yu Y … Miao Y.
GrowthPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-24
matched: nucleus, nuclear, chloroplast*, mitochondri*, cytoskelet*, organelle*, leaves, growth
abstract

The WHIRLY family comprises DNA/RNA-binding proteins localized to multiple subcellular compartments, including organelles and the nucleus. However, the subcellular distribution and functional roles of WHIRLY protein complexes are not fully characterized. In this study, a WHIRLY1 protein pulldown coupled with mass spectrometry analysis was used to profile the interacting proteome in an Arabidopsis thaliana plant overexpressing WHIRLY1-GFP. This analysis identified over 770 interacting proteins, with RNA-binding proteins representing the most enriched functional category. 8.5 Defining membrane-bound transcription factors in plant stress responses.
Zhu Z, Yi D, Peng J … Zhang S.
reviewCa2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressJournal of integrative plant biology · 2026-10-26
matched: H2O2, immun*, pathogen*, stress respon*, endoplasmic reticulum, ER, nuclear, Golgi, trafficking, organelle*, growth
abstract

Biotic and abiotic stresses rapidly alter water and H2O2 fluxes, membrane fluidity, organelle redox state, and endoplasmic reticulum (ER) proteostasis, affecting nuclear gene expression. Membrane-bound transcription factors (MTFs) and related membrane-associated pathways regulate the generation, localization, and activity of nuclear-competent forms of transcription factors through proteolysis, unconventional mRNA splicing, and post-translational regulation. This mini review examines ER-to-Golgi trafficking and proteolytic activation of bZIP17/28, IRE1-dependent splicing of bZIP60/OsbZIP50, the varied activation mechanisms of NAC/NTLs, and PIP-associated MYBs. PIP-MYB studies support transporter participation in MTF activation, although the contributions of transport activity and physical interaction remain difficult to separate. Most bZIP and NAC/NTL pathways respond to changes in ER, membrane, or organelle status. We discuss their roles in proteostasis, growth restraint, and immunity, and how pathogens exploit them. We also assess the evidence for direct transporter-MTF coupling and indirect regulation by membrane state. 8.5 Sample preparation of some plant tissues for transmission electron microscopy using high pressure freezing and freeze substitution.
Hsu CM, Chou YC, Yen HJ, Jane WN.
Abiotic stressGrowthMethodsBotanical studies · 2026-10-15
matched: freezing, cell wall*, vacuol*, organelle*, roots, leaves, microscop*
abstract

For sample preparation of biological block sectioning, high pressure freezing and freeze substitution (HPF-FS) is a better choice than chemical fixation. Freezing fixation can retain the original structures and preserve the soluble materials, and avoid the disadvantage of chemical fixation. Using high-pressure freezing, plant tissues are more difficult to freeze successfully than animal tissues and microbes because they have cell walls and varying degrees of vacuolation. For plant tissues, including the roots of Arabidopsis thaliana, 1-hexadecene is an ideal cryoprotectant/filler. The ability of highly vacuolated plant tissues (such as mature leaves and petals) to effectively remove intercellular air is crucial for successful high pressure freezing. Using the anthers of Arabidopsis thaliana, the leaves of Brachypodium distachyon, Axonopus compressus and Plantago asiatica, and the petals of Phalaenopsis bellina as examples, we found an effective method to remove air from the intercellular spaces: plant tissue in 1-hexadecene is placed in a vacuum desiccator, and then the air is removed using a vacuum pump, resulting in the release of numerous air bubbles. Plant tissue samples taken at different degassing times are then subjected to high pressure freezing and the subsequent required procedures of sample preparation. Based on the presentation of fine structures, the optimal time for different plant tissues can be determined and shows a positive correction with sample thickness. Good HPF-FS results show that the lipid bilayer within each organelle of the plant cells is clearly visible.

8.5 Synaptotagmin-mediated plasma membrane homeostasis is required to recruit the endocytic machinery during cold stress
Aznar-Moreno, J. A., Del Espino … M. A.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressbioRxiv · 2026-10-20
matched: signaling, receptor*, brassinosteroid*, cold stress, plasma membrane, endoplasmic reticulum, endocytosis
abstract

ABSTRACT Endocytosis depends on plasma membrane (PM) lipid composition, yet how plants sustain endocytic machinery recruitment during stress remains unclear. We investigated whether synaptotagmins SYT1 and SYT3 function in membrane homeostasis and endocytosis in Arabidopsis thaliana. In this work, we revealed that cold stress reversibly blocked FM4-64 internalization in syt1syt3 double mutants by displacing peripheral and core TPC subunits from the PM. Loss of SYT1 reduced brassinosteroid receptor internalization during cold stress, while double mutants showed enhanced brassinosteroid signaling. SYTs and VAP27 genes showed a condition-dependent genetic interaction in endocytosis: the syt1/vap27-1/vap27-3 triple mutant resembles vap27-1/vap27-3 under control conditions and syt1 during cold stress, suggesting overlapping contributions to endocytic regulation. Using molecular dynamics simulations of protein-membrane interactions, phosphoinositide depletion combined with increased diacylglycerol weakened TPC core membrane association. Our findings identify SYT1 and SYT3 as essential components for sustaining endocytosis during abiotic stress and support a model in which lipid homeostasis at endoplasmic reticulum-PM contact sites preserves the membrane composition required for TPC-dependent endocytosis.

8.5 Unexpected Localization of AGP Epitopes in the Plastids of Aldrovanda vesiculosa: New Questions About the Distribution of These Epitopes in Angiosperms.
Płachno BJ, Kapusta M, Feldo M … Leszczuk A.

matched: signaling, immun*, cell wall*, plasma membrane, cell expansion, roots, leaves, shoot*, development, confocal, microscop*
abstract

Arabinogalactan proteins (AGPs) are the glycoproteins that are characteristic of plant cells (except for some algae) and are essential for cell function (cell-cell communication, cell adhesion, cell expansion, cell-wall organization, and signaling) but also for the development of roots, shoots, leaves, flowers, and reproductive structures. They occur inside and on the surface of plant cells, but mainly at the interface between the plasma membrane and the cell wall. Reports on the occurrence of arabinogalactan proteins in plastids are very rare and unclear. Our goal was to determine whether AGP-reactive carbohydrate epitopes are associated with plastids and starch in turion cells of Aldrovanda vesiculosa L. To do this, the localization of the AGP-reactive carbohydrate epitopes was assessed using immunolabeling and confocal and electron microscopy. These findings provide further evidence for the unusual intracellular localization of AGP-reactive carbohydrate epitopes in plastids and highlight starch grains as a target for future studies aimed at determining the molecular identity and biological significance of these epitopes.

7.5 A single amino-acid switch reprograms ROP6 signaling to drive functional evolution in plants.
Zhang L, An Y, Lan T … Zhang M.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityThe New phytologist · 2026-10-20 · also: New Phytologist
matched: signaling, auxin*, PIN\d+, effector*, plasma membrane, cytoskelet*, actin, development
abstract

Rho-related GTPases of plants (ROPs) are evolutionarily conserved molecular switches that typically maintain PIN-formed proteins at the plasma membrane (PM), yet their functional divergence remains poorly understood. Here, we reveal that a single amino-acid substitution at residue 194 acts as a core determinant, driving the evolutionary split between Brassicaceae and Malvaceae ROP6 orthologs: glycine enforces PM stabilization, whereas alanine licenses endocytic function. In cotton, the Malvaceae-predominant alanine mutated GhROP6 displays an auxin-independent manner. The distinct activity is attributed to the recruitment of three GhRIC effectors that directly bind the actin cytoskeleton and promote F-actin assembly and bundling. The resulting actin reorganization facilitates GhPIN3a internalization and proteolysis, consequently enhancing cotton fiber elongation. Our findings demonstrate that a single amino-acid change can reprogram ROP signaling and uncover a molecular switch underlying the functional evolution of ROP6 in regulating plant development.

matched: Ca2+, immun*, plasma membrane, chloroplast*, actin, leaves
abstract

Chloroplasts maintain specific intracellular distribution patterns in response to environmental conditions to maximize photosynthetic performance and prevent photodamage. We previously proposed that a chloroplast anchoring mechanism functions to fix chloroplasts at appropriate positions and reported that this machinery is actin-dependent and regulated by Ca2+ (Takamatsu and Takagi 2011). In the present study, we examined the possible involvement of villin, a Ca2+-sensitive actin-binding protein (ABP), in chloroplast anchoring in spinach mesophyll cells. abstract

The expansion of soybean (Glycine max (L.) Merr.) cultivation into Western Canada is constrained by distinct abiotic stressors, including shorter growing seasons and drought. 6.0 PPR9 mediates mitochondrial nad transcript maturation required for complex I biogenesis and early plant development in Arabidopsis.
Kobaivanov E, Maltz T, Kittel M … Ostersetzer-Biran O.
GrowthPlant molecular biology · 2026-10-21
matched: nuclear, mitochondri*, seed*, embryo*, growth, development
abstract

The biogenesis and function of plant mitochondria relies on the expression of the organellar genome, which involves extensive post-transcriptional processing of organellar transcripts in order to generate mature functional RNAs. This includes the removal of introns from many essential organellar transcripts through splicing, a process mediated by diverse nuclear-encoded RNA-processing factors, including numerous members of the pentatricopeptide repeat (PPR) protein family. Here, we report the analysis of PPR9 (At1g03560), a mitochondrial P-class PPR protein originally identified in genetic screens for essential protein cofactors. Accordingly, CI assembly is disrupted in ppr9 plants, resulting in altered OXPHOS activity and broad physiological defects. Together, these findings identify PPR9 as a key regulator of mt-RNA maturation in Arabidopsis, enabling CI biogenesis and optimal respiratory functions, and further highlight the roles of nuclear-encoded factors in coordinating mitochondria functions and (early) plant growth and development.

Xu B, Chen ZH.

abstract

Abscisic acid (ABA) was first identified in plants over six decades ago and is now recognized as one of the central phytohormones in plants, regulating growth, development, and physiological adaptation to environmental changes. In this Centennial Update, we use two influential ABA-related articles published in Plant Physiology in the early 2000s and discuss complementary advances in stress-responsive transcription and ABA biosynthetic regulation. 13.0 Proteomic and Molecular Biological Analyses of Response Mechanism in Rice (Oryza sativa L. cv. Koshihikari) Under Drought Stress.
Shohag S, Yamaguchi H, Hitachi K … Komatsu S.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressInternational journal of molecular sciences · 2026-10-07
matched: phosphoryl*, immun*, osmotic stress, drought, nuclear, cytoskelet*, root, roots, primary root*, root growth, seed*, growth
abstract

Drought stress severely limits rice growth, while roots play a key role in sensing water deficits and triggering adaptive responses. To investigate time-dependent molecular responses in rice (Oryza sativa L. cv. Koshihikari) under drought stress induced by polyethylene glycol (PEG), seedlings were exposed to 10 and 20% PEG for 4 and 8 days, followed by morphological, proteomic, immunoblot, polymerase chain reaction, and ATP content analyses. Primary root length and total root fresh weight were significantly decreased with 20% PEG for 8 days compared with untreated. 13.0 Shoot to root transport of the HY5 transcription factor drives root developmental plasticity under neighbour shade
van der Velde, K., Ali … K.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-20
matched: signaling, signalling, auxin*, root, lateral root*, root development, leaves, phloem, shoot*, hypocotyl*, development, live imaging
abstract

Plants perceive competitors through the reflection of Far-Red light from neighbouring leaves. A decrease in the Red: Far-red light ratio (R:FR) is perceived by phytochrome photoreceptors and triggers elongation responses aboveground, allowing plants to anticipate future competition for light; their primary energy source. However, low R:FR perception by the shoot also prompts an alteration of root development, possibly to conserve resources. An important regulator of this response is the bZIP transcription factor ELONGATED HYPOCOTYL 5 (HY5), which can be transported from the shoot to the root, where it suppresses auxin signaling and lateral root outgrowth. However, it remains unknown whether HY5 transport is the direct link between shoot-perceived low R:FR and a decrease in lateral root outgrowth. We therefore sought to uncover the mechanics and speed of HY5 transport. To this end, we constructed shoot-specific inducible lines to perform live-imaging of HY5 transport using a vertical confocal microscope. This allowed us to follow the transport and unloading of HY5 from the phloem into the protophloem pericycle, where it becomes nuclearly localized, in real time over a 24-hour period. To mimic plant competition, we applied supplemental FR light and observed that this increased the HY5 protein levels in the shoot and subsequent HY5 protein transport from shoot-to-root. Finally, we were able to show that shoot-expressed HY5 is necessary and sufficient to induce the root response to shoot perceived Far-Red light. This work showcases in detail how a small general transcription factor functions as a long-distance signal between shoot and root and integrates environmental signalling over long distances in the plant.

12.0 Immunolocalization of L-DOPA and differential MpPPO1-3 expression in the embryonic axis and cotyledons of germinating Mucuna pruriens var. utilis seeds.
López-Sánchez L, Camas-Reyes A, Hernández-Orihuela AL, Martínez-Antonio A.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-14
matched: apoplast*, immun*, plasma membrane, root, roots, primary root*, meristem*, shoot*, cotyledon*, seed*, embryo*, microscop*
abstract

The cell-specific localization of L-DOPA (levodopa) in plant tissues has not been reported previously. Here, we describe the first immunolocalization of L-DOPA in mature seeds of Mucuna pruriens var. utilis at 2-6 days after imbibition (DAI), using silver-enhanced light microscopy immunocytochemistry with a specific polyclonal anti-L-DOPA antibody. Histological sections from the embryonic axis and embryo cotyledons revealed that L-DOPA accumulates predominantly in the cytoplasm of parenchymatous and starch-containing cotyledon cells, as well as in the cells of the fundamental SAM and RAM meristems of the embryonic axis. A strong immunoreactive signal at the plasma membrane and intercellular spaces of root apical meristem cells is consistent with apoplastic L-DOPA accumulation and suggests potential export to extracellular compartments. Complementarily, we analyzed the expression of the three polyphenol oxidase genes (MpPPO1, MpPPO2, and MpPPO3) by semi-quantitative RT-PCR in germinating tissues (shoot apex, primary root, and radicle) grown under light (LG) and dark (DG) conditions, alongside L-DOPA quantification by TLC. MpPPO1 was the only isoform expressed in aerial tissues (shoot apex), whereas all three isoforms were detected in radicle and root tissues, with MpPPO3 showing the highest transcript levels in roots. L-DOPA content, as estimated by TLC densitometry, showed a positive, though not statistically significant, association with MpPPO1 expression across tissues (Spearman ρ = 0.77, p = 0.10, n = 6 tissue-condition pairs). Taken together, these results may support a model in which L-DOPA stored in embryonic tissues serves as a pre-formed deterrent. At the same time, active biosynthesis via MpPPO genes in germinating roots may contribute to allelopathic exudation during early seedling establishment.

11.0 Synthetic activation of gibberellin signalling reveals spatial coordination of root growth.
Yagami Y, Yamada R, Ishikawa Y … Nakamura M.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesNature plants · 2026-10-22
matched: signalling, receptor*, gibberellin*, GA, hormone*, cell division, root, root growth, shoot*, epiderm*, endoderm*, growth
abstract

Gibberellins (GAs) influence cell division and elongation, profoundly shaping plant architecture and yield. GA perception occurs when bioactive GAs bind the receptor GID1, promoting DELLA degradation and activating transcriptional programs. While GA signalling in the root endodermis is essential for promoting root elongation, the contributions of other layers have remained unclear. Here we developed a synthetic GA (sGA) that exhibits little or no binding to endogenous GID1, together with a modified GID1 (mGID1) engineered to selectively recognize sGA, enabling cell-specific activation of GA signalling in vivo. Using this system in Arabidopsis, we demonstrate that coordinated action of GA signalling in the endodermis and epidermis is required for full root elongation. Moreover, the distal spatial distribution of GA signalling and GA biosynthetic enzymes supports a model in which bioactive GAs are transported from GA3ox-expressing tissues to the endodermis and epidermis to coordinate root elongation. The sGA-mGID1 system provides a versatile platform for spatially precise reprogramming of hormone signalling, enabling synthetic control of developmental processes such as root-shoot growth balance, thereby advancing applications in plant synthetic biology and sustainable crop improvement.

10.5 Integrated transcriptomic and functional trait responses reveal ecological strategy-associated biomass accumulation and allocation under drought in mulberry seedlings.
Zhang C, Yang S, Cisse EM … Xu X.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityTree physiology · 2026-10-23
matched: signaling, ABA, defense, drought, root, root growth, leaf, seed*, growth, woody
abstract

Drought alters both plant growth and biomass accumulation and allocation, but how molecular responses and functional traits jointly reflect these changes in woody plants remains insufficiently understood. We investigated whether plant ecological strategies, quantified using the competitive-stress tolerant-ruderal (CSR) framework, could provide an integrative description of drought-associated biomass responses in mulberry (Morus atropurpurea) seedlings. In this study, mulberry seedlings were maintained under well-watered, mild-drought, or moderate-drought conditions for 30 days, after which biomass accumulation and allocation, leaf and root functional traits, physiological and biochemical responses, and leaf transcriptomes were assessed. Transcriptomic and functional trait analyses revealed coordinated tradeoffs between growth-related processes and stress-defense responses. Total biomass was positively associated with photosynthetic performance, leaf and root growth traits, and genes related to light harvesting and carbon metabolism, whereas root-to-aboveground biomass ratio was positively associated with osmotic adjustment, antioxidant defense, ABA-related signaling pathways, and conservative root traits. The CSR scores revealed a corresponding shift from a predominantly competitive profile under well-watered conditions toward greater stress tolerance under moderate drought. Competitive scores covaried with resource-acquisitive traits and total biomass, whereas stress-tolerant scores covaried with cellular defense and increased relative belowground allocation. This result indicated that the shift in mulberry ecological strategies under drought stress represents an integrative adaptive syndrome that aligns with coordinated changes in biomass accumulation and allocation. The enhancement of competitive strategy is associated with greater aboveground growth and biomass accumulation, while the enhancement of stress-tolerant strategy corresponds to reduced overall growth and increased underground biomass allocation-patterns that collectively shape the balance between drought resistance and growth productivity in mulberry.

10.0 Genome-wide identification of UDP-glycosyltransferase gene family in Dendrobium huoshanense and functional characterization of DhUGT44 mediating flavonoid 7-O-glycosylation and cold tolerance.
Liu S, Ren J, Liu M … Yi S.
HormonesAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-30
matched: phytohormone*, cold stress, stress respon*, plasma membrane, chloroplast*, root, primary root*, root growth, leaves, growth, development, dendro*
abstract

UDP-glycosyltransferases (UGTs) play essential roles in glycosylating plant specialized metabolites, thereby modulating their stability, solubility, and bioactivity to regulate growth, development, and environmental adaptation. Dendrobium huoshanense, a rare and endangered medicinal orchid endemic to China, is renowned for its rich bioactive glycosides, including polysaccharides, flavonoids, and alkaloids, which underpin its high medicinal value. However, systematic genome-wide identification and evolutionary analysis of the UGT gene family in Dendrobium remain limited, hindering their application in stress tolerance and quality improvement. In this study, we performed the first comprehensive genome-wide identification and functional characterization of the UGT family in D. huoshanense by integrating bioinformatics analyses with molecular experiments. A total of 71 DhUGT genes were identified and phylogenetically classified into 12 subfamilies. These genes are unevenly distributed across chromosomes, with tandem duplications as the primary driver of family expansion. Cis-regulatory element analysis showed that most DhUGT promoters are enriched with phytohormone- and abiotic stress-responsive motifs. By integrating multi-omics transcriptomic data, we identified DhUGT44 (subfamily L) as a high priority candidate, exhibiting consistently high expression across tissues, cultivation conditions, and growth stages. In vitro enzymatic assays and transient expression in Nicotiana benthamiana revealed that DhUGT44 specifically catalyzes 7-O-glycosylation of flavonoid substrates. Furthermore, heterologous overexpression of DhUGT44 in Arabidopsis thaliana significantly enhanced primary root growth under cold stress compared to wild-type plants, indicating its positive role in cold tolerance. This study provides the first systematic insight into the evolutionary dynamics and transcriptional regulation of the UGT family in D. huoshanense, while elucidating the flavonoid glycosylation function and cold tolerance regulatory role of DhUGT44. These findings offer valuable genetic resources and a theoretical basis for molecular breeding and metabolic engineering of stress tolerance and bioactive compounds in Dendrobium.

matched: kinase*, signaling, auxin*, hormone*, stem, stoma*, meristem*, stem cell*, development, differentiation
abstract

MUTE functions as a pivotal transcription factor that governs the transition from self-renewal to differentiation in meristemoids during stomatal development. This transition requires MUTE expression to reach a critical threshold, yet the mechanisms that maintain its expression homeostasis remain unclear. Here, we show that the Mediator CDK8 kinase module (CKM) acts as a transcriptional coactivator of the MUTE-ICE1/SCRM2 complex to regulate meristemoid fate. Specifically, CKM cooperates with MUTE-ICE1/SCRM2 to promote the transcription of the auxin-responsive genes, such as SAUR52 and SAUR53, thereby enhancing local auxin response in meristemoids. AUXIN RESPONSE FACTORs (ARFs), including ARF6, ARF7, and ARF19, then bind to MUTE promoter and contribute to its transcription, establishing a positive feedback loop. abstract

Plant embryogenesis converts a zygote into a patterned, maturation-competent embryo. Since the major syntheses published in 2019 and 2021, allele-resolved transcriptomics, single-cell and spatial profiling, and three-dimensional imaging have revealed substantial variation in zygotic genome activation and cleavage geometry across angiosperms, challenging models derived solely from Arabidopsis. Here, we organize recent advances along a comparative zygote-to-seed trajectory and ask how conserved regulatory modules are redeployed across lineages, how early patterning is coupled to seed maturation, and which developmental transitions can be engineered in crops. We first compare the maternal-to-zygotic transition and associated chromatin-state changes, explicitly distinguishing observations from hypotheses about their evolutionary drivers. 10.0 Single-nucleus transcriptomic profiling reveals cell type-specific responses of the soybean root tip to aluminum stress.
Liu Q, Wang S, Yan R … Wei Y.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesMethodsJournal of hazardous materials · 2026-09-08
matched: defense, cell wall*, nucleus, nuclear, cell division, root, roots, root tip*, epiderm*, microscop*
abstract

Aluminum (Al) toxicity resulting from soil acidification poses a major constraint on crop productivity. Although the root apex serves as the primary site for Al perception and adaptive remodeling, cell type-resolved responses to prolonged Al exposure remain poorly characterized. Here, we employed single-nucleus RNA sequencing (snRNA-seq) to generate a cell type-resolved transcriptomic atlas of the soybean (Glycine max) root tip under Al stress. We profiled a total of 79,201 high-quality nuclei and identified nine major root cell types. Our results reveal that Al stress elicits shared transcriptional responses across cell types while reshaping root cell type composition. Notably, the proportion of nuclei increased in the epidermal cell layer (from 34.16% to 70.41%). In contrast, a significant reduction in nuclear number was detected within the cortical cells (from 40.70% to 8.53%). Interestingly, microscopic examination further indicated a pronounced expansion of regions exhibiting patterns inconsistent with the decrease in cortical nuclei. Cell type-specific analyses identified a total of 20,544 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) and showed that epidermal cells primarily responded to Al stress by activating transcriptional programs associated with transport, cell wall reinforcement, and defense, accompanied by enhanced fatty acid biosynthesis and cuticle-related pathways. In contrast, cortical cells exhibited repression of cell cycle-related transcriptional programs, consistent with their reduced cell abundance and indicating that limitations in cell division are closely associated with cortical cell loss. Furthermore, untargeted metabolomic profiling supported an important role of lipid metabolism in the response to Al stress. Collectively, our study reveals cell type-specific response patterns of the soybean root tip to long-term Al stress at single-nucleus resolution and provides new insights into the cellular mechanisms underlying aluminum tolerance in crop roots.

9.0 DELLA proteins modulate responsive and buffered transcriptional programs in Arabidopsis and rice roots under water limitation
Wong, C., Alabadi … B.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressbioRxiv · 2026-10-25
matched: signaling, gibberellin*, osmotic stress, root, roots, primary root*, root growth, growth
abstract

Water limitation constrains plant growth and productivity, yet the molecular mechanisms regulating root responses remain incompletely understood. The DELLA repressors of gibberellin signaling are key growth regulators under stress, but their role in shaping transcriptional responses to water limitation, and its conservation, remain unclear. Here, we investigated DELLA function in roots of Arabidopsis thaliana and Oryza sativa under reduced water availability simulated by polyethylene glycol (PEG). Loss of DELLA function reduced the sensitivity of root growth to water limitation, affecting primary root elongation in A. thaliana and crown root formation in O. sativa. Transcriptomic profiling revealed extensive DELLA-dependent regulation, partitioned into two modes: (i) genes requiring DELLAs for appropriate responses to water limitation ('WT-responsive'), and (ii) genes whose responsiveness is detected predominantly in the absence of DELLAs ('buffered', an operational label for an expression pattern, not a demonstrated mechanism). These patterns suggest that DELLAs both enable specific responses and apparently constrain broader transcriptional responsiveness under osmotic stress. Although the requirement for DELLA function is conserved, the developmental outputs and downstream transcriptional programs are largely species-specific. Together, our results identify DELLAs as key modulators of root responses to water limitation, with a proposed dual role in shaping and limiting transcriptional dynamics.

9.0 Effects of Seedling Grade on Biomass Accumulation and Allometric Growth Across Developmental Stages in Pinus yunnanensis Franch. Seedlings.
Wang P, Wang H, Lu Z … Yang B.
Trees and woody plantsBiology · 2026-09-23
matched: root, roots, stem, stems, seed*, growth, development, Pinus, conifer*
abstract

Pinus yunnanensis Franch. holds significant ecological value as a native conifer endemic to the southwestern uplands of China. The annual P. yunnanensis seedlings were categorized into three quality classes: Grade I, Grade II, and Grade III in accordance with the national specifications for seedling quality. We measured root, stem, needle, aboveground, and individual biomass at six stages from 240 to 690 days. 8.5 Advances in the Functions and Molecular Mechanisms of SPL Transcription Factors in Plant Flower Development.
Wu Q, Pan X, Zhou L.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesTrees and woody plantsPlants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-29
matched: signaling, gibberellin*, cytokinin*, leaf, meristem*, flowering, development, morphogen*, woody
abstract

SQUAMOSA PROMOTER BINDING PROTEIN-LIKE (SPL) transcription factors constitute a plant-specific family defined by a conserved SBP DNA-binding domain. They recognize cis-elements containing the core GTAC motif and cooperate with various co-regulators to govern downstream gene expression, thereby participating in leaf morphogenesis, phase transition, fruit development, anthocyanin accumulation and abiotic stress adaptation. Notably, the abbreviation SPL is also used for the unrelated SPOROCYTELESS/NOZZLE (SPL/NZZ) protein, which belongs to a separate family lacking the SBP-box domain; these two families should not be confused. Flower development, a central reproductive program in angiosperms, encompasses the acquisition of flowering competence, the vegetative-to-reproductive transition, floral meristem specification, organ patterning, and gametophyte formation. This review summarizes structural characteristics and classification of SPL proteins, distinguishes miR156/miR157-targeted paralogs from non-targeted ones, and synthesizes current understanding of their molecular functions across successive floral stages. Acting as signaling hubs, SPLs integrate photoperiod, gibberellin-DELLA, age-dependent miR156/miR157, temperature, sugar and cytokinin signals to fine-tune flowering time. They drive the reproductive transition via direct activation of LFY, FUL and AP1, and later remodel inflorescence architecture and floral organ size by balancing cell proliferation and expansion. In addition, they maintain fertility by modulating gametogenesis. Based largely on findings from Arabidopsis thaliana, we construct an updated regulatory framework for SPL-dependent floral development, compare conserved and lineage-divergent functions across herbaceous and woody species, identify unresolved questions, and discuss potential applications for crop improvement.

8.5 Root-applied taurine reduces grain cadmium and improves rice yield by decreasing Cd transport and increasing Cd retention in roots.

Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressEnvironmental research · 2026-10-21
matched: signaling, jasmon*, cadmium, cell wall*, vacuol*, root, roots, xylem, shoot*, seed*, growth
abstract

Taurine (TAU) can improve plant stress tolerance, but its effect on cadmium (Cd) accumulation in rice remains poorly understood. Here, we compared seed soaking, root application, and foliar spraying of TAU using hydroponic and full-season pot experiments with rice (Oryza sativa L. cv. Kitaake). The results showed that root application of TAU was the optimal approach, which can significantly promote rice growth and reduce Cd accumulation. In the hydroponic experiment, 10 mg L-1 TAU increased shoot and root dry weights by 37.3% and 63.3%, respectively, and decreased shoot Cd concentration by 29.0%. In the pot experiment, root-applied TAU decreased brown-rice Cd concentration by 23.3% and increased grain yield by 14.7%. Root application of TAU reduced expression of OsNRAMP1, OsNRAMP5, OsIRT1, and OsIRT2 genes associated with Cd uptake and the OsHMA2 gene in charge of xylem loading by 29.7%, 11.4%, 48.8%, 11.1%, and 22.0%, respectively, whereas increased expression of the OsHMA3 gene involved in Cd vacuolar sequestration by 27.5%.
