8.5 Shoot and Root Metabolomes of the Xeric Plant Ajania tenuifolia React Differently to Arid Conditions in a Hot-Dry Valley.
Yuan Z, Yang F, Zhu L … Du B.
Ca2+Physiologia plantarum · 2026-09-01
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, root, roots, leaf, leaves, stoma*, shoot*
abstract

Drylands cover almost half of the earth and are highly vulnerable to projected climate change. Understanding how domestic plants react to these harsh conditions is critical for vegetation management. We investigated the leaf and root metabolomes of the xeric shrub Ajania tenuifolia, as well as its responses to hot and dry conditions. Although the leaf water content of plants in dry and hot habitat decreased significantly compared to plants grown at the more humid site, the levels of reactive oxygen species (ROS), total carbon (C), nitrogen (N), δ13C, as well as the C and N fractions, were largely conserved. These findings suggest that A. tenuifolia has maintained its oxidative homeostasis and carbon-water balance in the face of adverse conditions. The present study demonstrated that A. tenuifolia employs a whole-plant resilience strategy involving stomatal regulation, ROS scavenging, and osmotic protection to cope with stressful conditions. Our results also underscore the significance of N metabolism in stress resilience in hot and dry conditions, and offer valuable insights for vegetation management in arid and semi-arid areas under changing climate.

abstract

8.0 Transcriptome and metabolome analyses reveal the molecular mechanisms of Rhododendron pulchrum under cadmium stress.

abstract

Soil contamination by cadmium (Cd) poses a persistent threat to plant growth and ecosystem health. 7.5 A Simple and Highly Efficient Regeneration Protocol for Bougainvillea glabra: Achieving Callus Induction, Shoot Organogenesis, and Elongation on a Single Medium
Abolade O, Sang L, Lin X … Zhao H.
preprintPreprints.org · 2026-10-21
matched: leaf, stem, shoot*, hypocotyl*, flowering, seed*, embryo*, morphogen*, organogenesis, differentiation
abstract

Bougainvillea is among the most economically and horticulturally important flowering shrubs worldwide, yet its genetic improvement is severely constrained by low natural seed-set and frequent embryo abortion. Existing micropropagation protocols rely predominantly on nodal stem segments and thus bypass a totipotent callus phase, limiting their utility for molecular breeding and genetic manipulation. In this study, we systematically evaluated and compared the in vitro morphogenic competence of -young leaf versus seed-derived juvenile hypocotyl explants in B. glabra Choisy 'Light Violet'. Although -young leaf explants readily formed vigorous calli on medium containing 2.0 mg/L 6-benzylaminopurine (6-BA) and 0.2 mg/L 2,4-dichlorophenoxyacetic acid (2,4-D), they consistently failed to differentiate into adventitious buds across all tested conditions, indicating an intrinsic morphogenic recalcitrance. In sharp contrast, juvenile hypocotyl explants successfully overcame this developmental block, establishing a complete and highly efficient callus-mediated whole-plant regeneration system. Among six tested combinations of 6-BA, 2,4-D, and kinetin on Murashige and Skoog (MS) medium, the combination of 2.0 mg/L 6-BA and 0.2 mg/L 2,4-D uniquely induced compact, actively dividing callus that spontaneously initiated bud primordia within 25 days, achieving the highest adventitious bud differentiation rate of 90.4 ± 2.5%. Regenerated shoots rooted most effectively on half-strength MS (1/2 MS) medium supplemented with 1.25 mg/L 1-naphthaleneacetic acid (NAA) and 0.75 mg/L indole-3-butyric acid (IBA), reaching a rooting rate of 95.2 ± 0.7%. Crucially, the entire developmental sequence from hypocotyl callus induction and de novo bud organogenesis to shoot elongation was coordinated on a unified single medium (2.0 mg/L 6-BA + 0.2 mg/L 2,4-D), substantially streamlining the operational process and enhancing multiplication efficiency. This robust protocol provides a practical pipeline for high-efficiency mass propagation and establishes an indispensable cellular foundation for future genetic transformation and molecular breeding in Bougainvillea.

abstract

Abstract Agroforestry systems are widely recognized for enhancing agricultural productivity and ecosystem sustainability in the Himalayan region; however, successful tree–crop integration depends on the compatibility between component species. This study evaluated the allelopathic effects of aqueous leaf and bark extracts of Myrica esculenta Buch.-Ham. ex D. Don on seed germination, seedling growth, and biomass production of five traditional Himalayan crops ( Eleusine coracana , Setaria italica , Amaranthus caudatus , Vigna umbellata , and Panicum miliaceum ) under laboratory and nursery conditions. A completely randomized design with four treatments (control, 5%, 7%, and 9% aqueous extracts) and five replications was adopted. Germination, growth, and biomass parameters were analysed using one-way ANOVA followed by Duncan's Multiple Range Test (P ≤ 0.05), while Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Hierarchical Cluster Analysis (HCA) were employed to evaluate multivariate treatment responses. The results revealed significant concentration-dependent allelopathic effects, with leaf extracts exhibiting greater phytotoxicity than bark extracts. Under laboratory conditions, leaf extracts reduced germination by 32.91% in E. coracana , 18.34% in S. italica , and 11.18% in A. caudatus , whereas bark extracts caused maximum inhibition in A. caudatus (21.52%). In nursery experiments, E. coracana was the most susceptible species, showing mean germination inhibition of 19.87% and 38.68% under leaf and bark extracts, respectively. Radicle growth was generally more sensitive than plumule growth, while low extract concentrations occasionally stimulated seedling growth, indicating hormetic responses. PCA showed that the first two principal components explained 72.9% of the total variation (PC1 = 46.9%; PC2 = 26.0%), and HCA clearly distinguished control from extract-treated groups. Overall, M. esculenta exhibits significant allelopathic potential, particularly through its leaves, with crop responses varying among species. The findings provide valuable insights into tree–crop compatibility and support the selection of suitable crop combinations for sustainable agroforestry systems in the Garhwal Himalaya.

7.5 Genome-Wide Characterization of Phosphate Transporters Reveals Differential Regulation of Growth and Medicinal Metabolite Accumulation Under Phosphorus Deficiency in Ginkgo biloba.
Chen Y, Zhang C, Cheng F … Li W.
HormonesAbiotic stressTrees and woody plantsBiology · 2026-09-22
matched: hormone*, nutrient*, stress respon*, root, roots, root development, leaves, seed*, growth, development, tree
abstract

Ginkgo biloba L. (G. biloba) is an important medicinal tree species rich in bioactive flavonoids and terpene lactones. Phosphate transporters (PHTs) play essential roles in phosphate uptake, transport, and homeostasis; however, their characteristics and functions in G. biloba remain unclear. In this study, 21 non-redundant GbPHT genes were identified from the G. biloba genome and classified into five subfamilies, including PHT1, PHT2, PHT3, PHT4, and PHO, containing five, one, four, six, and five members, respectively. These findings provide new insights into phosphorus adaptation and nutrient regulation of medicinal quality in G. biloba.

abstract

Abstract Chemical biology research using chemicals has elucidated several mechanisms underlying the endogenous biosynthesis of indole-3-acetic acid (IAA) in plants. Previous studies have predominantly utilized Ag + concentrations in the range of several mg/L (tens of µM), the levels known to affect plant physiology. 7.0 A cellular view of seed development and germination.
He C, Auroux L, Lewsey M, Whelan J.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressThe Plant cell · 2026-10-01
matched: signaling, hormone*, nutrient*, polarity, seed*, germination, embryo*, development, single-cell, spatial transcriptom*
abstract

Seeds are fundamental to global food security, yet their encapsulated structure, dense storage compounds, and spatial heterogeneity have created a "hidden box" problem, often restricting research to bulk and tissue approaches that mask cell type-specific regulatory programs. Advances in single-cell and spatial transcriptomics now enable systematic dissection of seed biology at cellular resolution. This review provides a cellular view spanning the developmental continuum from fertilization to seedling establishment by synthesizing single-cell and spatial transcriptomic studies in Arabidopsis thaliana and major crops (Oryza sativa-rice, Zea mays-maize, Hordeum vulgare-barley, Glycine max-soybean, Triticum aestivum-wheat). These technologies reveal previously undetected cellular complexity and heterogeneity: distinct transcriptional subpopulations within morphologically uniform tissues, specialized endosperm microdomains mediating nutrient transfer and metabolic polarity, cell type-specific genomic imprinting patterns, and spatially segregated hormone signaling hubs controlling dormancy and germination. Integration of transcriptional profiling with chromatin accessibility and transcription factor binding data has enabled construction of cell type-specific gene regulatory networks for seeds, advancing from correlation to causal inference and identifying master regulators of developmental transitions. These approaches illuminate how early embryonic patterning establishes transcriptional identities, how metabolic zonation coordinates storage compound accumulation and mobilization, how chromatin states maintain dormancy and license germination competence, and how spatiotemporal coordination integrates hormone signaling with energy metabolism across tissues. Single-cell and spatial approaches are establishing seeds not as opaque structures but as assemblies of coordinated cellular programs amenable to systematic analysis and targeted manipulation for crop improvement.

7.0 An environmentally regulated dichotomy in cell lineages of the root.
Oliva M, Stuart T, Tang D … Lister R.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressNature · 2026-10-21
matched: signalling, abscisic acid, ABA, nutrient*, root, roots, growth, development, single-cell
abstract

Plant development is highly plastic, with growth and architecture continuously shaped by environmental conditions. Roots, for instance, adjust their development to local water, nutrient and soil conditions. Despite this plasticity, individual root cell types are generally viewed as largely homogeneous populations that progress along a single developmental trajectory1. Here we show that rather than progressing along a single developmental trajectory, most Arabidopsis root cell lineage trajectories exhibit a stereotyped bifid topology, as revealed by single-cell RNA sequencing and spatial transcript mapping. One branch is characterized by a transcriptional program that is shared across multiple cell types and enriched for genes associated with environmental responses, including abscisic acid (ABA) signalling. This state occurs reproducibly in a subset of cells across multiple cell types, independent of developmental lineage, thereby revealing an additional layer of root cell identity. It is conserved across Arabidopsis ecotypes and persists despite major perturbations in developmental identity. Moreover, it is reduced in ABA-deficient and ABA-insensitive mutants and becomes more prevalent under water-deficit conditions. Together, these findings reveal an additional layer of root cell identity that is superimposed on developmental lineage and its prevalence is modulated by environmental conditions. These results uncover a previously unrecognized source of environment-associated transcriptional heterogeneity shared across root cell types.

7.0 Conifer Needle Epidermal Imprints for Stomatal Size and Density Measurements.
Sachsenmaier L, Franz S, Kahl A … Wirth C.
Trees and woody plantsMethodsCurrent protocols · 2026-10-01
matched: stoma*, epiderm*, conifer*, microscop*
abstract

Stomata play a central role in regulating plant water loss and gas exchange, making functional trait data on stomatal density and size increasingly relevant in the context of climate change. Expanding comparative datasets for these traits requires simple, low-cost methods that can be applied consistently across species and environments. However, while simple stomatal imprint techniques using nail varnish and adhesive tape are widely established for angiosperms, their application to conifer needles is challenging. Stomata in conifers are typically sunken within epistomatal chambers and occluded by densely meshed wax tubules, which can prevent accurate replication of stomatal complexes in epidermal imprints and thereby limit reliable measurement of stomatal size. In addition, stomata are often heterogeneously distributed across needle surfaces and may occur on multiple surfaces depending on species, requiring tailored sampling approaches for accurate density estimation. Consequently, reliable measurement of stomatal traits in conifers would require chemical processing and advanced microscopy, making these methods time-intensive, expensive and less suitable for large-scale comparative studies. Therefore, we lack an approach for conifers that generates high-quality trait data while remaining fast, simple, and comparable to established angiosperm methods. Here, we present a straightforward protocol for preparing stomatal imprints in conifer needles based on standard methods for angiosperm species but including simple preprocessing steps to improve imprint consistency and quality. We further provide guidance for identifying, imaging, and quantifying stomata while accounting for their species-specific spatial distribution on needle surfaces. The protocol enables reliable measurements of stomatal size and density using basic laboratory equipment and standard light microscopy. We demonstrate its applicability across 12 northern hemisphere conifer species, highlighting its suitability for comparative trait-based studies. © 2026 The Author(s). Current Protocols published by Wiley Periodicals LLC. Support Protocol 1: Sampling conifer needles for stomatal traits Basic Protocol 1: Epidermal imprints using nail varnish Basic Protocol 2: Measurement of stomatal density and size.

7.0 Cyto-genotoxic potential and in silico analysis of emerging contaminants of the Ganges River water.
Akhtar S, Malik A.
HormonesCell biology and organellesMethodsEnvironmental monitoring and assessment · 2026-10-21
matched: hormone*, cell division, root, root tip*, seed*, germination, growth, confocal, microscop*
abstract

The Ganges River, vital for drinking water, agriculture, fisheries, and socio-religious practices, is increasingly threatened by contaminants, compromising its ecological and biological integrity. Present study was undertaken to identify contaminants in the river water using chemical profiling integrated with bacterial and plant bioassays, complemented by in silico analysis. GC-MS analysis of hexane and chloroform extracts revealed the presence of pharmaceuticals, phenolic substances, siloxanes, hormone-disrupting agents, and antibiotics, including spironolactone, nalmefene, desmethylverapamil, bisphenol B, aminopterin, and minocycline. Computational docking analysis and 100-ns molecular dynamics simulations revealed stable binding of selected contaminants to molecular targets involved in nucleotide biosynthesis, DNA replication, topological regulation, and cell division. These findings suggested that the bioactive compounds may induce genetic and cellular damage, contributing to the biological effects associated with contaminant mixtures in river water.

abstract

This study evaluated the ability of a conventional plant growth-promoting rhizobacterium, B. subtilisubtilis RC72, and an extremophilic P. poaeoae strain, applied individually or in combination, to improve wheat (Triticum aestivum L.) performance under polypropylene microplastic and polyethylene glycol-induced osmotic stress. 6.5 Gfa1-dependent hexosamine metabolism maintains cell-wall integrity and developmental fitness required for crop colonization in Aspergillus flavus.
Qin Q, Wang B, Fang W.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesCell surface (Amsterdam, Netherlands) · 2026-10-15
matched: chitin, cell wall*, seed*, growth, morphogen*, differentiation
abstract

The fungal cell wall is a dynamic structure whose composition and integrity are closely coupled to metabolism, morphogenesis, and environmental fitness. Glutamine fructose-6-phosphate amidotransferase (Gfa1), the committed enzyme of the hexosamine biosynthetic pathway, supplies precursors for UDP-N-acetylglucosamine production and cell-wall chitin synthesis. Although Gfa1 is essential for growth and virulence in Aspergillus fumigatus, its role in the cell-wall biology and developmental program of the aflatoxigenic fungus Aspergillus flavus remains unclear. Here, we characterized a gfa1 deletion mutant and a complemented strain of A. flavus. Loss of gfa1 caused glucosamine/N-acetylglucosamine auxotrophy and markedly impaired vegetative growth and conidiation. Despite amino-sugar supplementation, the mutant exhibited increased sensitivity to cell-wall perturbing agents and significant reductions in cell-wall chitin and glucan, indicating disrupted cell-wall homeostasis. Gfa1 deficiency also severely impaired sclerotial formation and increased sensitivity to several environmental stresses. Importantly, the mutant showed markedly reduced colonization of peanut and maize seeds and no detectable aflatoxin B1 accumulation under the conditions tested. Consistent with its broad fitness defects, Δgfa1 displayed strongly attenuated virulence in Galleria mellonella. These findings establish Gfa1-dependent hexosamine metabolism as an important determinant of cell-wall homeostasis and developmental competence in A. flavus and reveal a link between amino-sugar metabolism, fungal surface integrity, sclerotial differentiation, and crop-associated fitness.

6.5 Phloem-Limited Disease Impacts Citrus Carbohydrate Dynamics Distinctly from Source-Sink Manipulation
Alade, D. O., Gersony … C.
preprintTrees and woody plantsbioRxiv · 2026-10-20
matched: leaf, leaves, stem, stems, stoma*, phloem, growth, tree, trees
abstract

Source-sink dynamics are defined by the interdependence of source and sink tissues and are key determinants of plant growth and productivity. This dynamic can, be altered by disease. One example is huanglongbing (HLB), a phloem-limited disease that impacts several economically important tree species by disrupting carbon partitioning and altering source-sink relationships. Though HLB is widespread in citrus, the specific mechanism in which phloem disruption impacts the co-regulation of carbon dynamics from source to sink has not been fully described. To address this, we took advantage of a natural study under field conditions, in which infected and uninfected trees were growing in the same field. After identifying trees that were uninfected, infected with symptoms, or infected without symptoms, we manipulated source:sink ratio by girdling branches and defoliating at 0%, 50%, and 75% of the original leaf area per fruit, while leaving a control treatment with no defoliation or girdling and used fruits as the sink of interest. We measured gas exchange, biomass, and non-structural carbohydrates in leaves, stems and fruits in response to these treatments. Our results show that leaf photosynthetic responses to HLB were primarily driven by stomatal regulation rather than biochemical limitations. HLB increased leaf dark respiration without altering the relationship between respiration and leaf soluble sugars. Among the plant organs, stem starch reserves were the most responsive to source-sink manipulation and were strongly correlated with multiple carbon pools and biomass in both fruits and leaves. Although HLB altered fruit carbon dynamics, it did not change fruit responses to source-sink manipulation. Overall, HLB affects source and sink tissues independently of the experimental source-sink manipulation. The stem serves as a transient carbon reserve and is sensitive to both HLB and source-sink manipulation. These findings raise further questions regarding total carbon fluxes and the importance of feedback inhibition of photosynthesis.

6.5 Root rot suppression by synthetic microbiota is associated with rhizosphere bacterial shifts and amino acid metabolism
Zhu N, Zhao P, Liu X … Wang Y
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityJournal of Experimental Botany · 2026-10-26
matched: pathogen*, microbiota, root, roots, root growth, growth
abstract

Abstract Root rot caused by Fusarium oxysporum (F. oxysporum) severely constrains Codonopsis pilosula (C. pilosula) production. Indigenous culturable bacteria associated with C. pilosula were screened for antifungal activity, plant growth-promoting traits, and interstrain compatibility, and two defined three-member synthetic microbial communities, SCI and SCII, were constructed. Both SynComs alleviated root rot, with SCI showing stronger overall performance and a biocontrol efficacy of 57.8%, together with improved root growth and photosynthetic performance. Rhizosphere bacterial profiling was integrated with rhizosphere metabolite profiling and root tissue metabolomics. SynCom treatment was associated with limited changes in overall bacterial diversity but variation in specific rhizosphere taxa. In roots, pathway-enrichment patterns shifted from sulfur metabolism and oxidative stress-related processes under pathogen stress toward amino acid-related metabolism after SynCom treatment, accompanied by treatment-associated variation in medicinally relevant metabolite features. Associations between metabolic changes and potentially beneficial bacteria indicated coordinated variation between host metabolism and rhizosphere bacterial composition. In vitro assays further showed that L-valine elicited chemotactic and growth responses in a representative Sphingomonas isolate. Collectively, SynCom-mediated root rot suppression was associated with constituent-strain antagonism, plant recovery, rhizosphere bacterial shifts, and amino acid-related metabolic variation.

6.0 Cyanide Metabolism in Apple Embryos During Dormancy Breakage Is Modulated by NO or HCN Application.
Piekarniak MA, Ciacka K, Gniazdowska A, Krasuska U.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressBiology · 2026-10-01
matched: signalling, pathogen*, defence, stress respon*, mitochondri*, seed*, germination, embryo*, development
abstract

Hydrogen cyanide (HCN) is an N-containing gasotransmitter with a regulatory function in plants and animals. Despite its toxicity, HCN acts as a signalling molecule in plants, influencing development, pathogen defence, and stress responses, with particular emphasis on biotic stress and seed dormancy/germination. Around 3000 species across > 130 families produce HCN from cyanogenic compounds. This study investigated how nitric oxide (NO) and HCN impact the activity of enzymes involved in HCN metabolism during dormancy breakage of apple (Malus domestica Borkh.) embryos. Based on earlier findings that HCN exposure elevates NO emission and alters the phytoglobin-NO cycle, we examined whether NO affects HCN metabolism. We measured the activities of β-glucosidases, β-cyanoalanine synthase (β-CAS), and sulfurtransferases (STRs) (HCN-detoxifying enzymes), and the transcript levels of their corresponding gene homologues. Both NO and HCN stimulated β-glucosidase activity and enhanced β-CAS and STR activities. Although both N-containing gasotransmitters increased β-CAS transcripts, they differentially implicated transcript levels of STR homologues. Additionally, only HCN increased the transcript levels of mitochondrial cysteine synthase (OASTL-C). These results, together with our previous work, potentially link NO and HCN in the metabolic network during dormancy breakage of apple embryos.

6.0 Embryonal degreening and seed longevity require plastid LEA proteins.
Brandt D, Vilchez AC, Eirich J … Schwarzländer M.
Cell biology and organellesMethodsThe New phytologist · 2026-10-23 · also: New Phytologist
matched: chloroplast*, mitochondri*, seed*, germination, embryo*, microscop*
abstract

Seeds enable plant embryos to endure extended periods and environmental extremes. Orthodox seeds acquire desiccation tolerance during maturation, before metabolic quiescence. Coordinated thylakoid disassembly and Chl degradation lead to embryo degreening, a hallmark of orthodox seed maturation. While degreening is hormonally controlled, our understanding of the molecular players involved and its physiological significance remains incomplete. By serendipity, we observed a stay-green seed phenotype in an Arabidopsis thaliana mutant lacking two LATE EMBRYOGENESIS ABUNDANT (LEA) proteins, LEA42 and LEA48, which we identified as the most abundant LEA proteins in seed mitochondria and plastids. Using lea48xlea42 as a model for impaired LEA function in seed mitochondria and plastids, proteomic, transcriptomic and transmission electron microscopy analyses indicate incomplete thylakoid degradation. Freshly harvested lea48xlea42 seeds were unaffected in germination but were impaired in germination after high-temperature/high-humidity ageing. Genetic complementation with specifically targeted LEA expression in either mitochondria or chloroplasts revealed that seed longevity support is associated with plastidial, but not mitochondrial, LEA. Light exposure of mature seeds ('light storage') reduced longevity, providing a physiological framework for embryo degreening. Our data identify a role for LEA proteins in remodelling seed plastids before maturation drying and highlight LEA-dependent embryo degreening as a decisive contributor to seed longevity.

6.0 Identification of a stable major locus and candidate genes for drought tolerance during wheat germination.
Liu J, Wang D, Zhou Q … Ma J.
HormonesAbiotic stressTrees and woody plantsTAG. Theoretical and applied genetics. Theoretische und angewandte Genetik · 2026-10-25
matched: jasmon*, JA, drought, stress respon*, root, shoot*, seed*, germination, growth
abstract

We identified QRSH.scwl-4B as a pleiotropic QTL for basal shoot growth and drought-stress response at the wheat germination stage, predicted its putative candidate genes, and confirmed it as a promising locus for drought-tolerance and limited yield loss. Drought stress during germination severely reduces the wheat germination rate and seedling establishment, leading to significant yield losses. Understanding the genetic control of this trait is important for breeding drought-tolerant and high-yielding wheat varieties. Here, we detected 21 quantitative trait loci (QTLs) for maximum root length (MRL), root number (RN) and shoot height (SH) under control (CK) and drought treatment (DT) at the germination stage using a recombinant inbred line (RIL) population. This suggests that QRSH.scwl-4B is a promising locus for improving wheat drought tolerance, with potentially minimal negative impact on yield. Collectively, these findings provide a foundation for cloning QRSH.scwl-4B and for improving drought tolerance in wheat through molecular breeding.

6.0 β-Carbonic anhydrase SlβCA4 is required for normal growth, carbon assimilation and stomatal plasticity in tomato
Meyers, O., Stern Tal … I.
preprintCell biology and organellesMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-21
matched: chloroplast*, root, leaf, stoma*, shoot*, growth, CRISPR
abstract

β-Carbonic anhydrases (βCAs) catalyze the reversible interconversion of CO 2 and bicarbonate and contribute to plant carbon metabolism and environmental responses. However, the physiological functions of individual βCA isoforms in crops remain poorly understood. Here, we investigated two chloroplast-localized βCAs, SlβCA1 and SlβCA4, in tomato ( Solanum lycopersicum ). We combined subcellular localization, CRISPR/Cas9 mutagenesis, grafting, leaf gas-exchange measurements, and controlled environmental treatments to examine their roles in growth, photosynthesis, and stomatal developmental plasticity. Both proteins localized to chloroplasts but their disruption resulted in markedly different phenotypes. While SlβCA1 mutants showed largely normal growth and photosynthesis, SlβCA4 mutants exhibited severe root and shoot growth defects. Grafting mutant shoots onto wild-type rootstocks partially restored vegetative growth, indicating that impaired root function contributes to the growth defects. SlβCA4 mutants also showed reduced CO 2 assimilation and stomatal conductance, while electron transport remained largely unaffected. Elevated CO 2 partially improved leaflet growth but did not restore plant height or root elongation. Furthermore, unlike wild-type plants, SlβCA4 mutants failed to increase stomatal index in response to elevated temperature. These findings reveal distinct physiological roles for chloroplast-localized βCAs in tomato and identify SlβCA4 as an important contributor to plant growth, photosynthesis, and temperature-dependent stomatal developmental plasticity.

17.0 MYB156 promotes root sucker formation by repressing IDD14 and CKX1 to elevate cytokinin levels in Populus.
Pang H, Li B, Chen Y … Zheng L.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthThe New phytologist · 2026-10-21 · also: New Phytologist
matched: signaling, cytokinin*, root, roots, cambium, meristem*, growth, development, poplar*, Populus, secondary growth, live imaging
abstract

Root suckers enable clonal propagation of rooting-recalcitrant poplars, conferring rejuvenated traits such as enhanced survival and accelerated growth. Despite widespread clonal propagation and ecological restoration, the essential genes controlling sucker development remain unidentified. MYB156 is a positive regulator of root sucker formation in white poplar (Populus davidiana × P. bolleana). MYB156 is predominantly expressed in proliferation-competent root cells, including vascular cambium and phellogen. Gain- and loss-of-function analyses demonstrated that MYB156 overexpression enhanced root suckering and secondary growth, whereas myb156 mutants were severely compromised in both. Meanwhile, cytokinin levels increased two- to fivefold in roots with MYB156 overexpression but were significantly reduced in myb156 mutant roots. MYB156 directly represses IDD14 and CKX1, reducing cytokinin catabolism and alleviating IDD14-mediated suppression of cytokinin accumulation; IDD14 RNAi lines similarly showed elevated cytokinin and enhanced suckering. Cytokinin response elements in the MYB156 promoter and MYB156 autoactivation establish a cytokinin-MYB156 positive feedback loop amplifying cytokinin signaling in meristematic zones. Live imaging with the cytokinin response reporter TCSn:RFP demonstrated that suckering in MYB156-overexpressing plants shifted from the phellogen to the vascular cambium. These findings establish a MYB156-CKX1/IDD14-cytokinin module that creates a permissive context for suckering, providing a transcriptional blueprint for clonal propagation.

17.0 Woody root and shoot xylem anatomy reveal patterns of whole-plant axial variation and suggest structure-function tradeoffs.
Jacobsen AL, Rodriguez-Zaccaro FD, Percolla MI … Pratt RB.
GrowthTree physiology · 2026-10-23
matched: root, roots, root tip*, xylem, shoot*, Populus, tree, trees, woody, hydraulic*, embolism
abstract

Plants must balance multiple functions across their large woody bodies, including water transport, biomechanical support, and storage. While vessel diameters within woody shoots have been intensively studied, woody roots compose a substantial portion of the plant body and remain comparatively understudied. Moreover, little is known about axial variation in xylem fibers and parenchyma. We excavated root systems of six Populus balsamifera L. spp. trichocarpa trees and examined xylem traits at 1-m increments from root tip to shoot tip. At each sampled location we measured several anatomical and functional traits. Anatomical traits included the percentage area of vessels, fibers, secondary wall area, and parenchyma; fiber diameter and wall thickness; vessel element diameter, wall thickness, and length; and inter-vessel pit area and density. Hydraulic functional traits included hydraulic conductivity and embolism resistance. Vessel diameter increased from organ apices toward the root-shoot junction in both roots and shoots, reaching maximum values in older root tissues. Model comparison strongly favored piecewise regressions over a single power-law relationship reflecting the inability of a single global scaling relationship to capture the pronounced structural transitions between root and shoot tissues. Axial trends for other traits differed between roots and shoots, resulting in unique combinations of vessel and non-vessel traits at each position along the tree axis. Across the plant axis, roots exhibited higher vessel and parenchyma fractions and lower fiber fractions than shoots, whereas shoots showed greater secondary wall investment. Structural and functional traits were highly correlated with one another, with roots generally showing higher hydraulic conductivity and greater embolism vulnerability relative to shoots. Together, these results show that whole-plant xylem structure is highly coordinated yet strongly divergent across different regions of the large woody plant body. Whole-plant xylem structure and function, especially that of woody roots, is essential to our understanding of whole-plant structure and function.

16.0 Comparison of whole-tree water transport and storage coordination in four tree species of contrasting hydraulic strategies.
Tucker LM, Reinhardt K, Johnson DM … Rojas J.
GrowthTree physiology · 2026-10-21
matched: xylem, Populus, aspen, oak, Quercus, pine, Pinus, tree, hydraulic*, sap flow
abstract

Our knowledge about coordination of water transport at the whole-tree scale has been limited in its consideration of water storage and release from different tissues (i.e., sapwood and heartwood) along the xylem pathway. Here, we measured water transport and storage at spatially and temporally intensive scales in four tree species of contrasting hydraulic strategies (loblolly pine (Pinus taeda), southern red oak (Quercus falcata), Douglas-fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii), and trembling aspen (Populus tremuloides)) over the growing season in North Carolina (mesic) and Idaho (semi-arid), USA. We quantified the coordination of sap flow and volumetric water content at different heights along the main tree trunk and xylem tissue depths. Further, we investigated seasonal patterns of daily water storage and use in the different systems. Coordinated lags in sap flow between upper and lower heights along the trunk were present in all study species except oak. Whole-tree coordination of water transport and storage was more evident in the mesic system than in the semi-arid system. The angiosperm species displayed greater water storage use than the gymnosperm species in both systems, with water storage use patterns related to rain events in the mesic system and related to soil-moisture dry down in the semi-arid system. Our findings emphasize the importance of understanding water transport and storage at species- and ecosystem-specific levels inferred from spatially and temporally intensive tree measurements.

16.0 The transcription factor PdePHR1 increases phosphorus accumulation in poplar shoot and promotes root growth.

matched: signaling, nutrient*, root, roots, root growth, shoot*, growth, poplar*, Populus, tree, trees, woody
abstract

BackgroundTrees are major primary producers and long-term carbon sink by yielding a substantial amount of woody biomass, which relies on sufficient acquisition of mineral nutrient from soil by roots. Phosphate (Pi) is an essential, but often limited in soils, macronutrient. To sustain growth during Pi starvation, plants trigger an array of adaptive responses that are mainly controlled by the transcription factor of the PHOSPHATE RESPONSE 1 (PHR1) family. 14.5 Characterization of the sexual reproductive process in the perennial tree Robinia pseudoacacia.
Lin H, Wang XJ, Sun K … Meng JG.
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthForestry research · 2026-09-08
matched: signaling, RALF*, plasma membrane, cytoskelet*, actin, seed*, germination, pollen tube*, growth, Robinia, tree, woody
abstract

Sexual reproduction in perennial plants, especially woody species, remains poorly understood, and most studies have focused on annuals such as Arabidopsis thaliana. This gap hinders understanding of perennial reproductive processes and limits tree breeding. Here we characterized the sexual reproductive process of Robinia pseudoacacia, a perennial leguminous tree, and established standardized methods for studying its reproduction. We optimized cytological assays for pollen viability, in vitro germination, and pollen tube dynamics, achieving over 90% pollen viability and germination efficiency within 8 h. R. pseudoacacia pollen exhibited reproductive thermotolerance and maintained over 20% germination at 37 °C, a temperature lethal to A. thaliana pollen. This thermotolerance was associated with maintenance of stable plasma membrane integrity and an organized F-actin cytoskeleton. R. pseudoacacia pollen also germinated on A. thaliana stigmas, penetrated the styles, and targeted 3.6% of A. thaliana ovules, although post-zygotic incompatibility prevented seed formation. The ANX-RALF signaling module is conserved in R. pseudoacacia, and antisense oligodeoxynucleotide-mediated suppression of RpANX2 disrupted pollen tube growth. Moreover, aberrant pollen tube guidance was linked to its low seed set. These findings provide a systematic characterization of sexual reproduction in a perennial tree and establish methodological foundations for reproductive studies in woody perennials, with implications for tree breeding.

14.5 Dynamic transcriptomic profiling of Salix triandra under freezing stress identifies StWRKY31 as a positive regulator of cold tolerance.
Jia C, Chen Y, Yin T.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-13
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, defense, freezing, nucleus, seed*, willow*, Salix, woody
abstract

Salix triandra is a fast-growing shrub willow native to temperate and cold regions, possessing remarkable freezing tolerance. However, the molecular mechanisms underlying this natural cold hardiness remain largely unexplored. In this study, we performed a time-course transcriptomic analysis of S. triandra seedlings subjected to freezing stress at -4 °C. A total of 11,287 differentially expressed genes were identified and clustered into six distinct co-expression modules with dynamic temporal patterns, revealing a multi-phasic transcriptional response involving early stress perception, metabolic adjustment, and sustained cellular protection. Among the differentially expressed transcription factors, a WRKY family member designated StWRKY31 (EVM0008367) was identified as a cold-induced candidate gene, belonging to a mid-to-late adaptation module peaking at 24 h. Subcellular localization analysis showed that StWRKY31 localizes to the nucleus. Heterologous overexpression of StWRKY31 in Arabidopsis significantly enhanced freezing tolerance, as evidenced by improved survival rates under both non-acclimated and cold-acclimated freezing assays. In the StWRKY31-overexpressing Arabidopsis lines, reactive oxygen species (ROS) accumulation was reduced, superoxide dismutase (SOD) and peroxidase (POD) activities were increased, proline content was elevated, and malondialdehyde (MDA) levels were decreased. Moreover, StWRKY31 upregulated the expression of cold-responsive marker genes (AtGSTF8, AtZAT10, and AtHY5) in a time-dependent manner, with elevated basal expression prior to cold exposure, suggesting a potential defense priming state. These findings suggest that StWRKY31 functions as a positive regulator of freezing tolerance. This study contributes to understanding the regulatory dynamics of cold adaptation in woody plants and provides a valuable candidate gene for breeding cold-tolerant varieties.

abstract

14.0 The point of disconnection: early-drought fine root shrinkage is driven by plant water use in Pinus radiata.
Harrison Day BL, Zhou Y, Tonet V … Choat B.
Abiotic stressGrowthJournal of experimental botany · 2026-10-24
matched: drought, root, roots, stoma*, xylem, Pinus, conifer*, hydraulic*, embolism
abstract

Understanding the root-soil interface is essential for determining when plants become hydraulically isolated from their soil environment during drought. Emerging evidence suggests roots shrink substantially during dehydration, but the drivers and water-flow implications remain elusive, with few data linking shrinkage across soil and plant water status. Using Pinus radiata, a widespread isohydric conifer, we use synchrotron-based Micro-CT to scan intact roots < 2.5 mm in soil during mild plant drying (prior to embolism) to isolate relationships between shrinkage, soil (Ψsoil) and xylem (Ψplant) water potential. Fine roots in situ began shrinking during the earliest stages of Ψplant drying (-0.2 MPa to -0.88 MPa), losing 14.8 ± 10.3% initial transverse-sectional area (62% of observed shrinkage) prior to incipient soil drying (0-72 kPa Ψsoil). Ψplant was a significantly better predictor of shrinkage than Ψsoil, showing a linear shrinkage-Ψplant relationship across this early drying gradient when normalised to shrinkage at -1 MPa, accounting for variable initial Ψplant. Ψplant decline was linearly coordinated with cortical lacunae formation. This Ψplant-driven pattern identifies root shrinkage as an essential component of plant hydraulic function during commonly experienced water potential gradients, occurring long prior to stomatal closure and xylem embolism. This reveals water use from cortical tissues as a key physiological process defining plant-soil disconnection during water deficit.

The Nucleotide-Binding Domain Leucine-Rich Repeat (NLR) gene family is a central component of plant immune systems, yet its diversity and evolutionary dynamics remain poorly characterized in long-lived tree species. Here, we performed a genome-wide analysis of the NLR gene repertoire in Quercus suber (cork oak) using a newly developed annotation tool-InterNLR-which identifies and classifies NLRs as canonical and non-canonical. 12.5 Physiological and Molecular Mechanisms of Tree Responses to Combined Drought and Low-Nitrogen Stress: A Review.
Xu Y, Yang Z, Yu L … Wei M.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionHormonesInternational journal of molecular sciences · 2026-10-04
matched: reactive oxygen species, signal transduction, signaling, abscisic acid, drought, root, tree, trees, hydraulic*
abstract

Drought and low nitrogen often co-occur in forests, yet their combined effects cannot be inferred reliably from single-stress studies. This review synthesizes physiological and molecular evidence for tree responses to combined drought and low nitrogen at four levels, namely stress perception, signal transduction, metabolic reprogramming, and adaptive phenotypes. It compares single-stress baselines and evaluates reported interactions in photosynthesis, water-use efficiency, root traits, and carbon-nitrogen allocation. The review distinguishes direct tree evidence from mechanistic context derived from crops and model plants. Abscisic acid-nitrogen signaling crosstalk is considered alongside reactive oxygen species, energy, hydraulics, and transcriptional regulation as candidate integration routes. Candidate genes are classified by species context and experimental evidence; responsiveness to the two single stresses is not interpreted as proof of a combined-stress function. The review also assesses the opportunities and current limitations of multi-omics and genome editing for trees. It identifies priorities for factorial experiments, multi-year validation, and molecular breeding for climate-resilient forestry.

12.0 Manipulation of the PtWOX11-PagKNAT2/6b Module Bidirectionally Regulates Petiole Traits in Populus.
Liu B, Li J, Yan X … Wang X.

matched: cell expansion, leaf, leaves, meristem*, flowering, morphogen*, differentiation, poplar*, Populus, trees, woody
abstract

Canopy architecture is a critical determinant of productivity in crops and trees, with leaf positioning being a key factor. Here, we show that a transcriptional module in poplar enables bidirectional control over petiole length and angle. 11.5 Contrasting resistance and recovery of co-occurring conifers to larch caterpillar defoliation in a Northeast China temperate forest.
Wang X, Jia X, Yan X … Yu D.
Abiotic stressGrowthPlant biology (Stuttgart, Germany) · 2026-10-21
matched: drought, nutrient*, growth, pine, spruce, conifer*, tree, trees, dendro*
abstract

Climate change has been linked to the intensification of forest pest outbreaks, posing a serious threat to non-coevolved tree species because of their potentially weak defensive capabilities. However, unlike broad-scale climatic disturbances such as drought and heatwaves, insect outbreaks are highly heterogeneous in space and among host species, and the response of occurring conifers to such outbreaks remains poorly understood. The core area of the Changbai Mountain Nature Reserve in northeastern China was severely defoliated in 2019 by larch caterpillars (Dendrolimus superans Butler)-a conifer-feeding defoliator that prefers larch but also attacks other conifers. In this region, a vegetation transition zone between the broadleaf-Korean pine and spruce-fir forests hosts multiple conifer species co-existing within short distances under similar defoliation intensities. We monitored 1,837 trees in 2019 (pre-disturbance), 2021 and 2024, and sampled needles of healthy individuals of each species in 2024 to obtain species-level proxies of intrinsic foliar traits. Results revealed that: (i) mortality of attacked Korean pine peaked within 2 years, with little subsequent increase, resulting in a cumulative mortality rate of 31.3%; in contrast, mortality of spruce and fir continued to increase until the fifth year, reaching a combined cumulative rate of 78.6%, while larch exhibited nearly 100% survival. (ii) a deciduous habit, low needle biomass allocation and low carbohydrate concentrations enhanced survival by reducing canopy regeneration costs. (iii) regarding radial growth responses, spruce showed stronger compensatory growth but weaker recovery ability than fir and Korean pine, likely due to its limited carbon and nutrient reserves. Considering these findings, prioritizing the protection of non-coevolved conifers is recommended to minimize biomass loss during pest outbreaks. This study also provides important insights for temperate and boreal conifer forest conservation, highlighting the high vulnerability of spruce-fir forests to larch caterpillar outbreaks.

11.0 ERF Transcription Factor PagERF135A Negatively Regulates Drought Tolerance in Poplar by Modulating Sucrose Metabolism and Brassinosteroid Biosynthesis.
Qi S, Yang M, Zhang R … Zhang H.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPhysiologia plantarum · 2026-09-01
matched: signaling, brassinosteroid*, drought, nuclear, growth, poplar*, Populus
abstract

Drought is one of the primary environmental constraints limiting poplar growth. Identifying key drought-resistance genes and their regulatory mechanisms is crucial for genetic improvement of drought tolerance in poplar. Time-course transcriptome analysis of 84K poplar (Populus alba × P. glandulosa) under drought stress revealed that the ERF family is among the most responsive transcription factor families, and PagERF135A exhibited the most significant upregulation, suggesting a key regulatory role. Genome-wide identification of the ERF family in 84K poplar identified 276 members, with expansion primarily through segmental duplication, and purifying selection dominated their evolution. PagERF135A is a nuclear-localized transcription factor with transcriptional activation activity. Overexpression of PagERF135A reduced drought tolerance in transgenic poplars, manifested by aggravated oxidative damage, diminished osmotic adjustments, and impaired PSII and PSI activities. Integrated transcriptomic and WGCNA analyses showed that PagERF135A overexpression suppressed drought-induced expression of soluble sugar synthesis genes in starch-sucrose metabolism, down-regulated brassinosteroid biosynthesis and signaling genes, and promoted catabolic genes. Thus, PagERF135A negatively regulates drought tolerance by disrupting carbon metabolic flow and brassinosteroid signaling homeostasis. Our findings provide new insights into ERF-mediated drought response mechanisms in poplar.

11.0 Foliar carbon dots improve drought tolerance in soybean through genotype-dependent hydraulic and metabolic pathways.
Su X, Li H, Zhang Y … Zhang B.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-16
matched: abscisic acid, ABA, defense, drought, leaf, stoma*, seed*, growth, hydraulic*
abstract

Although foliar application of carbon dots (CDs) has emerged as a promising strategy to alleviate drought, whether CDs act through hydraulic or biochemical pathways, and whether their effects are genotype-dependent, remains unclear. Here, we investigated the effects of foliar CDs application (50 mg L-1 CDs) at the podding and seed-filling stages on photosynthetic performance, hydraulic property, abscisic acid (ABA) and indole-3-acetic acid contents, oxidative stress, antioxidant defense, and osmotic adjustment substances in wild (Y15) and cultivated (Zhonghuang 13) soybeans under full (75%-95% field capacity, FC) and deficit (55%-65% FC) irrigation. The results showed that leaf net photosynthetic rate (An) and stomatal conductance (gs) were reduced by 10.6%-54.1% and 34.5%-75.9%, while intrinsic water use efficiency was increased by 31.5%-124.7% during the podding and seed-filling stages under deficit irrigation. Leaf hydraulic conductance (Kleaf) was decreased by 21.8% at the podding stage, and bulk modulus of elasticity (ε) was increased by 31.0% at the seed-filling stage under deficit irrigation. Wild soybean exhibited greater Kleaf and catalase activity, whereas cultivated soybean showed superior photosynthetic capacity and growth potential. Foliar CDs application primarily enhanced predawn leaf water potential by 27.9%, and ɛ by 49.6% at the seed-filling stage, and Kleaf by 35.4% at the podding stage, while concurrently down-regulating the stress-induced over-accumulation of CAT, proline, and soluble sugars (SS). Crucially, in wild soybean, CDs improved An by increasing Kleaf, which promoted stomatal opening and carbon assimilation. ABA played only a secondary role by negatively regulating Kleaf. In cultivated soybean, CDs promoted SS accumulation and then osmotic adjustment substances thereby enhancing gs and An, while ABA and hydraulic pathways were not significant. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that foliar CDs alleviate drought in soybean through genotype dependent mechanisms of hydraulic facilitation in wild soybean versus metabolic reprogramming in cultivated soybean. This study provides a physiological basis for the genotype-sensitive application of CDs as a drought-resilience strategy in soybean production.

9.5 Condition Monitoring of Standing Trees Based on Dendro-Acoustic Emissions
Schubert F, Duckhorn F, Reinhold M … Georgi R.
preprintPreprints.org · 2026-10-20
matched: beech, birch, tree, trees, sap flow, dendro*
abstract

In the areas of road safety, fruit growing and forestry, there is an increased need for assessing the vitality of standing trees. Various dendro-acoustic emissions (DAEs) potentially allow conclusions to be drawn about the vitality of the trees and various diseases and pest infestations. All types of DAE events produce structure-borne sound inside the tree and can be automatically measured and evaluated by a piezoelectric sensor node attached to the tree trunk. The events are characterized by short, broadband cracking signals. Depending on the type of event, these differ in their number, strength and acoustic center frequency and can be separated from various types of background noise such as traffic, wind, rain, etc. In the frame of a German collaborative research project (TreeMon), an autonomous sensor prototype for long- and short-term monitoring using LoRaWAN technology was developed and successfully tested in the field. We present selected results of various test and field measurements at different trees, including several hardwood species like beech, birch and fruit trees. We compare temporal DAE profiles of single trees with simultaneously recorded weather, dendrometer and sap flow data and demonstrate first interesting correlations between them.

9.5 Integrated pangenomics and population genomics reveal climate adaptation and maladaptation in an East Asian tree.
Chen L, Sang Y, Dan X … Wang J.
Ca2+Abiotic stressNature communications · 2026-09-15
matched: mechanosens*, drought, Populus, tree, trees, forest tree*
abstract

Characterizing genomic variation in keystone forest trees is critical for understanding and predicting the adaptation and maladaptation of terrestrial ecosystems to global climate change. In this study, we integrate five high-quality genomes and high-depth whole-genome resequencing data from 222 individuals across 44 natural populations of Populus davidiana in East Asia. Our findings reveal that genome divergence strongly correlates with epigenetic and regulatory features, with variable genomic regions closely associated with dynamic transposon activity linked to epigenetic modifications. A population-scale genotype-environment association analysis identifies 10,372 adaptive single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) and 1322 adaptive structural variations (SVs). Among these, a temperature-associated SV and a precipitation-associated SV undergo functional validation providing evidence of their roles in the expression of PdaMSL10, a mechanosensitive ion channel, and the transcription factor PdaWRKY40, respectively. Overexpression and RNAi experiments demonstrate that differential expression of PdaWRKY40 is strongly associated with drought sensitivity or tolerance, facilitating adaptation to diverse environmental conditions. Additionally, genomic vulnerability forecasts under future climate scenarios highlight the increasingly significant role of SVs in environmental adaptation. These findings advance our understanding of the genetic mechanisms underlying climate adaptation in non-model keystone species and provide invaluable genetic resources for breeding programs aimed at enhancing climate resilience.

9.5 PyMYB106-PyLAC12 Modulates Callus Browning Through Phenylpropanoid-Flavonoid Metabolism in Pinus yunnanensis.

abstract

Callus browning is the main obstacle in the process of in vitro culture of woody plants, which is more serious in conifers. Here, multi-omics integration and transgenic approaches are employed to dissect the regulatory network of callus browning in Pinus yunnanensis. Browning callus showed marked reductions in total phenolics, flavonoids, and flavanols, with a concurrent increase in total quinone content. 9.5 Trait-specific benefits of association-based marker preselection for genomic prediction in slash pine
Ding X, Zhang Y, Luan Q … Jiang J.
preprintCell biology and organellesGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-22
matched: cell wall*, growth, pine, Pinus, conifer*, tree, trees, wood formation
abstract

Abstract Slash pine ( Pinus elliottii Engelm. var. elliottii ) is a leading resin-producing conifer and multipurpose industrial tree species, valued as a renewable source of rosin and turpentine for the naval-stores and bio-based chemical industries, and of timber and pulp; yet simultaneous improvement of growth, wood quality, oleoresin yield, and resin chemistry is constrained by long generation intervals and late-expressed traits. Using 240 open-pollinated trees genotyped with 53,229 transcriptome-derived SNPs, we characterized marker associations for 12 traits and evaluated genomic selection (GS) with GWAS, SNP ranking, QTN discovery, and model fitting restricted to training folds. Training-fold GWAS showed differences in association patterns among trait classes: growth traits showed the strongest, most recurrent associations, wood-quality traits moderate signals, and oleoresin traits weaker, less stable associations. Annotation of significant, large-effect, and recurrent QTN-tagged transcripts converged on nucleotide and RNA processing, respiratory energy and cell-wall precursor supply, and lipid-terpenoid metabolism, providing hypotheses for growth, wood formation, and resin physiology. Using fold-contained GWAS outputs as marker priors, model-averaged predictive ability increased by 11.9% for growth, more than five-fold the 2.2% gain for wood quality, but did not improve oleoresin traits. The selected configurations for diameter at breast height and height yielded PA values of 0.493 and 0.365, respectively, no single GS model dominated across traits, and fixed large-effect or recurrent QTN covariates rarely surpassed optimized genomic baselines without fixed QTN effects, suggesting limited additional predictive value of these covariates under the tested configurations. GS-selected elite sets retained 80.3–95.1% of the same-cycle phenotype-BLUP benchmark gain; assuming 15-year conventional and 7-year GS cycles yielded a projected 1.7-2.0-fold annualized benchmark advantage. These results support further evaluation of trait-specific, GWAS-informed prediction in slash pine. Model selection reused the cross-validation results, and independent family, site, and generation validation is needed; realized breeding gains were not measured.

9.0 Canopy traits reveal climate-associated genetic differentiation in a California oak common garden
Goetz, A. R. B., Young … V. L.
preprintGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-23
matched: stem, growth, development, differentiation, oak, Quercus, tree, trees
abstract

Background and Aims: Phenotypic variation in plant populations reflecting historical climate adaptation may affect performance under future climates. In long-lived trees, height and stem diameter are commonly used to assess growth, but these measures may not fully capture complementary structural and functional dimensions of performance, such as canopy development and vigor. We investigated whether canopy traits reveal climate-associated genetic differentiation in Quercus lobata. Methods. Using drone-based remote sensing of 1,395 12-year-old Quercus lobata trees from 601 families in a common garden, we quantified canopy volume and greenness, using the Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI). Previous analyses indicated that trees originating from warmer climates grew more in common gardens than those from cooler climates. To assess whether canopy traits showed similar patterns, we tested for family-level variation in each trait and associations with climate of origin. Key Results. Canopy volume and greenness showed significant genetic variation among families and significant associations with climate of origin. Trees from hotter climates had greater canopy volume and greenness than trees from cooler climates. Conclusions. These covarying traits provide novel evidence of climate-associated genetic differentiation in tree performance, consistent with historical climate selection. Trees originating from climates with hot summers, moderate winters, and low precipitation performed best in the garden, suggesting that they will perform better than trees adapted to cool, wet conditions under future climate change.

9.0 Comparison of synthetic and post-synthetic molecular genetic methods of reducing xylan glucuronidation in hybrid aspen for biorefinery applications.
Sivan P, Urbancsok J, Gandla ML … Mellerowicz EJ.
Ca2+Cell biology and organellesBiotechnology for biofuels and bioproducts · 2026-10-15
matched: apoplast*, cell wall*, Golgi, Populus, aspen, woody
abstract

BackgroundThe pattern of xylan backbone substitution by 4-O-methylglucuronic acid (mGlcA) and O-acetyl groups generated during biosynthesis in the Golgi apparatus plays a key role in molecular interactions between secondary cell wall polymers in hardwoods, thus regulating biorefinery properties of woody biomass. This study aims at comparing the effects of two different approaches to reduce xylan glucuronidation, targeting either xylan biosynthesis by RNAi suppression of GLUCURONIC SUBSTITUTION OF XYLAN1 genes (denoted Gux1RNAi) or by xylan post-synthetic modification in the secondary cell wall by apoplastic wood-specific expression of a fungal α-glucuronidase from family GH67 (denoted WP:GH67) in hybrid aspen (Populus tremula L. x tremuloides Michx.). The effects of reducing xylan glucuronidation by these two approaches on xylan acetylation and solubility, and recalcitrance of wood to enzymatic hydrolysis were compared.ResultsBoth approaches reduced mGlcA/xylose (Xyl) ratio by approx. 30% without altering Xyl content in alkali extracts, while xylan acetyl content in subcritical water extracts was increased only in Gux1RNAi lines. The Gux1RNAi lines showed reduced glucuronidation in the major xylan domain containing evenly-spaced mGlcA, whereas the WP:GH67 lines were affected in both odd and even glucuronidation patterns. Interestingly, the arrays containing mGlcA every second xylosyl unit were not affected by any modification, whereas an increase in xylo-oligosaccharides containing O-2,3 diacetylated Xyl was found in Gux1RNAi lines only. Neither modification consistently affected glucose or Xyl yields in saccharification without or with acid pretreatment, whereas xylan extractability in subcritical water was increased in Gux1RNAi, but not in WP:GH67 lines.ConclusionOur study supports the hypothesis that acetylation competes with glucuronidation by GUX1 during biosynthesis, and increased acetylation caused by GUX1 suppression rather than reduced xylan glucuronidation per se improves xylan extractability. The 30% reduction in glucuronidation did not affect enzymatic saccharification, supporting the concept that cell wall structure and recalcitrance in transgenic lines are maintained by xylan domains which were not affected by either transgenic modification.

9.0 Genetic architecture and genomic prediction of stem forking across two generations of loblolly pine (Pinus taeda L.)
Guner P, Shalizi MN, Walker TD, Isik F.
preprintGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-23
matched: stem, pine, Pinus, tree, trees
abstract

Abstract Stem forking, assessed as a binary trait, is an important defect in Pinus taeda (loblolly pine) that reduces timber quality and product value. We investigated its genetic architecture and prediction accuracy using data from a two-generation breeding population evaluated in multi-environment trials. Genetic parameters were estimated using animal and family models with conditional penalized quasi-likelihood (PQL) and marginal PQL. We conducted genome-wide association and family-based linkage mapping to identify QTL. Individual-tree heritability on the latent scale was low (0.09–0.13), whereas family-mean heritability was high (0.93), indicating significant additive genetic control at the family mean level, but substantial environmental effect at the tree level. The probit PQL models produced slightly higher heritability estimates, whereas logistic and marginal models produced similar estimates. Association and linkage analyses identified a significant lineage-specific QTL on linkage group 3 but fitting it as a fixed covariate did not improve prediction accuracy. Across-generation family-level predictive ability was moderate (0.40). Forking had modest additive genetic correlations with tree height (0.36) and stem diameter (0.24). These results indicated that the underlying (unobserved) inheritance of stem forking in P. taeda is polygenic, while its expression among individual trees is strongly influenced by environmental and developmental factors. Response to genomic selection at the family level will be effective in reducing stem forking, while prediction of outcome for individual trees remains challenging.

9.0 The Ash Dieback Pathogen Resists the Chemical Defences of Its Host Tree, European Ash, by Depleting Them From the Growth Medium.
Lichnock B, Gentsch N, Papadopulos A … Lehenberger M.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPlant, cell & environment · 2026-10-20
matched: pathogen*, defence, leaf, leaves, growth, tree, trees, forest tree*
abstract

Plant pathogenic fungi are major drivers of disease in forest trees, often causing substantial ecological and economic damage. Ash dieback, caused by the ascomycete Hymenoscyphus fraxineus, has resulted in the widespread decline of Fraxinus excelsior populations throughout Europe. As infection is initiated in leaves containing diverse plant defensive metabolites, the ability of H. fraxineus to cope with host chemistry may determine successful host colonisation. We therefore investigated the responses of H. fraxineus to crude ash leaf extracts and individual defensive compounds. In contrast to the non-ash associated fungi tested, H. fraxineus was not inhibited by crude methanolic leaf extracts or most plant defence compounds tested. In addition, H. fraxineus depleted ash compounds extensively from the growth medium. Consistent with these findings, untargeted metabolomics revealed extensive alterations of the ash leaf-based medium by H. fraxineus, with 48% of detected chemical features being significantly reduced following fungal growth. Moreover, the growth of H. fraxineus was promoted by nearly all tested ash metabolites in minimal medium, indicating that these compounds can support fungal growth and may serve as carbon sources. Together, these results suggest that successful colonisation of ash tissues by H. fraxineus depends on its resistance to host defences, facilitated by its ability to deplete defensive compounds from colonised tissue.

8.5 Navigating Guaiacyl and Syringyl Lignin Structural Complexity in Intact Plant Cell Walls Through 13C Chemical Shift Mapping by Two-Dimensional Solid-State NMR.
Xiao P, Sahu P, Debnath D … Wang T.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthMagnetic resonance in chemistry : MRC · 2026-10-18
matched: cell wall*, nuclear, development, poplar*, spruce, lignin*
abstract

Lignin is a structurally complex and heterogeneous biopolymer whose in situ architecture remains difficult to resolve because of extensive spectral overlap and structural perturbations introduced during isolation and dissolution. These challenges have limited direct interrogation of native lignin within intact plant cell walls and underscore the need for analytical approaches capable of resolving lignin structure in its native context. Here, we present a framework that assists systematic 13C solid-state NMR identification of guaiacyl- and syringyl-based lignin motifs, including their associated monolignol units and interunit linkages with reduced level of ambiguity directly in native lignocellulosic materials. This approach is applicable to solid-state NMR analysis of 13C-enriched plant systems or dynamic nuclear polarization (DNP) analysis of unlabeled plant samples. By compiling and organizing diagnostic chemical shift patterns for syringyl and guaiacyl units across selected major linkage types, we delineate regions of uniqueness and overlap in 2D 13C correlation spectra. The scope is restricted to G/S-based lignin mapping, with applications to Arabidopsis, hardwoods, and softwoods. Application to Arabidopsis wild-type and lignin mutants, as well as to the hardwood species poplar and eucalyptus and the softwood spruce, reveals species-, genotype-, and developmental stage-dependent structural variations. The analysis further highlights the uncertainty arising from the structural complexity of the lignin network and demonstrates the need for higher dimensional correlation approaches for assignment of lignin structure. This work establishes a reference for the continued development of solid-state NMR-based G/S lignin analysis and advances the ability to interrogate native lignin architecture in intact plant cell walls.

8.5 Picea pan-transcriptomes reveal signatures of complex phylogenetic conflict and adaptation.
Fan Z, Liu L, Ma L … Ru D.
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthCladistics : the international journal of the Willi Hennig Society · 2026-10-24
matched: signalling, ubiquitin*, cell wall*, chloroplast*, differentiation, spruce, Picea, conifer*, trees
abstract

Coniferous forests are among the ecosystems most exposed to rapid climate warming, and a well-resolved phylogeny of Picea is essential for understanding the evolutionary processes that have shaped diversification and regional differentiation across the genus. Here, we analysed pan-transcriptomes spanning 37 Picea species from across Northern Hemisphere forests. Our analyses reveal a bipartite pan-transcriptomic architecture in which core genes (9.4%) are associated primarily with fundamental cellular functions, whereas dispensable and private components (90.6% combined) are more strongly associated with ecological differentiation. Species trees inferred from low-copy orthologues resolve four major clades, and approximately 275 loci are sufficient to recover a stable backbone topology. Coalescent analyses indicate pervasive genealogical discordance, with incomplete lineage sorting (ILS) showing the greatest relative importance (27.75%) and gene-flow signals contributing less (7.27%). The most variable gene families (7.4%) further delimit three biogeographically structured regional groups: Qinghai-Tibet Plateau lineages are characterized by genes involved in environmental stress, signalling and low-oxygen responses; East Asian lineages show greater representation of protein metabolism and ubiquitin-mediated regulation; and Holarctic lineages are characterized by photosynthesis, chloroplast function, cell-wall processes and secondary metabolism. Together, these findings link regional gene-family differentiation with the persistence of ancestral polymorphism and provide a phylogenomic framework for understanding spruce diversification and for developing hypotheses relevant to climate-resilient forest conservation.

8.5 Strategies for the Use and Conservation of Trichoderma spp. for the Growth of Forest Trees in Nurseries.
Barrera-Ramírez R, Gómez-Godínez LJ, Muñoz-Flores HJ … Aguirre-Noyola JL.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressGrowthMethods in molecular biology (Clifton, N.J.) · 2027-01-01
matched: pathogen*, nutrient*, seed*, germination, growth, trees, forest tree*
abstract

Deforestation and the degradation of forest ecosystems are critical problems affecting biodiversity and the sustainability of rural communities, especially in Latin America. Ecological restoration seeks to restore these ecosystems by using forest nurseries to produce native plants. However, diseases such as the damping-off complex, caused by fungi like Rhizoctonia sp., Pythium sp., and Fusarium sp., are common and affect seedlings in these nurseries. The use of biological agents, such as the fungus Trichoderma, has proven effective in controlling these pathogens and promoting plant growth. Trichoderma works through mechanisms such as nutrient competition, mycoparasitism, and the production of antimicrobial metabolites, thereby promoting plant health by activating their natural defenses. Strains such as T. harzianum and T. viride have been extensively studied and have shown good results in disease control. However, their use in forest nurseries has been limited by their high cost and limited knowledge of their efficacy. In Mexico, research has shown that inoculating seedlings with Trichoderma improves germination, growth, and disease resistance. Conserving Trichoderma through methods such as cryopreservation and freeze-drying is crucial to ensuring its long-term availability. Future research is expected to optimize commercial formulations and explore the use of native strains adapted to local conditions, contributing to the sustainability of forest ecosystems.

8.5 Substrate-mediated facilitation of softwood and hardwood deconstruction by sequential white-rot and brown-rot fungal treatment.
Wu D, Li Y, Huang C … Li X.
Bioresource technology · 2026-10-15
matched: oak, Quercus, pine, Pinus, woody, lignin*
abstract

Translating natural, successional wood decomposition into an engineered two-stage fungal pretreatment offers a promising strategy for biomass deconstruction, yet how pioneer white-rot fungal modifications facilitate subsequent brown-rot decay remains unclear. Scots pine (Pinus sylvestris L.) was pretreated with Trametes versicolor (Tv) and Phanerochaete chrysosporium (Pc) and subsequently exposed to Gloeophyllum trabeum (Gt). After 15-day BRF incubation, second-stage mass loss increased by 40.9% and 126.2% in the Tv- and Pc-pretreated groups, respectively, relative to the control without WRF pretreatment. 8.5 [Identification of SPS family genes and regulation of photosynthetic pigment stability by PthSPS1/2/3 in Populus cv.741].
Xu Z, Wang Z, Xu Y … Wu J.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthMethodsSheng wu gong cheng xue bao = Chinese journal of biotechnology · 2026-10-01
matched: chloroplast*, growth, development, poplar*, Populus, woody, CRISPR
abstract

Solanesyl diphosphate synthases (SPSs), are key enzymes in the isoprenoid metabolic pathway, catalyze the biosynthesis of solanesyl diphosphate (SPP). In plants, the SPS family includes geranyl diphosphate synthase (GPS), farnesyl diphosphate synthase (FPS), geranylgeranyl diphosphate synthase (GGPPS), and SPS, catalyzing the stepwise condensation to form SPP. SPP is involved in the biosynthesis of solanesol and plastoquinone (PQ), both of which play important roles in plant growth, development, and the regulation of photosynthesis. Here, to elucidate the functions of the SPS subfamily in woody plants, we took 'Poplar 741' [Populus. alba×(Populus. davidiana+Populus. simonii)×Populus. tomentosa] as the experimental material. A total of 29 family members distributed across four subfamilies-PthSPS/PthFPS/PthGPS/PthGGPPS-related to the isoprenoid pathway were identified at the genome-wide level, located on 8 chromosome groups. Among them, 24 members were localized to chloroplasts and 5 to the cytoplasm. Three highly homologous PthSPSs were predicted as unstable hydrophilic proteins in chloroplasts. Subsequently, PthSPS1/2/3-edited mutant regenerated plants of 'Poplar 741' were generated via the CRISPR/Cas9 system. Phenotypic observation revealed three phenotypes: albino, green, and chimeric. PCR identification and sequencing analysis confirmed insertions and deletions (indels) at the target sites of PthSPS1/2/3. Physiological and biochemical analyses showed that the content of SPP, chlorophyll a, chlorophyll b, and carotenoids were significantly lower in the albino mutants (pthsps1/2/3) than in the wild type, indicating that PthSPS1/2/3 regulated the stability of photosynthetic pigments. These results provide a new direction for further dissecting the roles of the isoprenoid metabolic pathway in photosynthesis, growth, and development of woody plants.

8.0 MGS-HCT enables resistance gene discovery and functional sequence design in forest trees.
Guan X, Wei Z, Wang L … Chen W.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityMethodsBMC biotechnology · 2026-07-29
matched: NLR*, tree, trees, forest tree*, deep learning
abstract

Resistance gene discovery in forest trees remains challenging because of their large, repeat-rich genomes and the sparse distribution of functional elements, which restrict the direct application of deep learning approaches developed for crop species. While genomic language models have shown promise in crop species, their application to forest trees remains limited. Here, we present MGS-HCT (Multi-scale Gated Sparse Encoder-Hybrid Convolutional Transformer), a deep learning framework tailored for forest tree genomic sequences. The model integrates multi-scale gated units with structure-aware sparse attention for efficient sequence compression, and combines convolutional modules with transformer layers to capture both local motifs and long-range regulatory dependencies. We show that MGS-HCT supports two key applications in forest tree biotechnology: (1) the conditional generation and prioritization of putative NLR candidates for Alnus glutinosa (common alder) and (2) the de novo generation of synthetic DNA sequences containing promoter-like and splice-site-like elements. These results provide a computational foundation for accelerating disease-resistance gene discovery and genomic-assisted breeding in forest tree species, expanding the scope of deep learning from crop plants to forest genomics. By prioritizing candidates supported by multiple computational criteria for subsequent experimental validation, the framework is intended to narrow the experimental search space and help shorten the long breeding cycles of forest trees.

8.0 Predation risk alters prey caching behavior and resultant tree establishment patterns.
Potash AD, McCleery RA.
GrowthEcology · 2026-10-01
matched: seed*, oak, oaks, Quercus, tree, trees
abstract

The drivers of plant dispersal and distribution are poorly understood. For nut-bearing trees, interactions between seeds and caching granivores can have a strong influence on population dynamics as unrecovered caches often recruit new trees. Granivore-seed interactions may be constrained by predators as fear alters caching and foraging behavior. We investigated how predation risk affects caching and foraging behavior in fox squirrels (Sciurus niger) and subsequent recruitment patterns of American turkey oak (Quercus laevis) acorns. We marked and tracked acorns to locate fox squirrel cache and consumption points where we assessed fox squirrel perceived predation risk, measured how caching influenced acorn survival and dispersal, and surveyed juvenile oak distributions. Fox squirrels perceived greater risk at caches than consumption points, suggesting predation risk influences caching decisions and cache distribution. Buried acorns survived at higher rates and only buried acorns germinated, though survival did not vary with dispersal distance. Juvenile oaks and cache locations showed similar hump-shaped patterns of establishment away from nearest potential source trees. We conclude that predation risk indirectly shapes plant establishment by altering granivore behavior.

matched: mitochondri*, willow*, Salix, tree
abstract

We present a genome assembly of Salix reticulata (Net-leaved Willow; Streptophyta; Magnoliopsida; Malpighiales; Salicaceae). The assembly consists of two haplotypes with total lengths of 396.19 megabases and 393.95 megabases. Most of the haplotype 1 assembly (89.89%) was scaffolded into 19 chromosomal pseudomolecules.
