This assembly was generated as part of the Darwin Tree of Life project, which produces reference genomes for eukaryotic species found in Britain and Ireland.

8.0 The genome sequence of the Dwarf Willow, Salix herbacea L. (Malpighiales: Salicaceae).
Ruhsam M, Hollingsworth PM, Royal Botanic Garden Edinburgh Genome Acquisition Lab … Darwin Tree of Life Consortium.

8.0 The heat stress transcription factors HsfA3a/b enhance heat stress resistance by regulating HSP26.5-1/-2 in Sorbus pohuashanensis.
Wu Q, Qi X, Di Z … Zheng J.
Abiotic stressGrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-14
matched: heat stress, stress respon*, leaves, growth, trees, woody, forest tree*
abstract

Heat stress impacts the growth and value of ornamental plants such as Sorbus pohuashanensis. Heat stress transcription factors (Hsfs) and heat shock proteins (HSPs) are critical in the response of plants to heat stress. However, the roles of HsfA3a/b and HSP26.5-1/-2 in the heat tolerance mechanisms of woody plants remain elusive. Here, we report the isolation of SpHsfA3a/b and SpHSP26.5-1/-2 from S. pohuashanensis and their molecular characterization. SpHsfA3a/b and SpHSP26.5-1/-2 were expressed at higher levels in leaves and fruits. Furthermore, their expression levels increased dramatically under heat stress. Overexpression of SpHsfA3a/b and SpHSP26.5-1/-2, as well as complementation of hsfa3 mutants with SpHsfA3a/b in Arabidopsis thaliana, significantly increased plant survival under heat stress. This enhanced survival was accompanied by reduced malondialdehyde (MDA) content, increased proline and soluble sugars, and elevated antioxidant enzyme activities. The positive regulatory genes related to the heat stress responses were upregulated, while the negative regulatory gene was downregulated in the transgenic SpHsfA3a/b-overexpressing Arabidopsis lines and the complemented hsfa3 mutant lines under high temperature stress. Yeast one-hybrid and dual-luciferase reporter assays showed that SpHsfA3a/b directly bound to and activated the promoters of SpHSP26.5-1/-2. In summary, SpHsfA3a/b directly regulate the expression of SpHSP26.5-1/-2 and play crucial, synergistic roles in enhancing thermotolerance. This study elucidates a key transcriptional regulatory module, SpHsfA3a/b-SpHSP26.5-1/-2, in the heat stress response of S. pohuashanensis and provides candidate genes for improving the heat resistance of forest trees.

8.0 Threatened Conifers Need Targeted and Integrated Conservation: A Case Study of Pinus dabeshanensis.
Xu Y, Tan C, Ferguson DK, Yang Y.
GrowthEcology and evolution · 2026-10-23
matched: seed*, pine, Pinus, conifer*
abstract

Due to global warming, over-exploitation, habitat degradation, environmental pollution, and alien species invasion, around 28% of assessed global species are threatened, with conifers being especially endangered. China has attached great importance to biodiversity conservation, and implemented a series of targeted initiatives over the past two decades to protect and conserve the endangered species in China. Pinus dabeshanensis W.C.Cheng et Y.W.Law is a conifer species with an extremely small population and is listed as Grade I in the national key protected wild plants in China. Despite conservation efforts, the effectiveness of these measures remains unknown. In this study, we have assessed the conservation effectiveness of this critically endangered conifer species, and identified conservation gaps to achieve a new conservation plan for the species. This paper should also be helpful for planning conservation of other endangered conifers. We conducted a systematic assessment of the current state of P. dabeshanensis, and the effectiveness of in situ and ex-situ conservation, artificial propagation, and reintroduction based on field investigations and a comprehensive review of the published literature, and thereby identified the conservation gaps. The results suggest that the natural population of P. dabeshanensis is extremely small, being composed of only 320 recorded individuals sporadically distributed in Henan, Hubei, and Anhui. Although populations in Yingshan of Hubei, Shangcheng in Henan, and Jinzhai of Anhui are protected in national nature reserves, the largest known wild population in Dawanggou of Yuexi in Anhui is not protected by any nature reserve. Ex-situ conservation is still at a preliminary stage, with very small numbers planted in botanical gardens and lacking the full range of the species' genetic diversity. We also found that the species suffers from reproductive failure including low pollination success and poor seed set. While reintroduction practices have been conducted at several localities, seedling survival rates vary greatly due to a lack of subsequent management/protection. The lack of standardized reintroduction protocols and management guidelines hinders a consistent and effective implementation of these practices. Although many Chinese institutions including universities, nature reserves, and botanical gardens have been involved in the conservation of P. dabeshanensis, the overall effectiveness has been limited due to inadequate coordination and fragmented efforts. To substantially advance conservation effectiveness, there needs to be a targeted species conservation plan. Within this framework, it will be necessary to combine different approaches in order to ensure the effective protection of this endangered pine. The present study has involved a comprehensive investigation into the conservation effectiveness of P. dabeshanensis, identified critical conservation gaps, and proposed an integrated conservation strategy. Our findings and suggestions will also be valuable for the conservation of other endangered gymnosperm species.

7.5 Conspecific density enhances sound seed production during high-seed years in European beech
Kondrat K, Szymkowiak J, Barczyk M … Bogdziewicz M.
preprintGrowthEcoEvoRxiv · 2026-10-22
matched: seed*, beech, Fagus, tree, trees
abstract

Climate change is reducing the supply of sound seeds available for forest regeneration, creating a need to identify management strategies that can counteract these losses. Managing local tree density offers one potential approach, as neighbouring trees can enhance pollination but also increase losses to seed predators. In masting species, which produce highly variable but synchronised seed crops, the balance between these benefits and costs may depend on annual reproductive effort. Here, using seeds collected beneath 314 European beech (Fagus sylvatica) trees across three stands in Poland and the United Kingdom over three years, we examined how conspecific density within 5–50 m buffers influenced pollination efficiency, pre-dispersal seed predation, sound seed production, and seed mass. During the high-seed year, predicted sound seed production increased almost 2-fold from low to high conspecific density, coinciding with a tendency for seed predation to decline with increasing density. In contrast, during low-seed years, seed predation increased with density, while pollination efficiency increased with density in one of two years. Mean seed mass decreased by approximately 9% with density in a high-seed year. These findings suggest that dense neighbourhoods can increase the number of sound seeds available for regeneration when their predation costs are weakened by high seed production. Future studies should determine whether greater seed supply outweighs reduced seed mass in its effects on seedling establishment and survival. Maintaining higher local densities of reproductive trees may therefore help sustain sound seed supply during mast events and partly buffer the consequences of masting breakdown for European beech regeneration.

7.5 Contrasting physiological-biochemical responses and elemental homeostasis in Pinus sylvestris families exposed to As, Cd and Pb.
Šilanskienė M, Striganavičiūtė-Oržekauskienė G, Sirgedaitė-Polikaitienė V … Vaitiekūnaitė D.
GrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-21
matched: root, roots, shoot*, growth, pine, Pinus, tree
abstract

Contamination with As, Cd and Pb can limit tree establishment on degraded substrates, but family-level variation in Scots pine responses remains poorly resolved. This study evaluated three open-pollinated maternal half-sib families of Pinus sylvestris L. under controlled hydroponic exposure to As, Cd and Pb. Growth, photosynthetic pigments, soluble sugars, phenolic and flavonoid contents, malondialdehyde, antioxidant enzyme activities, elemental composition and metal(loid) accumulation indices were analysed, with PCA and Random Forest used as exploratory integrative tools. Significant family and treatment interactions were observed for all measured physiological and biochemical traits, indicating non-uniform responses among families. Cd produced the clearest toxicity profile, characterized by strong chlorophyll (up to -56.0%) and carotenoid (up to -52.6%) losses, increased lipid peroxidation (up to +70.9%) and marked Fe, Mn and Zn disruption. Pb was retained predominantly in roots (up to 12.96 ± 0.45 g/kg DW), with very low translocation to shoots, but high Pb exposure strongly inhibited root elongation (up to -70.5%). As caused comparatively moderate pigment effects but was associated with increased S (up to +147.4%) and changes in secondary-metabolite and antioxidant traits. No family showed consistently superior performance across all metal(loid)s, although response strategies differed. These results identify integrated physiological and elemental screening of Scots pine reproductive material for phytostabilization-oriented restoration as potentially relevant, while field validation under realistic contaminated-substrate conditions before practical application remains necessary.

7.5 Short-Rotation Coppice Pellets as Biofuels in a Modern Boiler with an Electrostatic Precipitator: Particulate Matter and Carbon Monoxide Emissions.
Warmiński K, Dudziec P, Nocoń A, Stolarski MJ.
Molecules (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-27
matched: poplar*, willow*, birch, pine, black locust
abstract

Alternative pellet feedstocks must be evaluated for energy performance and air pollutant emissions. This study assessed particulate matter (PM) and carbon monoxide (CO) emissions from short-rotation coppice (SRC) pellets combusted in a pellet boiler equipped with an electrostatic precipitator (ESP). Pine and birch pellets served as references. Emissions were measured with and without the ESP and expressed per flue gas volume, fuel energy, and fuel mass. Pellet species significantly affected PM and CO, whereas the ESP significantly reduced PM but did not significantly affect CO. The ESP reduced mean PM concentrations by 52.5%, from 53.81 to 25.54 mg m-3. The highest PM concentration occurred for willow without the ESP (90.96 mg m-3), and the lowest for pine with the ESP (17.51 mg m-3); the highest value was 419.5% greater. Poplar produced relatively low PM but the highest CO emissions. Among SRC fuels, black locust provided the most balanced profile, combining relatively low PM and CO with the highest combustion efficiency (93.20%). Pine and birch, particularly with the ESP, produced the lowest emissions. These findings indicate that SRC pellets showed emission suitability for use in the tested boiler based on PM and CO emission levels.

7.5 Tropical leaf phenology as a thermoregulation trait: how changes in crown structure modulate thermal dynamics for two dominant Hawaiian trees.
Bayliss SLJ, Broadbent EN, Almeyda Zambrano AM, Pau S.
Abiotic stressGrowthThe New phytologist · 2026-10-20 · also: New Phytologist
matched: heat stress, leaf, leaves, tree, trees, phenolog*
abstract

Canopy and leaf temperatures often diverge from air temperature, influencing photosynthesis, water loss, and heat stress. While leaf traits are known to affect heat exchange, how changes in crown structure interact with environmental drivers to regulate canopy thermal dynamics is poorly understood, particularly in tropical forests. In a Hawaiian wet tropical montane forest, we used repeated UAV-based (Unmanned Aerial Vehicle) thermal and LiDAR (light detection and ranging) measurements of sunlit leaves within and across 397 tree crowns of Acacia koa and Metrosideros polymorpha to investigate (1) seasonal and species-specific variation in crown traits; (2) how crown traits and environmental drivers interact to determine canopy-air temperature differences (Tdiff), within-crown thermal variability (TcanSD), and maximum leaf temperatures (TcanMAX); and (3) whether crowns with greater seasonal structural change exhibit enhanced thermoregulation. All crown traits showed species-specific seasonal dynamics, with trait-trait and trait-environment interactions shaping thermal metrics differently across species. In M. polymorpha, crowns with greater seasonal structural change exhibited lower relative Tdiff, whereas A. koa did not show this buffering effect. These results indicate that canopy thermoregulation emerges from interacting crown traits dynamically reshaped across phenological and seasonal cycles. Seasonal crown dynamics provide partial thermal buffering in some species, highlighting species-specific pathways of thermal acclimation or vulnerability under climate change.

7.0 A heat-assisted tt Lb Cas12a workflow incorporating crRNA 3′-tailing enables efficient multiplex biallelic genome editing in hybrid poplar
Zahn, V., Fendel … T.
preprintbioRxiv · 2026-10-23
matched: poplar*, Populus, trees, forest tree*
abstract

In forest trees, high genome-editing efficiency in primary transformants is particularly important for functional genomics and trait improvement, as long generation times severely limit the use of subsequent generations to recover fully edited genotypes. This challenge is further compounded in hybrid poplar, where paralogous gene copies and allelic variation make efficient multiplex editing particularly difficult. We optimized Lb Cas12a-mediated editing in Populus × canescens ( Populus tremula × Populus alba ), clone INRA 717-1B4, by targeting four paralogous PtaABA8ox4 loci using temperature-tolerant tt Lb Cas12a, a two-stage heat treatment, and crRNA 3′-tailing. The heat-treatment regime preserved transgenic recovery, while ttLbCas12a -based constructs and heat treatment substantially increased biallelic editing. Mutations were dominated by small deletions, and biallelic out-of-frame recovery increased in ttLbCas12a -based contexts without scaling proportionally with biallelic editing. Thus, heat-assisted tt Lb Cas12a-mediated approaches enable efficient multiplex editing in hybrid poplar, whereas genotype quality remains dependent on repair outcome and somatic uniformity.

7.0 Combustion Kinetics of Natural Polymers in Lemon-Tree Pruning Wood at High Heating Rates: Criado Master Plots and Šesták-Berggren Modelling.
Ismail MA, Dubdub I, Mousa S, Al-Yaari M.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthPolymers · 2026-09-25
matched: cell wall*, xylem, tree, woody, lignin*
abstract

Lemon-tree pruning wood is a lignocellulosic natural-polymer solid whose cell wall is built from cellulose, hemicellulose and lignin. This study evaluates the use of this polymer matrix as a solid biofuel by combining physicochemical characterization with non-isothermal oxidative conversion at 20-80 K min-1. Lemon-tree branch (LTB) from orchards in Nubaria, Egypt, was analyzed for proximate and ultimate composition, Van Soest fiber fractions, HHV, FTIR, XRD and SEM-EDX. Thermogravimetric combustion was carried out in air at 20, 40, 60 and 80 K min-1. Apparent activation energies were obtained by five isoconversional methods (FR, FWO, KAS, STK, VY) and by Kissinger analysis of the two dry-fuel DTG peaks. Reaction models were screened by Criado master plots, a single-event Šesták-Berggren fit and Coats-Redfern. LTB is a moderately crystalline xylem (cellulose 52.11 wt%, CrI 52.6%, lignin 17.11 wt%) with moisture 7.10 wt%, volatile matter 77.80 wt%, fixed carbon 19.90 wt%, ash 2.30 wt% and HHV 18.50 MJ kg-1. Nitrogen and sulfur are 0.55 and 0.18 wt%. Combustion proceeds in three stages: Stage 1 moisture release, Stage 2 holocellulose-dominated oxidative devolatilization (65-68 wt% loss, Tp1 ≈ 600-640 K) and Stage 3 char oxidation (≈25 wt% loss, Tp2 ≈ 710-730 K). Mean isoconversional Ea on α = 0.1-0.7 is 98-115 kJ mol-1 and rises to ~212 kJ mol-1 at α = 0.8. Criado plots follow F2/F3/D3 up to α ≈ 0.5; a Šesták-Berggren function with m = 0.174 and n = 2.3 describes the Stage 2 window (R2 = 0.890) as an empirical fit only. Coats-Redfern selects F2 for Stage 2. ΔH tracks Ea, ΔG is positive, and ΔS is negative on Stage 2. LTB is a low-ash woody pruning fuel suitable for pellets or co-firing if Stage 3 is completed above ~750 K.

7.0 Elucidation and reconstitution of the poplar salicortin biosynthetic pathway.
Lackus ND, Ma D, Nakamura Y … Köllner TG.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPlant communications · 2026-10-23
matched: defense, roots, poplar*, Populus, willow*
abstract

Salicinoids are specialized benzenoid (C6-C1) metabolites characteristic of the Salicaceae family. Although salicinoids have long been recognized as effective defense compounds and pharmacologically active constituents in willows and poplars, the biosynthetic pathway underlying their formation remains largely unresolved. Through integrated metabolomic and transcriptomic analyses of black cottonwood (Populus trichocarpa) roots, we identified candidate genes involved in salicinoid biosynthesis belonging to the cytochrome P450 monooxygenase family 92 (CYP92) and the progesterone 5β-reductase/iridoid synthase-like enzyme (PRISE) family of short-chain dehydrogenases/reductases. Co-expression of all identified candidate genes, together with the previously characterized glycosyltransferase gene PtUGT71L1, acyltransferase gene PtACT47, and cinnamic acid-CoA ligase gene PtCNL5, in Nicotiana benthamiana resulted in the production of salicortin, the most abundant salicinoid in poplar. Further characterization of the P450 candidates revealed that two enzymes catalyze ortho-hydroxylation of both aromatic rings of the proposed salicortin precursor, benzyl benzoate, while two other P450s catalyze the subsequent oxidative dearomatization of the benzoic acid-derived ring of benzyl benzoate. The resulting dieneone intermediate is converted by PRISE enzymes via a 1,4-reduction to form the characteristic 1-hydroxy-6-oxocyclohex-2-en-1-carboxylic ester moiety of salicortin. Functional redundancy among the identified CYP92 and PRISE enzymes may reflect the central role of these compounds in plant defense.

7.0 Forest succession and demographic groups interact to influence insect herbivory on woody seedlings.
Cinoğlu D, Weissflog A, Dent DH … Comita LS.
GrowthOecologia · 2026-10-24
matched: leaf, seed*, growth, tree, trees, woody
abstract

Most tropical forests are subject to human disturbances, creating successional communities of varying forest ages. Changes in abiotic conditions and dominant plant species over succession may drive shifts in insect herbivory with forest age, influencing patterns of forest recovery. We quantified leaf insect herbivory (incidence and percentage standing damage) on 4774 seedlings of 185 woody species within 10 1-ha forest plots spanning a successional gradient from 9 to 129 years since disturbance in lowland tropical moist forest in Panama. We classified species as lianas vs. trees and further divided trees into five demographic groups according to their position along the growth-survival and stature-recruitment trade-off axes (slow, fast, long-lived pioneer, short-lived breeder, intermediate). We measured understory light availability to determine whether differences in herbivory were related to decreasing light availability during succession. At the forest plot level, herbivory incidence, but not damage, on woody seedlings increased with forest age. Herbivory incidence increased similarly with forest age for seedlings of trees and lianas. However, the relationships between forest age and herbivory incidence varied among tree demographic groups. Specifically, fast and short-lived breeders had a significantly greater increase in herbivory incidence with forest age compared to slow species. Understory light availability decreased on average with forest age but did not explain variation in herbivory. Our findings improve understanding of how past human disturbance influences natural enemy pressure on seedlings and suggest that decreased herbivory in early-successional forests may contribute to forest recovery while increased herbivory in late-successional forests may maintain seedling diversity.

7.0 IRON DEFICIENCY RESHAPES THE POPLAR ROOT AND EXUDATE METABOLITE PROFILES AND DRIVES EXUDATE-MEDIATED IRON MOBILIZATION
Cieschi, M. T., BOUZARI … S.
preprintCa2+Abiotic stressGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-19
matched: pH, nutrient*, root, roots, poplar*, Populus
abstract

ABSTRACT Iron (Fe) deficiency under alkaline conditions limits Fe bioavailability and reshapes plant metabolism. We asked how Fe deficiency at pH 7.5 alters the metabolite profiles of roots and root exudates of Populus × canescens , and whether exudates mobilize Fe at high pH. Hydroponically grown plants were deprived of Fe for 20 and 27 days. Roots and exudates, collected in nutrient solution and in distilled water, were profiled by UHPLC–QTOF MS, and metabolites were annotated using MS/MS molecular networking (MetGem) and public spectral libraries. Fe deficiency caused significant global shifts in both root and exudate metabolomes. In roots, phenylpropanoids increased at 27 days (proanthocyanidins, neochlorogenic acid, naringenin), whereas the salicylate glycoside salicortin decreased, suggesting a redirection of phenylpropanoid flux. In nutrient-solution exudates, eight flavonoids, including quercitrin, accumulated strongly at 27 days, and catecholic coumarins such as fraxetin were detected in roots and exudates. Exudates from Fe-deficient plants increased Fe mobilization from Fe(III) oxides at pH 7.5, and quercitrin solubilized Fe as efficiently as fraxetin. Alkaline Fe deficiency thus reconfigures poplar root and exudate metabolomes, and exuded flavonoids, not only coumarins, may contribute to Fe mobilization at high pH. HIGHLIGHT Iron deficiency at alkaline pH reshapes poplar root and exudate metabolomes; exuded flavonoids such as quercitrin, alongside coumarins, contribute to iron mobilization from insoluble iron oxides.

7.0 Interspecific difference in seasonal change of stomatal O3 uptake and annual cumulative stomatal O3 uptake estimated by species-specific Jarvis-type models for 10 Japanese urban greening tree species.
Yagita A, Oi N, Endo W … Izuta T.
GrowthEnvironmental research · 2026-10-15
matched: leaf, leaves, stoma*, tree, trees, phenolog*
abstract

Tropospheric ozone (O3) is a detrimental gaseous air pollutant for human health. Urban greening trees can reduce the atmospheric O3 concentration in urban areas by absorbing it through the stomata into the leaves, but the most effective species for this purpose remain unclear. This study addresses this gap by developing new species-specific Jarvis-type stomatal conductance (gs) models for 10 typical Japanese urban tree species. Using these models, we examined the seasonal changes in stomatal O3 uptake, the annual cumulative stomatal O3 uptake (COU), and their determining factors in the 10 species. Firstly, developing species-specific gs models is imperative for accurately quantifying O3 uptake, because the optimal gs models for all the species incorporated limiting functions due to photosynthetic photon flux density (flight) and leaf phenology (fphen), while other environmental functions were not necessarily included. Secondly, evergreen trees generally showed greater annual O3 uptake due to longer leaf duration, whereas deciduous trees had potential for greater O3 removal in early spring, when atmospheric O3 concentrations peak. Thirdly, within the five evergreen broad-leaved tree species, Osmanthus fragrans exhibited relatively high physiological activity over a prolonged period, resulting in the highest COU. Within the five deciduous tree species, Styrax japonicus exhibited the highest COU, due to the highest gmax and tolerance to relatively high air temperature and vapor pressure deficit, some of which differ from previously reported findings. These insights provide a scientific basis for optimizing urban tree selection to improve air quality.

7.0 Modeling diseased xylem networks.
Walker NC.
reviewPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthAmerican journal of botany · 2026-10-15
matched: pathogen*, stems, xylem, growth, development, hydraulic*
abstract

Vascular wilt pathogens induce xylem blockages that disrupt plant water transport, posing critical threats to agricultural productivity and food security. Key processes including xylem transport, vessel blockage, and pathogen spread within the xylem remain poorly understood, largely due to our limited ability to internally monitor infected tissues. This review explores how complementary mathematical modeling alongside laboratory studies can elucidate features governing these critical processes. I first provide a brief history of xylem imaging and quantification, highlighting technological advances, and persistent limitations. I then review modeling approaches to describe xylem water transport and pathogen dynamics, emphasizing that different pathogens require fundamentally different modeling frameworks. Existing xylem flow models may provide suitable hydraulic conductivity estimates for healthy stems under idealized conditions; however, they lack explicit representation of pit membranes and lumen-occluding structures, limiting their applicability to diseased stems where these features are actively modified during infection. Previously described biofilm models can replicate bacterial colony growth in controlled experimental settings but are often too complex for integration with flow simulations and rely on parameters that are difficult to quantify experimentally. Finally, I highlight directions and challenges for modeling occlusion and spread at the local scale and discuss how these models might be scaled to network-level architecture. While these types of models remain computationally challenging, advances in multiscale imaging technologies and scaling approaches promise significant progress in the coming decades. Insights from such models stand to advance both our understanding of disease progression and the development of resilient crops for an uncertain climate future.

7.0 Temporal turnover and fire severity shape postfire beetle (Coleoptera) diversity at a forest range edge.
Yáñez BE, Strobel JE, Gabbard VL … Maguiña-Conde R.
Journal of insect science (Online) · 2026-09-01
matched: pine, pines, Pinus, tree, woody
abstract

In recent decades, wildfires have increased in frequency and severity, reshaping forest structure and biodiversity. Yet it remains unclear whether postfire insect assemblages are driven primarily by fire severity, vegetation structure, or broader temporal community turnover, particularly in fragmented range-edge forests. We investigated flight-active beetle (Coleoptera) assemblages 2 years after a 2022 wildfire in the Lost Pines region of Texas, an isolated loblolly pine (Pinus taeda L.) forest at the species' western range margin. Using historical data from 2011, we evaluated temporal turnover and compared diversity and assemblage composition between contemporary burned and unburned sites. We also quantified fire severity and vegetation structure to analyze their effects on richness and abundance. Contemporary and burned sites supported higher richness and abundance than historical and unburned sites, although evenness declined as opportunistic species became dominant. Beetle assemblage differed significantly between sampling periods and burn status, indicating substantial temporal turnover amplified by wildfire. Fire severity showed strong positive associations with richness and abundance, whereas vegetation structure played a secondary but important role. Richness and abundance increased with woody plant richness, tree diversity, tree size, live tree density, and shrub richness. In contrast, dead tree variables were generally weak or negative predictors, suggesting that the influence of fire-generated deadwood may be transient and strongest shortly after disturbance. Together, these findings indicate that postfire flight-active beetle assemblages in the Lost Pines are shaped by the interaction of long-term temporal turnover and fire-driven successional dynamics, with fire severity acting as the dominant driver of community change.

6.5 Comparative Study on Photoinhibition Characteristics and Adaptation Mechanisms of New Current-Year Needles of Pinus densiflora var. zhangwuensis and Pinus sylvestris var. mongolica in Sandy Land Environments.
Meng P, Zhang M, Wang X, Liu J.
Plants (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-28
matched: Pinus, tree, trees, hydraulic*
abstract

Whether photoinhibition occurs in tall trees of Pinus densiflora var. zhangwuensis and P. sylvestris var. mongolica in summer and their response mechanisms to strong midday light in sandy land are not yet clear. Therefore, three standard trees were selected for each species as biological replicates by randomly setting up standard plots. Daily changes in the chlorophyll a fluorescence parameter of current-year needles (CYNs) and one-year needles (OYNs) in different parts of the standard tree crowns (in sun branches and shade branches) of the two species were comparatively studied, and the potential hydraulic transport capacities of the sample branches were also evaluated. The maximum non-photochemical quenching (NPQ) of the sun CYNs in this species was observed at noon, and an NPQ of greater than 1 lasted for 4-5 h. (2) The midday Fv/Fm of the sun CYNs in P. densiflora var. zhangwuensis was close to the morning and shade CYN levels. The above results suggested that photoinhibition might also be involved in the tall trees of P. sylvestris var. mongolica, while P. densiflora var. zhangwuensis had a more effective mechanism to avoid photoinhibition.

6.5 DendroClim: A Free Interactive Web Application for Interpreting Climate–Growth Relationships in Tree Rings
Méndez-González J, Sosa-Díaz L, Nájera-Luna JA … García-Montiel E.
preprintGrowthPreprints.org · 2026-10-19
matched: stem, growth, tree, trees, dendro*
abstract

Climate directly influences tree-ring width, making tree-ring records a key indicator of environmental variability. This study aimed to develop DendroClim, a free interactive web application that transforms numerical predictions from climate reconstruction models into real-time geometric representations of tree-stem cross-sections, thereby facilitating the visual interpretation of the relationship between climate and radial growth. The platform was developed using HTML5, CSS, and JavaScript. For model parameterization, samples from 36 Pseudotsuga menziesii (Mirb.) Franco trees were collected in the Sierra de Arteaga, Coahuila, Mexico, and analyzed using dendrochronological techniques. Meteorological records of precipitation (P) and temperature (T) were correlated with the ring-width index (RWI), and regression equations were derived to estimate RWI and geometrically represent it as a stem cross-section. The results showed that RWI was positively associated with winter precipitation and negatively associated with spring maximum temperature explaining 55.6% of the variance in radial growth in the multiple regression model and 24.5% in the precipitation model. DendroClim incorporates four modes for generating tree-stem cross-sections (Random, Widths, Climate, and Model), enabling the geometric reconstruction of up to 1,500 rings. A dedicated module for constructing skeleton plots was also integrated to facilitate the understanding of their construction, interpretation, and cross-dating. Evaluation of DendroClim using 3,360 unit tests across 33 functions demonstrated high reliability: 30 functions (90.9%) achieved a 100% success rate, two achieved 50%, and one achieved 0%. DendroClim provides a dynamic, interactive, intuitive, gamified, and freely accessible platform for understanding tree-growth responses to precipitation, temperature, or their combined effects. In conclusion, DendroClim represents a scientifically rigorous tool for paleoclimatic simulation and environmental education without compromising the integrity of forest resources.

6.5 Growth responses of precious tree species with different plant characteristics to three lithological soil types and their interaction effects.
Zhou X, Wei X, Su S, Wang X.
Ca2+Abiotic stressGrowthBMC plant biology · 2026-07-23
matched: pH, nutrient*, root, leaf, seed*, growth, differentiation, conifer*, tree
abstract

Based on specific nutrient requirements, precise tree species-site matching is the cornerstone of effective silviculture. To explore plant-soil interactions, this study investigated the nutrient utilization strategies of three tree species with distinct traits (Ormosia hosiei Hemsl. et Wils, Ormosia henryi Prain, and Phoebe bournei (Hemsl.) Yen C. Yang) cultivated in soils derived from slate, sandstone, and coal-bearing sandy shale. We further analyzed the interactive effects between plant characteristics and lithological substrates. Results indicated that cultivation significantly altered soil physicochemical properties. Notably, O. hosiei significantly increased soil organic matter in slate and sandstone (p < 0.05) while minimizing AN consumption in these two soil types. Lithological soils significantly influenced seedling growth, nutrient utilization, and biomass accumulation. Although all species performed optimally in coal-bearing sandy shale, their driving soil factors varied. In contrast, the growth of P. bournei was primarily driven by soil pH. It regulated inter-organ phosphorus partitioning and whole-plant NUE, and PUE via pH-mediated nutrient availability, thereby maintaining growth homeostasis across different environments. This study systematically elucidates the adaptive strategies and nutrient allocation mechanisms of different species across varied lithological soils. These findings provide a robust theoretical framework for precise species-site matching and efficient plantation management during the structural adjustment of degraded coniferous forests in southern China.

6.5 Host genotype, resistance status and seasonal temperature shape the Norway spruce phyllosphere mycobiome.
Ren W, Wen Z, Wang K … Asiegbu FO.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPloS one · 2026-10-23
matched: microbiome*, seed*, development, spruce, Picea
abstract

The influence of the phyllosphere microbiome on plant health and disease development is well established. However, the roles of host resistance phenotype, seasonal variations, and accumulated temperature in shaping the phyllosphere mycobiome of Norway spruce (Picea abies) remain poorly understood. In this study, we investigated (i) the phyllosphere mycobiome assembly of a subset of Heterobasidion partially resistant and susceptible Norway spruce genotypes, (ii) how fungal community composition in these genotypes changes across seasons and with temperature, and (iii) the extent to which the phyllosphere mycobiome shared taxa with the seed-associated fungal communities of the same genotypes. Needle samples were collected from Heterobasidion partially resistant and susceptible Norway spruce seedlings growing in an open field in May, July and September 2022. Fungal communities were characterized using internal transcribed spacer (ITS) amplicon sequencing. Alpha diversity metrics (richness, diversity and evenness) of the phyllosphere fungal community were significantly different between susceptible and partially resistant Norway spruce genotypes. Although the distributions showed substantial overlap between resistance classes, all three alpha-diversity metrics differed significantly between the groups (p < 0.05). Community composition was associated with both host resistance phenotype and temperature-related variables, with average and accumulated temperature explaining a greater proportion of variation than host resistance phenotype. Less than 1% of phyllosphere fungal taxa overlapped with the seed-associated fungal communities. Network analyses further revealed seasonal differences in fungal association patterns between partially resistant and susceptible seedlings.

6.5 Integrating niches of the pathogen, multiple host and vector species highlights abiotic constraints and host-vector interactions shaping pine wilt disease risk in China.
Wang W, Du T, Ye J … Shi L.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityPest management science · 2026-10-16
matched: pathogen*, pine, Pinus
abstract

BackgroundPine wilt disease (PWD), caused by Bursaphelenchus xylophilus (PWN), is a major threat to forest health and pest management in China. Because PWD results from interactions among the pathogen, vector insects, and host plants, single-component analyses may misrepresent disease risk and overlook species-specific contributions. We integrated the environmental niches of the pathogen, hosts, and vectors to characterize abiotic constraints and biotic influences on PWD risk, quantify species-specific contributions and interactions, and inform surveillance priorities.ResultsEnsemble ecological niche models were developed for PWN, 17 susceptible hosts, and 10 potential cerambycid vectors across China and integrated with GeoDetector analysis. Although suitability patterns were broadly consistent, PWN had a much narrower suitable range than hosts and vectors, and this concordance declined under future climate scenarios. Bioclimatic variables were the primary determinants of habitat suitability for most species, whereas PWN showed narrower optimal ranges for key environmental drivers than most hosts and vectors. GeoDetector revealed substantial interspecific variation in explanatory power for PWN suitability, with Pinus massoniana, P. thunbergii, and Monochamus alternatus showing the highest q-values (0.78, 0.77, and 0.79, respectively). All host-vector combinations explained PWN suitability better than either species alone.ConclusionPWD risk in China cannot be inferred from host or vector availability alone, but is shaped by stronger abiotic constraints on PWN and host-vector interactions. Climatically marginal regions with suitable hosts and vectors should be prioritized for surveillance, and species with high q-values warrant further investigation. © 2026 Society of Chemical Industry.

6.5 Mammalian phosphodiesterase inhibitors reveal a role for cAMP in plant cell reprogramming toward embryogenesis in crop and forest species.
Pérez-Pérez Y, Carneros E, Rueda-Varela C … Testillano PS.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-19
matched: signaling, auxin*, embryo*, Quercus, tree, forest tree*
abstract

Somatic embryogenesis is an example of plant cell totipotency and an essential tool for plant breeding and biotechnology, yet its low efficiency remains a major limitation in many species. Although 3', 5'-cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) is increasingly recognized as a signaling molecule in plants, its role in developmental cell reprogramming is unknown. Here, we investigated whether pharmacological modulation of cAMP homeostasis using small-molecule inhibitors of mammalian phosphodiesterases (PDEs) could increase the rate of cell reprogramming towards embryogenesis in plants. Four structurally diverse PDE inhibitors were evaluated in three embryogenesis systems: microspore embryogenesis of Brassica napus and Hordeum vulgare, and somatic embryogenesis of Quercus suber. PDE inhibitors quantitatively enhanced cell reprogramming and initiation of embryogenesis in all three species, whereas structurally-related inactive analogues did not. Biochemical analyses demonstrated that treatment with the representative PDE7 inhibitor TC2.43 significantly increased cAMP levels, providing functional evidence that PDE inhibition modulates cAMP homeostasis in plant embryogenic cultures. Increased cAMP level was accompanied by increased expression of key embryogenesis regulators (FUS3, LEC2 and SERK1-like) and auxin biosynthesis and signaling genes (TAA1/TAR2 and ARF5), suggesting that cAMP signaling would be integrated into the molecular network controlling embryogenic competence acquisition. These findings on three evolutionarily distant angiosperms, two crops and a forest tree, suggest that the embryogenesis-promoting effects of PDE inhibition would be conserved across diverse taxa. Moreover, they identify changes in cAMP levels as a new player in embryogenic induction and suggest mammalian PDE inhibitors as promising chemical additives for improving in vitro plant regeneration.

6.5 Metagenomic profiling reveals that divergent fire-adaptive strategies drive distinct fungal succession in pine litter decomposition.
Fang YH, Liu HN, Chen YJ … Huang CL.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityIMA fungus · 2026-10-06
matched: microbiome*, pine, Pinus, lignin*
abstract

Decomposing litter is a critical driver of fuel bed formation and forest fire regimes, yet how divergent plant fire-adaptive strategies influence litter chemistry and subsequent fungal decomposition dynamics remains poorly understood. Here, we conducted a 6-month reciprocal litterbag experiment by incubating litter from two contrasting pine species-the fire-tolerant Pinus taiwanensis and the fire-avoiding P. morrisonicola-in both their own native habitat and that of the other species, coupled with genome-resolved metagenomics to track fungal succession and lignocellulolytic functional profiles. We found that the fire-avoiding P. morrisonicola exhibited significantly accelerated decomposition, driven by inherently lower initial C:N ratio and less recalcitrant litter, whereas the fire-tolerant P. taiwanensis exhibited slower decomposition rates. Metagenomic profiling revealed a distinct functional succession underlying this process. Early decomposition was dominated by Ascomycota (e.g., Leotiomycetes), which rapidly colonized the litter to initiate biopolymer breakdown. As easily decomposable substrates depleted, a functional shift occurred. Specifically, the highly recalcitrant P. taiwanensis litter selectively recruited Basidiomycota (e.g., Agaricales and Trechisporales) equipped with diverse lignin-modifying enzymes, reflected by a late-stage temporal surge in ligninolytic gene abundances. Ultimately, these findings demonstrate that plant fire-adaptive strategies act as a strong filter, shaping litter chemistry to drive specific taxonomic and functional fungal succession. This study provides a mechanistic understanding of the eco-evolutionary linkage between plant traits, microbiome assembly, and ecosystem-level fire dynamics.

6.5 Physicochemical Assessment of Plant-Derived Residues as Potential Components of Substrates for Pleurotus ostreatus Cultivation.
Koziak K, Piekut J, Krasowska M … Zapora E.
Ca2+Abiotic stressMaterials (Basel, Switzerland) · 2026-09-27
matched: calcium, pH, heavy metal*, oak, birch
abstract

Plant-derived residues may provide locally available components for mushroom cultivation substrates, but their variable composition requires preliminary physicochemical characterization. The aim of this study was to evaluate the physicochemical parameters of selected plant-derived residues in terms of their suitability as substrate components for the cultivation of Pleurotus ostreatus. The primary raw materials used were: oak sawdust, birch sawdust, wheat straw, triticale straw, rapeseed straw, and hemp shives. In addition, spent coffee grounds and apple pomace were applied as supplements enriching the substrate. The tested materials were subjected to physicochemical analyses including the determination of pH value, nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium, magnesium, calcium, sodium, organic carbon, and heavy metal content. The lignocellulosic materials showed high C:N ratios (80-257), indicating the potential need for nitrogen supplementation. Spent coffee grounds had the highest nitrogen content (21.61 g·kg-1 DM) and the lowest C:N ratio (25.96), while apple pomace showed the lowest pH (3.85). Wheat and triticale straw supplemented with spent coffee grounds were identified as promising candidates for further cultivation trials, supporting the valorization of plant-derived residues in line with circular economy principles. As the study was limited to preliminary physicochemical screening, lignocellulosic-fraction analysis, physical characterization, and cultivation trials are required before the technological suitability of the proposed mixtures can be confirmed.

6.0 Burlap-ribbon traps are effective for detecting Agrilus (Coleoptera: Buprestidae) eggs and associated parasitoids.
Petrice TR, Duan JJ, Schmude JM.
GrowthJournal of economic entomology · 2026-10-22
matched: bark, birch, Betula, tree, trees
abstract

Agrilus species (Coleoptera: Buprestidae) often oviposit in bark cracks and crevices to protect their eggs from natural enemies and unfavorable conditions, making field detection of eggs and associated parasitism challenging. Burlap-ribbon traps, which mimic tree bark texture, have been shown to induce emerald ash borer (EAB), Agrilus planipennis Fairmaire, to lay eggs on covered ash (Fraxinus sp.) surfaces. abstract

Genetic transformation is a powerful tool in the realm of plant sciences, enabling the study of endogenous and heterologous genes and conferring novel traits to transformed plant species, notably in crop species of agricultural importance. 6.0 Water use strategies of Populus euphratica across age classes along groundwater depth gradients in the lower Tarim River.
Li G, Hao S, Ye M … He D.
GrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-05
matched: leaf, xylem, Populus, tree, trees
abstract

How water source use and intrinsic water-use efficiency (iWUE) are linked across tree age classes under contrasting groundwater conditions remains unclear in desert riparian forests. We assessed water use strategies of young, middle-aged, and mature Populus euphratica in the lower Tarim River during ecological water conveyance (EWC) and non-EWC periods by combining hydrogen and oxygen stable isotopes of xylem and potential source waters with a Bayesian mixing model and leaf δ13C-derived iWUE. Across the five monitoring sections, mean groundwater depth was significantly lower during EWC than during non-EWC periods, averaging 4.7 and 4.9 m, respectively. At the Kunasite (DW) and Yingsu (F) sections, MixSIAR posterior mean contributions during EWC were 16.0-23.1% for groundwater, 14.4-17.6% for river water, and 6.6-19.0% for individual 40-cm soil layers. During non-EWC periods, posterior mean contributions from individual soil layers ranged from 10.9 to 20.3%, with relatively greater contributions from deeper layers. Associations between water source contributions and iWUE varied among age classes during EWC but were nonsignificant during non-EWC periods. Across sampling periods, mean iWUE was 58.29, 53.08, and 51.37 μmol mol-1 in young, middle-aged, and mature trees, respectively. The relationship between iWUE and groundwater depth also varied among sampling periods, with no consistent directional pattern. Based primarily on changes in the contributions of shallow and deep soil water along the groundwater depth gradient, we identified 4.5-6.0 m as a preliminary groundwater depth range associated with shifts in water source use by P. euphratica. These results show that water source use and iWUE in P. euphratica vary with hydrological conditions and tree age, providing empirical evidence for assessing its responses to EWC and groundwater variation in desert riparian forests.

19.5 Metabolically robust ratiometric autoluminescent reporters
Morozov VV, Balakireva AV, Gao F … Mishin AS
Nature Plants · 2026-10-23

abstract

abstract

Cerebral organoids have emerged as advanced three-dimensional in vitro models capable of recapitulating essential aspects of human brain development, thereby offering new opportunities for studying brain physiology and disease. 11.0 Live spatial validation of plant-microbe interactions in the rhizosphere with light-sheet fluorescence microscopy.
Dong X, Cheng Q, Ma J … Lin H.
reviewPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-10-05
matched: rhizobi*, mycorrhiz*, root, root hair*, growth, light-sheet, microscop*
abstract

Plant-microbe interactions in the rhizosphere are spatial events, yet mechanisms are often inferred from bulk samples, endpoint images, or community profiles averaged across heterogeneous root zones. Rather than surveying LSFM hardware or rhizosphere imaging broadly, this review evaluates light-sheet fluorescence microscopy (LSFM) as a platform for claim-level live spatial validation. Evidence is separated into four levels: A, direct LSFM evidence; B, an LSFM-compatible sample or device demonstrated with another modality; C, a correlative or validation modality; and D, a proposed future application. Within the verified full-text evidence set, direct evidence is concentrated in whole-rhizosphere tracking of Bacillus populations, plant reporter responses during early root-fungus interactions, and rhizobial attachment and infection of root hairs. Many arbuscular mycorrhizal and biocontrol applications remain prospective. We compare instrument and sample choices across field of view, numerical aperture, voxel sampling, time interval, spectral channels, and matrix realism; define root-centric metrics for arrival, residence, occupancy, growth, and persistence; and distinguish simultaneous, sequential in situ, and destructive endpoint validation. LSFM can identify where and when a candidate interaction occurs, but identity, activity, perturbation, and functional assays are still required before mechanism is assigned. A minimum reporting checklist is proposed to connect optical settings, environmental context, hierarchical sampling, registration error, and open analysis. The strongest role of LSFM in rhizosphere research is therefore targeted and evidence-bounded: resolving a live spatial prediction and directing independent tests of what the interaction does and why.

11.0 Nanobody-fluorescent protein fusions for erasable immunostaining.
Xu X, Kong Z, Su Z … Wan Y.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityThe Analyst · 2026-10-06
matched: receptor*, immun*, reporter*, fluorescent protein*, fluorescent reporter*, genetically encoded
abstract

Immunofluorescence (IF) staining is widely used to visualize biomolecules in cells and tissues. Conventional IF typically relies on fluorophore-conjugated antibodies, whose preparation requires chemical modification procedures that can compromise antibody performance and incur product loss during subsequent purification. In addition, iterative immunostaining depends on harsh stripping or fluorescence-quenching treatments that may damage sample architecture and epitope integrity. Here, we present protease-cleavable nanobody-fluorescent protein fusions (NFPFs) for reversible IF staining. Unlike fluorophore-labeled antibodies, NFPFs are genetically encoded and can be produced without post-synthetic chemical modification. Sequential labeling of distinct receptors demonstrated the feasibility of iterative immunostaining within the same specimen. Collectively, NFPFs provide a simple, scalable, and versatile approach for molecular imaging, enabling repeated rounds of immunostaining.

abstract

Upstream open reading frames (uORFs) are intrinsic regulatory elements that fine-tune gene expression at the translational level without altering genomic sequences or protein structures. Here, we targeted the conserved uORF that resides in the 5'-untranslated region (5'-UTR) of a GDP-galactose phosphorylase gene (GhGGP1) in cotton (Gossypium hirsutum). Together, our findings establish the uORFGhGGP1 as a dual-layer regulatory module that integrates transcriptional activation by GhBBM with translational control of the downstream protein encoding open reading frame, thereby fine-tuning drought stress responses in cotton.

7.5 A fluorescent/colorimetric/electrochemical tri-modal sensing platform for cross-validated dynamic monitoring of endogenous glutathione in plants under stress.
Dong H, Cui J, Zhang H … Xu M.
Abiotic stressGrowthTalanta · 2026-10-11
matched: cadmium, stress respon*, leaf, ratiometric, confocal, microscop*
abstract

Glutathione (GSH) serves as a core cellular redox regulator for plant abiotic-stress responses. Reliable quantification of endogenous plant GSH remains challenging owing to complex matrix interferences, as well as dependencies on specialized instrumentation or transgenic plant materials. Herein, we design and synthesize RFNSP, a resorufin-based molecular probe functionalized with a 2-fluoro-4-nitrobenzenesulfonyl ester recognition moiety, and further construct a fluorescence-colorimetric-electrochemical tri-modal sensing platform for cross-validated GSH quantification in plant samples. Upon reaction with GSH, RFNSP undergoes a two-step sequential activation pathway to generate the conjugated adduct Re-GSH, simultaneously triggering three independent signal outputs; this kinetic feature enables effective discrimination of GSH against cysteine and homocysteine. A graphitic carbon nitride/single-walled carbon nanotube modified carbon-paper electrode (CP/SWCNTs/g-C3N4) is adopted as the electrochemical sensing interface. Benefitting from complementary analytical performances, the platform achieves ratiometric fluorescence detection with a limit of detection (LOD) of 0.5 μM (0.01-1.5 mM), visual colorimetric readout, and electrochemical measurement reaching an LOD of 0.1 μM (2-200 μM). We established chemically tuned Arabidopsis thaliana leaf extracts featuring varied endogenous GSH levels, including cadmium-triggered GSH up-regulation, buthionine-sulfoximine-induced GSH depletion, and N-acetylcysteine-driven GSH restoration. Consistent quantitative results were acquired across all three sensing channels. Free from transgenic materials, confocal microscopy and commercial assay kits, this cross-validated multimodal platform provides a practical analytical tool to advance plant stress-redox biological studies.

7.5 Transcriptomic and functional evidence for a CiHY5-CiMYB12-like module associated with UV-B-enhanced drought acclimation in Chrysanthemum indicum var. aromaticum.
Yang Y, Zhu Q, Li Z … He M.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-09
matched: signaling, defense, drought, leaves, luciferase*
abstract

An aromatic ornamental and medicinal plant resource, Chrysanthemum indicum var. aromaticum grows at elevations above 2000 m, where it is exposed to strong ultraviolet-B (UV-B) radiation and other environmental constraints. Our previous research indicated that supplemental UV-B improves drought acclimation in C. indicum var. aromaticum. However, the underlying molecular mechanisms remain unclear. This study performed transcriptome sequencing of C. indicum var. aromaticum leaves following 4 h of supplemental UV-B exposure. The response involved multiple signaling and defense-related processes, while genes in the UV-B signaling and phenylpropanoid-flavonoid pathways were prominently induced. Heterologous overexpression of the UV-B photoreceptor CiUVR8 in Arabidopsis thaliana enhanced drought acclimation. Furthermore, virus-induced gene silencing of CiUVR8 in C. indicum var. aromaticum compromised UV-B-induced drought acclimation. Focusing on the MYB transcription factor SG7 subgroup responsive to UV-B, we identified CiMYB12-like as the most significantly upregulated member. As a central component of the UV-B signaling pathway, CiHY5 bound to the CiMYB12-like promoter in yeast one-hybrid assays and activated its transcription in dual-luciferase assays. CiMYB12-like also bound to and activated the promoter of the flavonoid biosynthetic gene CiCHI. Transient expression of CiMYB12-like increased the expression of flavonoid biosynthetic genes and total flavonoid content. A candidate co-expression network associated with CiHY5 and CiMYB12-like contained 28 genes related to transcriptional regulation, secondary metabolism, antioxidant defense, and transmembrane transport. Together, these findings establish a functional link between UV-B perception and drought acclimation in a native high-altitude species. The CiHY5-CiMYB12-like flavonoid module and its associated candidate co-expression network extend the canonical UV-B signaling framework to an ecologically relevant multi-stress context and provide targets for further investigation of high-altitude plant acclimation.

7.0 Blender tissue cartography: An intuitive tool for analyzing 3D microscopy data
Claussen N, Regis C, Wopat S … Streichan S
GrowthDevelopment · 2026-10-26
matched: stem, stem cell*, microscop*, time-lapse, image analysis
abstract

We present a powerful and user-friendly tool for tissue cartography, a 3D image analysis and visualization method that maps curved surfaces, like epithelial sheets, onto 2D projections. Volumetric microscopy can image complex 3D tissues, but 3D image data remains difficult to visualize and quantify. Many biological systems are organized as thin, curved sheets. Tissue cartography extracts and cartographically projects these curved surfaces from volumetric images. This converts 3D into 2D image data, facilitating visualization, analysis, and computational processing. Existing tools, however, demand advanced coding expertise and are limited to simple tissue geometries. Here, we describe https://nikolas-claussen.github.io/blender-tissue-cartography/blender tissue cartography (btc), an interactive add-on for the 3D editor Blender that makes tissue cartography user-friendly through a graphical interface and handles complex biological shapes through powerful computer graphics algorithms. An accompanying Python library supports faithful 3D measurements in 2D cartographic projections and custom analysis pipelines. Time-lapse data can be batch-processed by algorithmically aligning all time points to a single key frame. We demonstrate btc on diverse and complex tissue shapes from Drosophila, stem-cell organoids, Arabidopsis, and zebrafish. btc enables quantitative cartographic analysis of complex 3D tissues, broadening access to methods previously restricted to specialists, while leveraging tools from computer graphics to unlock new capabilities.

7.0 BnaA8.RAP2.1 negatively regulates cuticular wax accumulation and drought tolerance in Brassica napus.
Luo N, Li R, Wang S … Ni Y.
Abiotic stressGrowthTrees and woody plantsPlant physiology · 2026-10-23
matched: drought, stoma*, luciferase*
abstract

Cuticular wax is an important component of the plant surface barrier that contributes to water retention and drought adaptation. However, the transcriptional mechanisms that negatively regulate wax accumulation remain poorly understood. Here, we identify BnaA8.RAP2.1, an AP2/ERF transcriptional repressor, as a negative regulator of cuticular wax accumulation and drought tolerance in Brassica napus. Loss- and gain-of-function analyses revealed reciprocal effects of BnaA8.RAP2.1 on wax deposition, cuticle permeability, water loss, and drought tolerance. BnaA8.RAP2.1-overexpressing plants exhibited reduced wax accumulation, increased cuticle permeability and water loss, and greater drought sensitivity, whereas knockout plants showed the opposite phenotypes. Stomatal aperture measurements further revealed genotype-dependent responses under drought, suggesting that altered stomatal responses may also contributes to BnaA8.RAP2.1-mediated drought phenotypes. Mechanistically, BnaA8.RAP2.1 directly bound to and repressed the promoters of BnCER1-2 and BnGCN5, as demonstrated by ChIP-qPCR, EMSA, and dual-luciferase assays. Genetic rescue analysis showed that BnCER1-2 overexpression partially restored wax accumulation, water retention, and drought tolerance in RAP2.1-overexpression plants, supporting BnCER1-2 as a functional downstream target. Moreover, constitutive expression of BnaA8.RAP2.1 in Arabidopsis rap2.1 mutant backgrounds produced wax- and drought-related phenotypes consistent with the negative regulatory activity of BnaA8.RAP2.1 in a heterologous system. Together, our findings establish BnaA8.RAP2.1 as a transcriptional repressor of cuticular wax accumulation and reveal that its effects on drought tolerance involve both cuticle-associated water retention and stomatal responses, providing a potential target for improving drought resilience in rapeseed.

7.0 Genome-wide identification of the WRKY gene family in Codonopsis pilosula and functional analysis of CpWRKY8 under drought stress.
Song S, Liu M, Zhang M … Ma W.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-30
matched: receptor*, abscisic acid, ABA, drought, nucleus, roots, reporter*, luciferase*
abstract

Codonopsis pilosula (Franch.) Nannf., a perennial herbaceous species in the family Campanulaceae, is widely used in traditional medicine for its dried roots. Drought stress severely constrains both the yield and the accumulation of bioactive compounds in this species. In this study, we performed a genome-wide identification and phylogenetic analysis of the WRKY TF (TF) family in C. pilosula, identifying 49 members that were classified into seven subfamilies. Among them, CpWRKY8, a member of the Group IIa subfamily, localized to the nucleus and showed transcriptional activation activity in yeast. Under drought stress conditions, Nicotiana tabacum plants overexpressing (OE) CpWRKY8 exhibited greater tolerance to drought stress than wild-type (WT) N. tabacum plants, as evidenced by higher survival rates, lower malondialdehyde (MDA) levels, and higher superoxide dismutase (SOD), peroxidase (POD), and catalase (CAT) activities; furthermore, these physiological advantages were largely maintained following rehydration. Furthermore, yeast one-hybrid (Y1H) and dual-luciferase reporter (DLR) assays demonstrated that the abscisic acid (ABA) receptor CpPYR/PYL activated the CpWRKY8 promoter in heterologous systems. In summary, we propose a working model in which CpPYR/PYL indirectly upregulates CpWRKY8; this module enhances drought tolerance by increasing antioxidant capacity, thereby providing a theoretical basis for the molecular breeding of drought-tolerant medicinal plants.

7.0 Laccase-Based Biosensors for Functional Screening of Transcription Factors in Trichoderma reesei.
Xia Y, Yu J, Wang X … Lin X.
GrowthACS synthetic biology · 2026-10-01
matched: development, biosensor*, reporter*
abstract

Efficient transcription factor (TF) screening is essential for the rational optimization of industrial microbial strains. Here, we present an absorbance-activated laccase-based biosensor (AALB) platform designed for rapid functional screening of TFs in Trichoderma reesei. By using laccase as a reporter, the AALB platform offers a robust and cost-effective screening system for TF functional evaluation. The secreted heterologous laccase also complements T. reesei's native cellulolytic machinery, expanding its enzymatic repertoire for lignocellulosic biomass deconstruction. The core of the AALB platform is a Pcbh1-regulated laccase expression cassette, enabling sensitive and efficient selection of TF variants based on absorbance readouts. To further improve sensitivity, we introduced a quadruple repeat of the cis-element-dense segment in the cbh1 promoter and integrated a high-activity heterologous laccase at the SLP1 site of the T. reesei genome. Following a miniaturized 2 day pre-culture in 24-well plates, the AALB platform enables TF variant ranking within 30 min of absorbance readout in 200 μL 96-well reactions, reducing the need for extended shake-flask fermentation and separate downstream enzymatic or molecular assays during the initial screen. Using AALB, we successfully identified TF variants with robust transcriptional regulation. The screening results were validated through transcript quantification of laccase and XYR1 and extracellular laccase activity assays. Notably, the XYR1 mutation XYR1_V821F, selected via AALB, significantly increased laccase transcript levels compared to PoLAC-SLP1 chassis control, with laccase activity reaching 17.74 ± 0.76 IU/mLa 2.7-fold improvement. Further characterization of the AALB-selected XYR1_V821F strain showed increased extracellular laccase activity together with increased xylanase and filter paper activities, with extracellular xylanase and filter paper activities increased by 2.7- and 1.9-fold, respectively, and enhanced rice straw hydrolysis under the tested fermentation and hydrolysis conditions. The AALB platform provides valuable insights into the synthetic biology and metabolic engineering of T. reesei, facilitating the development of industrial strains for lignocellulosic biomass processing and enzyme production.

6.5 A JAZ-VQ-WRKY transcriptional feedback loop enforces homeostasis in cytotoxic alkaloid biosynthesis.
Zhang Y, Liu Q, Liu P … Tang K.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityJournal of advanced research · 2026-10-09
matched: signaling, jasmon*, JA, phytohormone*, immun*, defense, roots, reporter*, luciferase*, CRISPR
abstract

IntroductionPlants face the challenge of balancing the production of defense-related secondary metabolites, requiring inducible "accelerators" and intrinsic "braking" systems to avoid overproduction. While the phytohormone jasmonate (JA) is a well-established accelerator, the molecular nature of the braking machinery and its coordination with JA signaling remain unclear. The medicinal plant Catharanthus roseus produces cytotoxic terpenoid indole alkaloids (TIAs) and serves as an ideal system to address this question.ObjectivesThis study aims to identify the core JA-induced transcription factors regulating TIAs synthesis, and to elucidate how their activity is precisely constrained to prevent metabolic runaway, thereby deciphering the molecular mechanisms by which plants achieve metabolic homeostasis.MethodsWe used DNA affinity purification sequencing (DAP-seq), yeast one-hybrid (Y1H), and dual luciferase reporter (Dual-LUC) assays to identify and validate the direct binding and transactivation of the transcription factor CrWRKY4 on TIAs pathway genes. Gene knockout was achieved in hairy roots using an optimized CRISPR/Cas9 system. Protein interactions were analyzed using yeast two-hybrid (Y2H), bimolecular complementation (BiFC), and immunoprecipitation (Co-IP) techniques. The functional role of the regulatory module was systematically elucidated through subcellular localization studies, electrophoresis mobility shift assay (EMSA), and metabolite quantification (UPLC-QQQ-MS/MS).ResultsWe identified a JA-inducible master regulator transcription factor, CrWRKY4, it directly binds and activates multiple TIAs pathway genes. Concurrently, two co-repressors, CrVQ4 and CrVQ9, were characterized and inhibit CrWRKY4's DNA-binding capacity. Moreover, CrJAZ1 interacts with VQ proteins and interferes with the VQ-WRKY regulatory complex. Crucially, CrWRKY4 directly activates the expression of CrVQ4, CrVQ9, and CrJAZ1, forming an intrinsic negative feedback loop. Upon JA signaling, the repressive CrJAZ1-CrVQ4/9 module is relieved, allowing CrWRKY4 to activate TIAs gene expression.ConclusionThis study elucidates a core regulatory mechanism comprising the CrJAZ1-CrVQ4/9-CrWRKY4 module. This module forms a self-braking transcriptional circuit by integrating JA signal.

6.5 An electrochemical DNA biosensor based on gold nanoparticles/reduced graphene oxide nanocomposites for the detection of drug-resistant bacterial strain.
